I 

A. Defined and distinguished

Alimony is the provision which a husband is required to make for the support of his wife and children by direction of the court in a matrimonial action. It is predicated on the marital status and springs from the husband's obligation arising thereunder.

It is an allowance out of his estate, not a distribution of his assets; neither a determination of property rights nor a grant of interest in them. Payment of alimony is not the discharge of a debt but the fulfillment of the husband's continuing duty to furnish his wife with the means of obtaining future necessaries (Crain vs Cavana, 62 

Barb. 109, 1862; Sleeper, 65 Hun, 454, 20 Sup. 339, 1892, aff'd 142 N. Y. 625; Wilson vs Hinman, 182 N. Y. 408, 1905, rev❜g 99 A. D. 41, 90 Sup. 746; Pompilio, 129 Misc. 

207, 221 Sup. 594, 1927).

It is founded not on contract but on natural duty and public policy, and is made specific and measured by the court's mandate (Surut, 191 A. D. 570, 181 Sup. 631, 1920; Romaine vs Chauncey, 129 N. Y. 566, 1892, aff'g 60 

Hun, 477, 15 Sup. 198; Livingston, 173 N. Y. 377, 1903, aff'g 74 A. D. 261, 77 Sup. 476; Re Emil J. Smith, 3 Am. 

B'k'ptcy Rep. 67, 1899).

The allowance, which is in personam, may not be sued for independently, but only as an incident of the action, which is quasi in rem (Averett, 110 Misc. 584, 181 Sup. 1

all نوع 

645, 1920, aff'd 181 Sup. 927). We have no action for maintenance such as there is in other states (Atwater, 53 Barb. 621, 1868; Benner, 63 Ohio St. 220, 1900).

The result is therefore somewhat anomalous. The equity courts have no power to compel a husband to support his wife unless either one first demands the severance or suspension of the marital ties. That is, his obligation will not be enforced unless the very status which occasions and justifies it is sought to be or is actually impaired. Nevertheless, it is a result which is justified by the fact that the wife has no remedy at law. She may be unable to pledge her husband's credit for necessaries because third parties may refuse to deal with her; she may sue her husband directly for reimbursement of expenditures made by her for her support, now that the common law disability has been removed (Hauser, 154 

Sup. 1072, 1915; De Brauwere, 203 N. Y. 460, 1911, aff'g 

144 A.D. 521, 129 Sup. 587), only if they were out of her private estate, and she may have had none; and resort to the Police Court cannot be considered legal redress for it may be had only when she is so destitute as to imperil the public funds with the burden of her care (Peo. ex rel 

Heinle vs Heinle, 115 Misc. 469, 188 Sup. 399, 1921).

The wife's general, or political right to support is substantially different from alimony, which is an accrual, so to speak, or "consummation" of the right. In the case of 

Sandford, 60 Sup. Ct. 52, 17 Sup. 181, 1891, the matter under consideration was the status of support for children, which is the same in principle and effect as support for married women (Schafer, 118 Misc. 254, 193 Sup. 43, 1922; Rice vs Andrews, 127 Misc. 826, 217 Sup. 528, 1926). Before the commencement of the action, it was held, the right is not vested, cannot be enjoyed as a matter of right and is devoid of characteristics giving it a

present right of enforcement. Thus, a statute which provided for additional allowances for children under certain circumstances and which was repealed, with the reservation that proceedings already begun were not to be affected by the change, was held not to be available in actions commenced after the repeal. Prior to the institution of the suit, children (and by the identity stated, married women as well) had no such subsisting interest, arising from their right to be maintained, as could be illegally impaired by the repeal. It is significant in this respect that the authority for the allowance is not to be found in any substantive enactment, such as the Do mestic Relations Law, but in the Civil Practice Act. Ali mony is a remedy, not a right. 

B. Limitation of the allowance to matrimonial actions

Divorce, separation and annulment are the only matrimonial actions. In all of them the relief sought affects the marital status itself, either by dissolving it outright or defeating one of its essential purposes. Alimony is available only in these actions and only when they are between the spouses as parties plaintiff and defendant respectively. It may not be had in any other action or proceeding, legal or equitable, civil or criminal. Abandonment and non-support prosecutions are excluded from present consideration.

Thus, alimony and other allowances in the nature of alimony were denied in a proceeding to dissolve the mar riage under Section 7A of the Domestic Relations Law 

(In re Powell, 123 Misc. 172, 205 Sup. 339, 1924); an action to annul the marriage brought by the husband's parent for his non-age (Perkins, cited in Osgood, 2 Paige 

Ch. 621, 1831; Stivers vs Wise, 18 A.D. 316, 46 Sup. 9,

1897); a statutory action to annul the marriage brought by the relatives of the deceased husband (Farnham, 227 N. Y. 155, 1919); an action to enforce a separation agreement (Phillips vs Peacock, 63 Misc. 520, 117 Sup. 625, 1909); the wife's proceeding to recover for her support from the estate of her husband, an incompetent (In re 

Warren, 207 A.D. 793, 202 Sup. 586, 1924); habeas corpus for the custody of children (Peo. ex rel Klee vs Klee, 202 

A.D. 592, 195 Sup. 778, 1922); an action which purported to be for separation, but in whose complaint there was no prayer for such relief (Ramsden, 91 N. Y. 281, 1883, aff'g 28 Hun, 285, 2 N. Y. Civ. Pro. Rep. 416).

A counsel fee-which, for the purpose of the present discussion, may be considered the same as alimony-was held to be proper in the wife's action to set aside a separation agreement for fraud, in Johnson, 151 A.D. 545, 136 

Sup. 249, 1912, and 150 A.D. 306, 134 Sup. 1081, 1912.

The court ruled that the inherent power which it possessed to entertain any suit to set aside a contract where there was no meeting of the parties' minds included the authority, where the litigation was between the spouses, to award the wife the means of carrying it on; and cited a number of old cases in which the allowance was granted, such as Yeo, 2 Dickens Rep. 498 and Head, 3 Atkins 295, both English cases and both for specific performance of a separation agreement, and Culver, 8 B. Mon. (Ky.) 

128, a similar action and brought against the administrator of the husband's estate. The failure of the statute to provide for such an allowance in actions other than divorce and separation did not bar it in actions to annul the marriage (as more fully taken up in the following pages), and for the same reason was not deemed a bar in the instant one. 

The decision was reversed, however (206 N. Y. 561, 1

1912). The established policy of confining the allowance to those actions only in which the marital status is in issue was invoked. An action to set aside a separation agreement, or, for that matter, any action other than divorce, separation or annulment was held not to possess the properties of a matrimonial action merely because it was between husband and wife. "Undoubtedly an action in equity may be maintained on proper grounds to set aside a separation agreement. But such an action would rest on the same general principles as a similar action affecting any contract. The plaintiff would be allowed to maintain it not because the agreement related to the marital relation but because she had been persuaded by fraud or duress to enter into a contract impairing her legal rights. The fact that the defendant in such suit was her husband might be a potential one in proving fraud or duress but it would not constitute a cause of action. Such suit would be governed by the same principles as one to procure the cancellation of a contract for the sale of land . . . and I am not aware of any influential authority for the proposition that in an ordinary equity suit between husband and wife affecting property rights counsel fees should be allowed to the former (p. 567) 

...

C. History and development-actions for annulment 

Jurisdiction before the statute

There was no common law jurisdiction over divorce. 

The Ecclesiastical Courts administered the law in England and we never adopted it here (Walker, 155 N. Y. 77, 1898). Divorces were granted by our legislatures in 

Colonial days.

The only matrimonial actions which the equity courts entertained in the absence of statute were annulments for fraud, duress and insanity. They considered their

authority to hear them inherent, and as a necessary incident of it, awarded alimony. The situation is explained at some length in Griffin, 47 N. Y. 134, 1872. "So long as the marriage is not ascertained to be invalid, the court, having jurisdiction of the parties and the controversy, must be deemed to have power to compel the husband to furnish these means, whether the jurisdiction over the prescribed case be original or purely of statutory creation. (p. 142) 

The allowance was granted in these annulment actions only pendente lite and only when the wife was defendant, a rule which has never changed. She could not in one breath repudiate the conjugal status by claiming that the marriage was never valid, and demand alimony from the defendant on the ground that he was still her husband, which would be virtually her position as plaintiff (North, 1 Barb. Ch. 241, 1845; Kinzey, 7 Daly 460, 1878; 

Lee, 4 Civ. Pro. Rep. 321, 1883; Wabberson, 27 Misc. 

125, 57 Sup. 405, 1899; Butler, 204 A.D. 602, 198 Sup. 

391, 1923).

The plaintiff wife was granted alimony in one case (Allen, 59 How. Pr. 27, 1880) on the ground that the marriage remained valid and subsisting up to the final adjudication, the wife's representations to the contrary notwithstanding-and, presumably, that the plain fact of the matter should not yield before an implication at most, particularly where it operated to her disadvantage; but the case was quickly repudiated (Bloodgood, 59 How. 

Pr. 42, 1880) as being incompatible with the settled authorities. See also Bartlett, 1 Clarke Ch. 322, 1841; 

Meo, 2 Sup. 569, 1888; Park, 24 Misc. 372, 53 Sup. 677, 1898; Herron, 28 Misc. 323, 59 Sup. 861, 1899.

Nor does the fact that the marriage is voidable merely and not void justify an allowance to the plaintiff wife,

as was done in another case (Gore, 103 A.D. 74, 92 Sup. 

634, 1905). The distinction has been removed, and the rule now is that both classes of marriage become void ab initio by the decree (In re Moncrief, 235 N. Y. 390, 1921, rev'g 203 A.D. 893, 196 Sup. 940).

The plaintiff wife may have alimony, however, where she sues to annul the husband's subsequent, bigamous marriage, for she seeks to affirm her own status as wife (Procita, 190 Sup. 21, 1921).

The wife may have an allowance in the husband's action to annul the marriage where he had a wife living when it took place (Erlanger, 173 A.D. 767, 159 Sup. 

353, 1916). 

The allowance in annulment actions under the matrimonial statutes

The several matrimonial statutes are silent as to alimony in annulment actions, and specify it only in actions for divorce and separation. But just as alimony was formerly granted in the absence of statutory sanction on the theory that the power was necessarily incidental to the inherent jurisdiction over the subject matter, it is granted to-day in the exercise of the express jurisdiction conferred; and instead of being limited to those cases. only which are based on fraud, etc., the award is made in every case.

It is considered also that the statute, in so far as it does provide the remedy in divorce and separation actions, is the expression of the legislative intent to clothe the courts with general power to protect the wife's in-. terest in actions before them. Another explanation advanced is that the statute must have contemplated annulment actions as well because there is nothing to indicate and there is no reason for assuming that they were

meant to be excluded. In short, it is another instance of judicial construction becoming as effectual as a formal enactment of its own. The various cases which have been chiefly responsible for this judicial legislation (Griffin, 47 N. Y. 134, 1872; Brinkley, 50 N. Y. 184, 1872; 

Higgins vs Sharp, 164 N. Y. 4, 1900; Jones vs Brinsmade, 183 N. Y. 258, 1905) are reviewed in Schroter, 57 

Misc. 199, 107 Sup. 1065, 1907, where they are declared to "decide that, although the statute which authorizes and regulates actions to annul a marriage is silent as to alimony and counsel fees, the court is not without power to allow the same to a wife where she defends the action; the general jurisdiction so conferred to entertain such action carrying with it by implication any incidental powers necessary for its proper exercise, among them being the guarding and protecting of the rights of the wife which have always been regarded as a part of the general equitable jurisdiction of the court. În all these cases it is recognized that the same rules respecting the allowance of alimony and counsel fees apply to actions to annul a marriage as to actions for divorce." (pp. 1065, 1066) 

See also O'Dea, 31 Hun, 441, 1884, aff'd 95 N. Y. 667 and Brand, 178 A.D. 822, 166 Sup. 90, 1917.

D. Statutory nature of the remedy-the Civil Practice 

Act

Alimony is a statutory remedy, not inherent (Wood, 61 A.D. 96, 70 Sup. 72, 1901; Van Horn, 196 A.D. 472, 188 Sup. 98, 1921). Jurisdiction is exclusive to the state court. The United States courts have no jurisdiction over divorce and therefore none over alimony (Barber, 21 How. (U.S.) 582, 1858).

Thus, both authority and practice depend upon the statute, exactly as in the case of matrimonial actions in

general. When the statute is silent, however, the procedure follows the rule in equity (Hayes, 74 Misc. 533, 134 Sup. 482, 1911, rev'd on other points 150 A.D. 842, 135 Sup. 225).

For a history of the statutory development of alimony, see Erkenbrach, 96 N. Y. 456, 1884.

The Civil Practice Act contains six sections which make provision for alimony or allowances in the nature of alimony. They are: 1140, support for children in a judgment of annulment, out of the property of either parent if the marriage is voidable, out of that of the guilty parent if the marriage is void; 1155, alimony and support for children in a judgment of divorce to the wife; 1164, alimony and support for children in a judgment granting or denying the wife a separation; 1169, temporary alimony and support for children in divorce and separation actions brought by either spouse, as well as means to enable the wife to "carry on or defend"; 1170, temporary and permanent alimony and support for children in divorce and separation actions brought by the wife, support for children only when brought by the husband; 1175, alimony and support for children in the interlocutory judgment of divorce to the wife. 

It will be seen, in examining the sections, that provision for children is made in all six and is the sole provision in 1140; that 1140 is the only one to specify annulment actions or to charge the wife with the duty of supporting the children; in all the sections except 1140 and 1169, the wife may have the allowance only in actions brought by her; 1169 is the only one to provide an allowance other than alimony or support for children, viz., expenses, which includes counsel fees, as specified in the expression, "sum or sums of money necessary to enable the wife to carry on or defend the action."

TA 

There are the following duplications: both 1169 and 

1170 provide temporary alimony in divorce and separation actions where the wife sues, both 1155 and 1170 provide alimony in the wife's divorce judgment and both 1164 and 1170 provide it in the judgment granting or denying her a separation. In passing, it may be added that Section 1170 is important chiefly for its specifications as to modification. 

E. Fundamental incidents of alimony 

Wife's creditors

Alimony is not available to the wife's creditors, unless the debt accrued after the allowance was made and was for necessaries. Otherwise, the allowance would be diverted from its purpose of meeting her future needs (Romaine vs Chauncey, 60 Hun, 477, 15 Sup. 198, 1891, aff'd 129 N. Y. 566; Stevenson, 34 Hun, 157, 1884). The academic explanation is that alimony is a personal responsibility on the husband's part and a personal right to the wife. It cannot be treated as a debt in the conventional sense, that is, such a chose in action, or personality, as could be reached by third parties. 

Nor may the wife's creditors pursue alimony as equitable assets, on the theory that they have a legal claim unenforceable by legal means, viz., execution. Alimony will not be treated as equitable assets in so far as it may become liable as property (Romaine vs Chauncey, supra). It is unavailable to the creditors even after it has left the husband's possession by being delivered to a third party for payment to the wife (West vs Washburn, 153 A.D. 460, 138 Sup. 230, 1912). This usually takes place when remittances are forwarded to the wife's at-

torney. In short, alimony is a specific fund for a specific purpose and will not be diverted to any other (Andrews vs Whitney, 82 Hun, 117, 31 Sup. 164, 1894). 

Anyone advancing money or credit to the wife for her necessaries and on the faith of the alimony awarded is entitled to subrogation. This includes her attorney (Turner vs Woolworth, 221 N. Y. 425, mod❜g 165 A.D. 70, 151 

Sup. 93, 1917; also, 153 A.D. 293, 137 Sup. 1071, 1912).

Creditors may sometimes resort to the fund if they can show that it is not actually necessary for her support, or at least that part of it which is not necessary. This may not be done, however, if the allowance includes support for children, unless the order or judgment granting the award specifies what portion is for them (Maurice Baskin vs Howe, 225 A.D. 553, 233 Sup. 648, 1929). 

Husband's creditors

The wife has priority over the husband's creditors. Her claim for alimony may be preferred on his assignment for the benefit of creditors (In re Thrall, 12 A.D. 235, 42 

Sup. 439, 1896, aff'd 153 N. Y. 644). She was permitted to reach the surplus of a trust fund bequeathed to her husband, remaining after he had drawn a sufficient amount for his own needs, and his creditors were deferred until such disposition had been effected (Wetmore, 149 N. Y. 520, 1896). The express provision in the will of the husband's testator that a portion of the bequest be applied to alimony prevailed against the husband's creditor even though his claim had precedence in time (DeMuth vs Kemp, 79 Misc. 516, 140 Sup. 152, 1913).

Accumulated arrears in the husband's possession are not available to his creditors (Van Valkenburgh vs 

Bishop, 164 Sup. 86, 1917).

11 Assignments 

Alimony is unassignable (Van Vleck, 21 A.D. 272, 47 

Sup. 470, 1897). Contracts between the wife and her attorney for the assignment to him of the alimony or part of it are condemned by public policy, in addition to being legally unenforceable; and it does not matter that part of the fund specified in the agreement to be paid to him is to be realized from another, non-matrimonial litigation (In re Brackett, 114 A.D. 257, 99 Sup. 802, 1906, aff'd 189 N. Y. 502). He may not keep the alimony even to reimburse himself for expenditures made for her (In re Bolles, 78 A.D. 180, 79 Sup. 530, 1903). 

Husband's counter-claims

The husband stands in no better position than any other creditor of the wife. He may not counterclaim or set-off his claim against the wife unless it accrued after the award was made and was for necessaries (Locke, 71 

Hun, 363, 24 Sup. 1129, 1893). 

Husband's bankruptcy

Alimony is neither provable nor dischargeable in bankruptcy, both as to accrued and future instalments (Re 

Emil J. Smith, 3 Am. B'k'ptcy Rep. 67, 1899; Audubon vs Shufelt, 181 U.S. 575, 1901; In re Anderson, 97 Fed. 

Rep. 321, 1899; Maisner, 62 A.D. 286, 70 Sup. 1107, 1901; 

Young, 35 Misc. 335, 71 Sup. 944, 1901; In re Williams, 208 N. Y. 32, 1913, aff'g 152 A.D. 385, 136 Sup. 707). 

Intimations to the contrary (Beach, 29 Hun, 181, 1883) are not to be followed. Exemptions

Exemption statutes are ineffectual against enforcement of alimony. Police pensions protected by special

ordinance may be reached on the ground that the funds are intended for the pensioners and their families (Zwingmann, 150 A.D. 358, 134 Sup. 1077, 1912; Monck, 184 

A.D. 656, 172 Sup. 401, 1918). Salaries are not exempt because the wife is one of the persons for whose benefit the exemption is made (Valentine vs Williams, 159 Sup. 

815, 1916, aff'd 179 A.D. 884, 165 Sup. 116, aff'd 223 N. Y. 574). Restitution 

F. Amount

The wife need not make restitution of payments made prior to the reversal or setting aside of the order or judgment granting alimony (Averett, 110 Misc. 584, 181 

Sup. 645, 1920, aff'd 181 Sup. 927). This is because the husband has a duty independently of the award to provide for his wife, and she is free to accept the payments as being in discharge of the former. In another case, however, (Surut, 191 A.D. 570, 181 Sup. 631, 1920) it was suggested that the rule is applicable when the wife has expended the money and has no means to restore it. 

Governing principle

The court considers every item touching the financial status of both parties, in determining the amount of alimony to be allowed, as well as their general circumstances, social station and standard of living (Stevens, 214 A.D. 785, 211 Sup. 913, 1925, mod❜g 125 Misc. 451, 211 Sup. 192; Dula, 217 A.D. 790, 217 Sup. 224, 1926; 

Hallock, 4 How. Pr. Rep. 160, 1849). For a comprehensive discussion see Conklin, 186 Sup. 191, 1921.

The amount is generally one-third of the husband's income, although there is no rule to that effect. It is

11 seldom, if ever, more, except where there are children. 

Such a proportion is considered fair if the husband's income is received from a fixed salary or certain investments, but not where it depends upon his performance of personal services and may be reduced or taken away by conditions beyond his control (Cowles, 29 A.D. 476, 51 Sup. 1057, 1898). The degree of certainty of his income has a direct bearing upon the amount of alimony which should be awarded.

The underlying considerations are the same as to both mporary and permanent alimony. There are early statements favoring a larger amount on the permanent award (Hammond, 1 Clarke Ch. 151, 1839; Burr, 7 Hill, 207, 1843, p. 213). 

Husband's means

The husband's earning capacity as well as earnings should be considered (Scragg, 63 Hun, 633, 18 Sup. 487, 1892).

Profits in stock speculations may be considered, but are not a satisfactory basis (Johnstone, 130 Misc. 243, 223 

Sup. 744, 1927).

The following were considered as items properly reducing the husband's means, and, therefore, his ability to pay alimony: the existence of judgments against him (Rickels, L. J., N. Y. Co., Dec. 19, 1930); payment by him of carrying charges on joint property (Bergson, id., Kings 

Co., Dec. 10, 1930); provision made by him for the support of his mother and sister (Bressette, 95 A.D. 167, 88 

Sup. 580, 1904). The last case would seem to be supportable, however, only where the claims of the husband's kin have some legal justification. In this connection the following is worth noting-that gratuitous contributions

from the husband's mother should not be taken as tending to improve his paying capacity because it would mean an indirect coercion of the mother (Sidway, 156 A.D. 61, 141 Sup. 14, 1913), whereas contributions to the wife from her mother may be considered as improving her status when the question is whether the wife should be charged with the duty of supporting the child (Earle, 164 

A.D. 713, 150 Sup. 173, 1914, aff'd 222 N. Y. 589). 

Wife's means

The wife's means and pecuniary status in general are given increasing consideration and emphasis (Waterman, 147 A.D. 464, 131 Sup. 741, 1911; Leroy, 175 A.D. 120, 161 Sup. 503, 1916). The fact that she is wealthy, however, and can dispense with her husband's support does not necessarily excuse him from the responsibility (Seitz, 192 A.D. 924, 183 Sup. 79, 1920). It will serve, at most, as a measure of the allowance, particularly where the wife's property has not come from her husband and is entirely her own, and she is not so rich as to make any allowance unnecessary (Merritt, 99 N. Y. 643, 1885).

A good statement of the prevailing rule, taking all the decisions together and as they collectively seem to disclose, is to be found in Dean, 48 Misc. 149, 96 Sup. 472, 1905, that if the wife's means are so substantial and the husband's so limited that an allowance would have to be nominal, there is no requirement in law or equity to grant one. For this reason, it was denied in Richardson, 94

Sup. 582, 1905. This, it would seem, is the limit of the husband's obligation in this respect. 

Circumstances tending to improve the wife's material state, other than pecuniary ones, are taken into consideration, as that she is in good health, accustomed to

work and not hampered by children (Hodge, 85 Sup. 

1133, 1904). The court also takes cognizance of advantages which she may be enjoying, as that her father is supporting her through a creditable arrangement with the husband (Bartlett, 1 Clarke Ch., 322, 1841); that she and the child are living in the husband's house (Bruggeman, 191 A.D. 689, 182 Sup. 87, 1920); that she is being supported by the two children (Wehner, L. J., Kings Co., Dec. 29, 1930). 

Equitable considerations

Purely equitable circumstances are often influencing factors, in determining the amount of the alimony to be awarded. They may be either the husband's extreme misconduct, which justified the allowance, or more often, the wife's behaviour tending to provoke it or to contribute to the domestic rift. Thus, the allowance was made smaller than it would otherwise have been because of her wasteful habits and the fact that she was partly to blame for the husband's delinquency (Hardy, 53 Hun, 635, 6 Sup. 300, 1889). The subject is taken up more fully in the discussion on modification of permanent ali mony (Chapter IV, The Judgment). 

Individual cases

The court allowed $1000 for wife and child annually where the husband earned from $3000 to $5000 annually and the wife had an annual income of $1000 (Williams, 53 Hun, 636, 6 Sup. 645, aff'd 130 N. Y. 193, 1891); $500 for the wife and $300 for two children where it was a little more than one-half of his income (Valentine, 87 

A.D. 156, 84 Sup. 37, 1903); $8400 annually for the wife and three children where his earnings were from $17000

to $20000 annually (Rosenwasser, 117 Misc. 123, 190

Sup. 774, 1921); $100 per month where he earned $5000 annually and had debts of $4500 incurred by the wife (Hardy, supra). 

References, examinations and appeals

References to determine the means of the parties are proper and are frequently resorted to (Hart, 186 Sup. 

941, 1921; Suriano, L. J., N. Y. Co., Nov. 22, 1930). Examinations before trial for the same purpose are sometimes permitted (Nottingham, 209 A.D. 462, 204 Sup. 

753, 1924); but see Fried, 230 A. D. 708, 1930; Safrin, 205 A. D. 628, 200 Sup. 51, 1923; Lewis, L. J., Kings 

Co., April 29, 1931. The husband may be required to submit to an interrogation by the wife's attorney, where the issue of means takes an important turn in the controversy (Kennedy, id., N. Y. Co., Dec. 19, 1930). 

Temporary orders and judgments are appealable on the ground that the alimony provisions are inadequate or excessive (Brown, 191 A.D. 917, 181 Sup. 929, 1920). The appeals will be upheld only for abuse of discretion (DeLlamosas, 2 Hun, 380, appeal dis'd 

62 N. Y. 618, 1875). In this connection, it should be noted that the fact that the husband undertakes the payment of fees to his attorney and the various disbursements incidental to the prosecution of an appeal by him is a circumstance which may count against him with the court, on the question of his ability to pay the sums awarded (Kabatchnick, 26 A.D. 292, 49 Sup. 612, 1898).

Agreements of the parties—support for children Agreements on the amount to be awarded are approved. The court will hold the parties to their word,

as where the husband resisted the enforcement of the stipulation on the ground that the amount was excessive (Thrall, 83 Hun, 188, 31 Sup. 591, 1894). Separation agreements which are ineffectual to bar alimony may be taken as criteria on the amount to be allowed (Eisenbrock, 187 A.D. 85, 175 Sup. 67, 1919).

The court will ignore stipulations fixing the amount for the support of children unless their welfare is thereby assured (Chamberlin, 193 A.D. 784, 184 Sup. 464, 1920; 

Craver, 186 A.D. 847, 175 Sup. 26, 1919). For an instance of the computation of support for children, see 

In re Narelle's Estate, 136 Misc. 781, 243 Sup. 754, 1930, in which the Surrogate made the disposition out of a trust fund established by a judgment of divorce to be applied for the infant's benefit.

G. Miscellaneous features 

Mode of payment

Alimony is either temporary or permanent, that is, pursuant to order made pendente lite, on the wife's motion and payable only until judgment, or by final judgment after trial and payable forever thereafter until terminated by order of the court or death of the parties. 

It is payable in instalments, not by a gross sum (Doerle, 96 Misc. 72, 159 Sup. 637, 1916) and in advance (Weingarten, 133 Misc. 681, 233 Sup. 268, 1929), usually weekly or monthly. A different arrangement is sometimes necessitated, as where the husband was a soldier in service and was paid at irregular intervals and in uncertain amounts because of the government allotments. The court merely fixed a certain sum as alimony and directed that it be paid out of his income, without specifying by whom or when (Merriman, 103 Misc. 445, 171 Sup. 

252, 1918).

Stipulations

Not only the parties' agreement fixing the amount (as where it followed a motion for alimony, or where it was submitted at the trial to be incorporated in the judgment) but their agreement in general that an allowance be had, is approved. The litigants' rights are not thereby impaired and the agreement is merely in furtherance of the husband's duty. Nor are such agreements deemed to be an inducement to either an estrangement or a litigation, providing they are made while the parties are separated (Werner, 153 A.D. 719, 138 Sup. 633, 1912).

The agreements are proper even where they recite that they are made in contemplation of a decree and that they are to be incorporated therein, in the event that such a judgment is rendered. Expressions to the contrary, as that such a stipulation is improper without the sanction of the court (Daggett, 5 Paige 509, 1835), are inaccurate.

An order or judgment rendered on a stipulation is as binding and effective as one had after a litigated motion or trial (Cohn, 120 Misc. 731, 200 Sup. 431, 1923). It cannot be altered or modified by another agreement of the parties, but only by a formal order of the court (Jones, 90 Hun, 414, 35 Sup. 877, 1895; Hobby, 5 A.D. 

496, 39 Sup. 36, 1896; Gewirtz, 189 A.D. 483, 178 Sup. 

738, 1919; Hallow, 200 A.D. 642, 193 Sup. 460, 1922).

The stipulation may be signed by respective counsel (Slater, L. J., Bronx Co., Jan. 10, 1931). If they have not been authorized to do it, the stipulation may be set aside at the instance of the aggrieved party. Acceptance by the wife of a check for alimony in the amount agreed upon by counsel was held not to constitute a ratification (Hallow, supra).

The court will ignore the parties' arrangements pro-

vided for in their stipulations as to the custody of children, if their effect will be to fix an inadequate sum for their support (Cariola, 131 Misc. 66, 225 Sup. 692, 1927). 

Necessity for personal service

Personal service of the summons must be effected, or the husband must appear following constructive service, before alimony may be had. Alimony is a personal mandate and must be predicated on jurisdiction in personam. 

Our statute makes no provision for an allowance based on constructive service, and such a statute, if it existed, would be illegal and in violation of the "due process" clause of the Federal Constitution (Burch, 116 A.D. 865, 102 Sup. 305, 1907; Edwards vs Edson, 119 A.D. 684, 104 Sup. 292, 1907; Myers, 131 Misc. 318, 227 Sup. 13, 1928; Stoltz, 135 Misc. 713, 238 Sup. 207, 1929; 

Matthews, 247 N. Y. 32, 1928, rev'g 220 A.D. 825, 222 

Sup. 852; Thompson vs Tanner, 287 Fed. 980, 1923; 

Laing vs Rigney, 160 U.S. 531, 1896, rev'g 127 N. Y. 

408). Intimations to the contrary (Waters, 7 Misc. 519, 27 Sup. 1004, 1894) are inaccurate.

The husband's subsequent appearance in opposition to a motion to punish him for contempt for non-payment of alimony does not cure the defect (Baylies, 196 

A.D. 677, 188 Sup. 147, 1921).

The refusal of the court to give validity to orders or judgments for the payment of alimony based on constructive service, as happens most often in the case of foreign judgments seeking recognition in this state, does not affect adversely the status of the proceedings had in so far as they determine the marital status. Alimony and the judgment of divorce have nothing, in legal contemplation, to do with each other (Matthews, supra).

Husband's default

The husband will be permitted, despite his default, to be heard on the question of alimony (Genauer, L. J. 

N. Y. Co., Nov. 12, 1930), and to appear at the inquest for that purpose (Musolino, id., Kings Co., Dec. 12, 1930). Even the husband's express admission that the wife is entitled to a decree does not preclude him from contesting the amount of alimony sought by her (Liebowitz, id., Queens Co., Feb. 26, 1931). 

Prayer for alimony 

The wife's complaint need not pray for an allowance of alimony, in order that an award be made. Alimony is not a part of the cause of action, and the request for it is embraced by the various recitals in the pleading, including the prayer for general equitable relief in the premises (Hecht, 14 Misc. 597, 36 Sup. 271, 1895). The same is true as to support for children (Kellogg, 122 Misc. 

734, 203 Sup. 757, 1924). But either party may set forth facts in the pleadings as a basis for determining the amount or the propriety of an award (Devaismes, 3 Code 

Rep. 124, 1850). 

Support for children

The provision for children when it constitutes the entire allowance, is not, properly speaking, alimony, and is referred to throughout this work as support for children. It will be granted to the wife only when she has their custody (Johnson, L. J., Queens Co., Jan. 22, 1931).

It continues until they are self-supporting or of age (Abbott, 132 Misc. 11, 228 Sup. 611, 1928; Skidmore, 160 

A.D. 594, 145 Sup. 939, 1914; Halsted, 228 A.D. 298, 239 

Sup. 422, 1930).

It is improper to make an allowance to the wife for her grandchildren (Parker, 189 A.D. 603, 179 Sup. 51, 1919); or for her step-children, because she is under no natural duty to care for them and is not as a rule entitled to their custody (Wood, 61 A.D. 96, 70 Sup. 72, 1901).

Separation agreements 

The allowance provided for in a separation agreement is neither alimony nor in the nature of alimony. The agreement is an ordinary civil contract between the parties, whereby the husband's duty to support the wife is measured, reduced to writing and made enforceable by ordinary civil remedies. The provision for the wife's support is sometimes referred to as alimony (Butler, 204 

A.D. 602, 198 Sup. 391, 1923), but it is not to be taken in the literal, i. e., legal sense. For that reason, separation agreements are treated here only in so far as they are related to or identified with the principles of alimony proper.

The differences between alimony and the allowances under separation agreements are legion, but for the purposes of the present, two instances will suffice the latter are assignable (Phinney vs Andrus, 108 Misc. 717, 178

Sup. 760, 1919) and are subject to the claims of the wife's creditors (Anna Tappe, Inc. vs Battelie, L. J., City Ct., N. Y. Co., Jan. 20, 1931).

TEMPORARY ALIMONY 

II 

A. The statute

This chapter is a discussion of the allowance provided by Section 1169 of the Civil Practice Act, more particu larly that part of it which specifies temporary alimony and support for children as distinguished from suit fees and other awards.

For a statement of the power of the court to award temporary alimony independently of the statute, see 

Ford, 10 Abb. Pr. (N.S.) 74, 1871.

Section 1169. Alimony and expenses in an action for divorce or separation. In an action for divorce or separation, the court, in its discretion, during the pendency thereof, from time to time, may make and modify an order or orders requiring the husband to pay any sum or sums of money necessary to enable the wife to carry on or defend the action, or to provide suitably for the education and maintenance of the children of the marriage, or for the support of the wife, having regard to the circumstances of the respective parties. 

As previously stated (IC, ante), Section 1170, which also makes provision for temporary alimony, is important chiefly for its specifications as to modification of the final judgment. For that reason it will be found in Chapter IV, relating to permanent alimony.

B. Nature of temporary alimony-defined and distinguished 

An intermediate provision

Temporary alimony is not a judgment. It is a mere 23

intermediate provision, made in the course of the action, enduring only pending the action and terminating with its conclusion (Surut, 191 A.D. 570, 181 Sup. 631, 1920).

Thus, enforcement cannot be effected through any agency or medium other than the action, and civil recovery for arrears may not be had (Flashberg, L. J., Kings Co., March 13, 1931). Discretionary 

The remedy is strictly discretionary (Bissell, 3 How. 

Pr. Rep. 242, 1847; Jones, 2 Barb. Ch. 146, 1847). It is possible that the allowance in actual practice is granted almost as a matter of course, but suggestions that the fact has support in legal principle (Hammond, 1 Clarke 

Ch. 151, 1839) are not to be followed. Except as authorized and assured by law, the remedy is a favor to the wife, affording her a summary means of enforcing her husband's duty to support her in the absence of legal evidence that she is entitled to ultimate relief. The right to the remedy, however, is absolute. Its waiver by the wife is a valuable consideration for the husband's promise, made before the action, to pay her a fixed sum, and the contract is enforceable civilly (Burnham, 97 Misc. 199, 162 Sup. 840, 1916). 

Difference from Police Court award

The function of the Domestic Relations Court (and other quasi-criminal tribunals) is not to adjust the conjugal status but to prevent public burdens, arising from the husband's failure to provide for his wife or children. 

Where her grievance entitles her to a decree in equity it is improper to refer her to the Police Court, particularly where her action is not based on desertion or abandon-

TEMPORARY ALIMONY 

25 ment (Miers, 35 Misc. 476, 71 Sup. 1058, 1901, mod'd and aff'd 65 A.D. 615, 73 Sup. 1141; Weigand, 103 A.D. 42, 92 Sup. 679, 1905).

Even where the gravamen is non-support, extreme care must be exercised to ascertain that resort to the Police Court will substantially serve the wife's purpose and meet the ends of justice before referring her there. This is a departure from a rule which formerly found some favor (Le 

Bowsky, 27 Misc. 759, 59 Sup. 499, 1899; Israel, 28 Misc. 

57, 59 Sup. 800, 1899, aff'd 46 A.D. 623).

Where the circumstances of the parties are such, however, as to show clearly that what the wife is seeking, despite her appeal to the Supreme Court, is support and nothing else—as where they are very poor, actually separated and the action is for a separation—it is proper to refuse her temporary alimony. In such a case, both her interests and those of the community make it expedient that the matter be determined in the Police Court (Ruopp, 1 Gib. 380, 35 Sup. 251, 1894; Patton, 13 Misc. 

726, 35 Sup. 250, 1895; Rosenblatt, L. J., N. Y. Co., Dec. 

18, 1930). 

C. The motion

The venue 

The motion for temporary alimony may be deferred pending the determination of another motion to change the venue of the cause (Zenn, L. J., N. Y. Co., Nov. 29, 1930), although there is no such requirement in the statute. 

When motion may be made

The motion is in order at any time after commencement of the action and before final judgment. It is

enough that a summons alone has been served (Whitney, 22 How. Pr. 175, 1861). Where the wife is defendant, there must be joinder of issue first (Lewis, 3 Johns. Ch. 

519, 1818; Nelson, Divorce and Separation, Vol. II, p. 799).

The practice of moving for alimony simultaneously with the commencement of the action, where the wife is plaintiff, is proper and has always been resorted to (Louden, 65 How. Pr. 411, 1883).

The motion may be brought on after the case appears on the calendar and very shortly before it is due to be tried (Cook, L. J., Bronx Co., April 28, 1931). It is improper pending trial, whether before the court or the referee (Fessenden, id., N. Y. Co., Feb. 21, 1931), although in another case, because of the special circumstances, the allowance was granted after the filing of the referee's report (Schloemer, 49 N. Y. 82, 1872).

It was entertained following dismissal of the husband's action and before entry of judgment (Freeman, 8 Abb. Pr. (N. C.) 174, 1880).

The motion may be made after judgment if an appeal is taken, as the action is deemed thereby to be pending (Bauman, 209 A.D. 238, 204 Sup. 265, 1924).

It is proper even though the wife has been stayed from proceeding with the action pending the determination of another action between the same parties (Grisenow, L. J., N. Y. Co., Dec. 3, 1930). But see Russo, id., Kings Co., Jan. 22, 1931. 

Necessary papers

The moving papers consist of a notice of motion (or order to show cause, which may be a judge's order), an affidavit by the wife, corroborating affidavits if the wife sues and the action is for divorce, and copies of the plead-

TEMPORARY ALIMONY 

27 ings. The motion will not be entertained on the pleadings alone, as was once permitted (Bissell, 3 How. Pr. 

242, 1847).

The notice of motion reads as follows: 

Supreme Court: New York County 

... X 

MARY SMITH 

-against- 

WILLIAM SMITH 

Plaintiff Defendant

Sir:

Please take notice that on the annexed affidavits of Mary 

Smith, Henry Jones and Thomas Brown, all duly sworn to on

May 5, 1931, copies of the pleadings and on all the other proceedings heretofore had herein, the undersigned will move this Court, at a Special Term, Part 1 thereof, at the County Courthouse, in the Borough of Manhattan, City of New 

York, on the 15th day of May, 1931, at 10 o'clock in the forenoon on said day, or as soon thereafter as counsel can be heard, for an order directing the defendant to pay to the plaintiff temporary alimony for her support and for the support, maintenance and education of the two infant children of the parties hereto, and a sum as counsel fees to defray the plaintiff's expense of prosecuting this action; and for such other and further relief as may be just. 

Dated, New York City, May 9, 1931. 

Yours, etc. 

Frank Graham 

Attorney for Plaintiff

Office & P. O. Address 

1 Wall Street

Borough of Manhattan 

New York City 

To: John Cooper, Esq. 

Attorney for Defendant (or to defendant in person)

The notice need not specify the amount desired. 

The wife's affidavit must set forth all the facts of the married life of the parties, with particular stress upon those incidents and events which predicate the wife's action or defence. It should also set forth her pecuniary status and the husband's means, to afford the court a basis for determining the amount of the allowance (Kennedy, L. J., N. Y. Co., Dec. 19, 1930).

It must appear that the application is at the wife's instance, and if all the papers disclose that she has abandoned the action and does not desire to continue it, and that the motion is being prosecuted solely by her attorney, it must be denied (Furman, id., Dec. 9, 1930).

The disqualification of either spouse from testifying to the other's adultery extends to depositions, and the parties are not free to make the charges in the moving or opposing papers, if objection is made by the other side; and the same is true of confidential communications (Stillman, 115 Misc. 106, 187 Sup. 383, 1921).

Corroboration is preferable, but not absolutely necessary, in separation actions brought by the wife (Nester, L. J., Queens Co., Jan. 22, 1931). 

Replying papers

When the wife is given leave to reply, the husband should have the same right (Poillon, 75 A.D. 536, 78 Sup. 

323, 1902; Browning, L. J., N. Y. Co., Feb. 25, 1931). 

Construction of motion papers

The court ignores formalities in passing on the respective affidavits. The wife's failure to state that the parties were married, in an application for counsel fees which is governed by the same general principles as one for ali-

TEMPORARY ALIMONY 

29 mony, was deemed cured by the pleadings which she had made part of her motion, and in which were recited the marriage, in the husband's complaint, and an admission of it, in her answer, coupled with her own statement that she was the defendant and desired in good faith to resist the suit (Dean, 48 Misc. 149, 96 Sup. 472, 1905).

The court disregarded the fact that the wife's affidavits were largely scandalous and irrelevant (Hawley, 95 A.D. 

274, 88 Sup. 606, 1904).

References 

References were formerly limited to investigations into the means of the parties, which are taken up in the discussion on the amount of alimony which the courts allow (IE, ante). They were not favored as inquiries into the merits of the applications, because they would be tantamount to trials on short notice, particularly, if the parties were too poor to afford a reference (Monroy, 1 Edw. 

Ch. 382, 1832). Thus, the courts were reluctant even to refer any particular issue, such as the residence of the parties on the question of jurisdiction (Lloyd, 225 A.D. 

652, 1928).

Of late, however, a tendency has grown among judges to refer the entire motion when the facts are involved and incapable or difficult of ascertainment from the affidavits (Schane, L. J. N. Y. Co., March 12, 1931). 

when... neither the equities of the situation nor the financial circumstances of the parties appear in bold relief, and thus do not shape the proper determination, the court declines to pass upon the papers, but refers the matter to an official referee to hear and report with his recommendations." (Albes, 137 Misc. 

356, 242 Sup. 3, 1930, p. 5)

1 The practice, however, is not entirely new. In an early case (Ward, 21 Sup. 795, 1892), the province of the reference was defined. The wife defaulted on one occasion during the hearings, and the default was noted. The husband's objection to the confirmation of the report, on the ground of the default, was overruled. The usual rules of trial practice were held to be inapplicable, because the reference was not a trial but a proceeding designed to "inform the court's conscience."

References may be directed even after an award has been made, to determine if it is adequate (Boyce, L. J., N. Y. Co., Jan. 2, 1931). Such a course is expedient when the wife is in severe straits and requires an immediate provision, which a formal hearing might unduly delay. 

D. The order

The order awarding temporary alimony (and support for children and counsel fees) reads as follows:

PRESENT 

Hon.

At a Special Term, Part 1, of the Supreme 

Court of the State of New York, held in and for the County of New York, at the County 

Courthouse, in the Borough of Manhattan, City of New York, on the 18th day of May, 1931 

JUSTICE MARY SMITH 

-against- 

WILLIAM SMITH 

Plaintiff Defendant

TEMPORARY ALIMONY 

31 A motion having duly come on to be heard before this Court on the 15th day of May, 1931, for an order directing the defendant to pay to the plaintiff temporary alimony and counsel fees, and after reading and filing the affidavits of Mary Smith, Henry Jones and Thomas Brown, all sworn to on May 5, 1931, the notice of motion dated May 9, 1931, copies of the complaint and answer annexed to the moving papers and the affidavit of Robert Lyons, sworn to on May 1, 1931, showing due personal service upon the defendant of the summons herein, all submitted in support of the motion; and the affidavits of 

William Smith and Charles Perry, both sworn to on May 12, 1931, submitted in opposition thereto; and after hearing Frank 

Graham, Esq., attorney for the plaintiff and John Cooper,

Esq., attorney for the defendant, for and against the motio respectively, and due deliberation having been had, NOW, on motion of Frank Graham, Esq., attorney for the plaintiff, and upon filing the opinion of the Court, it is ORDERED that the motion be and the same hereby is granted, and it is

FURTHER ORDERED that the defendant pay to the plaintiff, at the office of Frank Graham, her attorney, 1 Wall 

Street, New York City, the sum of Thirty ($30.00) dollars per week as temporary alimony for the support of the plaintiff and for the support, maintenance and education of the two infant children of the parties hereto, payments to commence on May 15, 1931 and to be made every week regularly thereafter, in advance; and it is

FURTHER ORDERED that the defendant pay to the plaintiff, at the office of her attorney as aforesaid, the sum of One hundred and fifty ($150.00) dollars as counsel fees (payable in two instalments of Seventy-five ($75.00) dollars each, the first instalment to be paid within ten days from the date of service upon the attorney for the defendant of a copy of this order, the balance on or before the day of trial).

Enter 

J. S. C.

The foregoing is the usual form. It is advisable, if possible, to make separate provision in the order for alimony and for support for children, as it may facilitate a

disposition of the allowance in the event of modification, etc. (Brunkhardt, L. J., Kings Co., March 11, 1931).

Otherwise there will be no way of distinguishing the portion of the fund intended for the one and for the other (Tooker, 137 Misc. 158, 241 Sup. 623, 1930).

It would seem that the alimony may be made payable from the date of service of the motion, (Horter, 177 A.D. 

827, 164 Sup. 889, 1917) a fact which attains a degree of importance when the argument is adjourned for any length of time; but the settled practice has been to make it payable from the return day, or, more often, from the day the matter is finally heard.

Short form orders are not recommended, but they may be sufficient for the purpose of enforcement (Adams, 179 

A.D. 152, 166 Sup. 167, 1917). 

E. Determination of the motion-considerations before the court 

Governing principle 

Temporary alimony will be granted only if the wife shows a reasonable probability of succeeding in the action and lack of sufficient means of her own to support herself and to carry on or defend the action (Jones, 2 

Barb. Ch. 146, 1847; Maxwell, 28 Hun, 566, 1883; Desbrough, 29 Hun, 592, 1883; Procita, 190 Sup. 21, 1921).

The suggestion that the purpose of the moving and opposing papers is primarily to afford the court a basis for determining the amount to be allowed (Wright, 1 Edw. 

Ch. 62, 1831) is not to be followed. 

Reasonable probability of success

It is a requirement that the wife show merits (Boubon, 3 Rob. 715, 1865), and is justified by the warrant

TEMPORARY ALIMONY 

33 of discretion conferred by the statute (Whitney, 22 How. 

Pr. 175, 1861).

The probability can arise only from at least a prima facie showing by the wife (Weigand, 103 A.D. 42, 92 

Sup. 679, 1905). For this reason, the husband's position must be ascertained; if he is defendant, the merits of his defence must be examined (Snodgrass, L. J., N. Y. Co., Feb. 27, 1931).

If from all the facts it is clearly apparent that the wife cannot succeed, the motion must be denied; as where she sued for separation while a prior separation decree was still subsisting and in force (Hobby, 5 A.D. 

496, 39 Sup. 36, 1896).

There are three recognized exceptions to the rule that the allowance will be granted only on the wife's showing of a fair prospect of success, if the motion is otherwise deserving, and denied on her failure to make the showing. If the wife is defendant but is guilty of no wilful misconduct, as where the action is to annul the marriage for her insanity, alimony will be allowed even though the action seems meritorious (Dolgenis, L. J., Kings Co., April 15, 1931).

It will be refused if the action is to annul on the ground that the defendant wife had a husband living when the second marriage was contracted, which fact is conceded, even though she satisfies the court that she has a good chance of defeating the action for the plaintiff's bad faith in entering into the marriage (Appleton vs Warner, 51 Barb. 270, 1868; Hopper, 92 Hun, 415, 36 Sup. 610, 1895).

The third exception is in divorce actions brought by the husband (II F II B, post).

The wife has the burden of showing the probability of succeeding (Dye, 140 A.D. 309, 125 Sup. 242, 1910).

See also, Hollerman, 1 Barb. 64, 1847; Kirsch, 18 Sup. 

447, 1892; Heyman, 119 A.D. 182, 104 Sup. 227, 1907; 

Beck, 2 Brad. 498, 1912; Hobbs, id., 517, 1912; Abramowitz, 140 Sup. 275, 1913; Tabor, 140 Sup. 313, 1913, aff'd 

141 Sup. 1148; Merrell, 168 A.D. 896, 152 Sup. 970, 1915; 

DeVide, 186 A.D. 814, 174 Sup. 774, 1919; Domb, 195 

A.D. 526, 186 Sup. 306, 1921; Rolnick, 223 A.D. 772, 227 

Sup. 45, 1928; Downes, 225 A.D. 886, 233 Sup. 39, 1929; 

Pagnucco, L. J., Bronx Co., Dec. 30, 1930.

Insufficiency of means Temporary alimony will be denied if the wife has sufficient means to maintain herself (Rodell, 176 Sup. 919, 1919, rev❜g 175 Sup. 268; Goodale, 225 A.D. 791, 1929; 

Gumpel, L. J., Kings Co., Jan. 29, 1931; Cuff, id., N. Y. 

Co., April 3, 1931).

When the question is very close, the court may be influenced by other considerations, such as the reasonable probability of success (Brand, 178 A.D. 822, 166 Sup. 90, 1917).

The wife has the same burden of proof as to insufficiency of means. When the husband raises the issue, the wife must meet it (Klesper, 181 Sup. 941, 1920). 

Rule as to alimony and counsel fees

Inasmuch as somewhat different considerations determine alimony and counsel fees, it is necessary to restate the foregoing propositions in the form of a single embracing principle, in order to harmonize the numerous and frequently conflicting decisions which have been handed down, and which continue to appear in larger numbers, in Special Term.

If the wife makes out a prima facie case and shows a lack of sufficient means of her own, she will be granted

TEMPORARY ALIMONY 

35 counsel fees; if the opposing papers then fail to throw any serious doubt upon her chances of succeeding, she will be granted alimony as well (Douglas, 13 Abb. Pr. (N.S.) 291, 1872; Dimond, 124 Sup. 493, 1910). "The mere allegation of an abandonment and neglect or refusal (to support the wife) are not sufficient to warrant the granting of alimony where they are denied. There must be something more than this and with reasonable certainty of its truth presented for the consideration of the court. The plaintiff must make it appear that she has been injured and a meritorious cause of action must be presented. It should also appear that she is unable to support herself. . . . With regard to the counsel fee, the rule is different, inasmuch as one may be granted where the plaintiff's statement makes out a prima facie case and her own income is not sufficient to support her and to pay the expense of a suit." (Bertschy, 14 N. Y. W'kly 

Dig. 111, 1882, pp. 111, 112). 

The Dimond case, supra, was cited in Browne, 9 Civ. 

Pro. Rep. 180, 1886, where the court said: "The principle governing the subject . . . is that the wife should be granted a counsel fee when her own statement makes out a prima facie case, and her income is not sufficient to support her and defray the expenses of the suit. The reason of the rule . . . is that it does not follow that the plaintiff may not maintain her complaint although it is seemingly answered." (p. 181).

The expression "means," in this connection, requires amplification, for it may mean either the degree with which the wife is supporting herself or her ability to do so at all, on the one hand; or, the ability to pay the expenses of the action, as distinguished from support, on the other. 

Taking all the cases together, the underlying principle may be stated as follows: if the wife makes out a prima facie case, and shows that she lacks funds which are

readily available and sufficient to pay her expenses (such as money in the bank), she will be granted counsel fees; if, in addition, it appears that she has a fair prospect of succeeding and is unable to support herself during the action (as that she is unaccustomed or unable to work, or cannot obtain employment), she will be granted alimony.

This accounts for the numerical excess of cases granting counsel fees and refusing alimony (Shaw, 5 Misc. 

497, 26 Sup. 715, 1893; Smith, 92 A.D. 442, 87 Sup. 137, 1904; Rodell, 176 Sup. 919, 1919, rev'g 175 Sup. 268) over those refusing the one and granting the other (Klein, L. J., Bronx Co., Nov. 26, 1930).

Possession of funds by the wife which are sufficient both to support her during the action and to defray the expenses of the suit will preclude an allowance for either alimony or counsel fees, regardless of her earnings or earning power and whether she shows a prospect of succeeding or not (Osgood, 2 Paige Ch. 621, 1831; Sawyer, 108 Misc. 447, 178 Sup. 472, 1919; Huber, L. J., Queens 

Co., Jan. 29, 1931).

Facts which may be passed upon The court, as in any other case, is limited to the papers; but exceptions are made when the extrinsic facts appear plainly and have a strong bearing on the controversy. Thus, the wife's appearance in the courtroom, following shortly after the alleged assault by the husband, was taken into consideration (Jewett, L. J., Bronx 

Co., Nov. 29, 1930). When the motion follows a direction for a new trial, it is proper to submit the stenographic record of the first (Procita, 190 Sup. 21, 1921). The court will not go into events occurring before the mar-

TEMPORARY ALIMONY 

37 riage (Gould, 125 A.D. 375, 109 Sup. 910, 1908), unless they are pertinent to the issues (Meyers, L. J., Queens 

Co., Nov. 17, 1930). Conditions

It is improper to condition the allowance on the parties' consent (Patterson, 4 A.D. 146, 38 Sup. 637, 1896), or on the wife's leaving the husband's residence (Smith, 92 

A.D. 442, 87 Sup. 137, 1904) or on her waiving a jury trial (Lowenthal, 68 Hun, 366, 22 Sup. 858, 1893); but she was required to accept a short notice of trial where the action was commenced a long time after the alleged abandonment by the husband (Brown, 109 Sup. 637, 1908, aff'd 110 Sup. 1123), and to consent to the withdrawal of a joint bank account (Goodman, L. J., Bronx Co., March 

12, 1931) and to consent to the modification of the injunctive provision of a judgment against the husband which tied up his assets (Stokes, 205 A.D. 328, 199 Sup. 504, 1923). The amount allowed was conditioned on the husband's paying the rent of his wife's apartment (Hirschfeld, L. J., Bronx Co., March 12, 1931), in connection with which see the discussion on Amount (IE, ante). 

Support for children

Support for children pending the action may be refused if it appears that they have been taken from the husband by the wife against his consent, and he is desirous of having them back and to support them (Coddington, 10 Abb. 

Pr. 450, 1860). 

Default by husband

The wife must make the requisite showing of merits and lack of means even though the husband defaults on the motion. An allowance will not follow as a matter of

course (Michelson, 136 Sup. 533, 1912; Rosenthal, L. J., Kings Co., April 15, 1931).

She was not relieved of the duty even by the statement made in open court by the husband's attorney that his client would not defend the action, together with the filing of an unverified answer and the giving of notice that the plaintiff's witnesses would not be cross-examined on the issue of adultery (Conklin, 196 A.D. 607, 188 Sup. 

141, 1921, mod'g 186 Sup. 191).

F. Classification of cases-objections to the motion and defences by the wife

Motions for alimony may be conveniently classified into two groups, one according to the nature of the objection which is urged by the husband and which is either to the motion, or to the action where the wife sues; the other according to the defence interposed by the wife, in actions brought against her. The discussion which follows does not purport to take up every situation or variety of situation which may come up, but only those which are frequent, or which, for one reason or another, are important or significant.

The following outline will disclose the scope of the subject under discussion:

I. HUSBAND'S OBJECTIONS 

A. To the action 

1. Insufficiency of complaint 

2. Lack of jurisdiction 3. 4. 

No marriage 

Wife's bad faith 

B. To the motion 

5. Separation agreements

TEMPORARY ALIMONY 

39 6. Settlements 

7. Other allowance pending 

8. Other action pending 

9. Lack of corroboration, indefiniteness and incompetency of proof 

10. Husband's poverty 

11. Wife's bad faith

II. WIFE'S DEFENCES 

A. Prior divorce decree

B. Denial of guilt in divorce actions 

Insufficiency of complaint (IA1) 

The wife's complaint must be sufficient (Whitney, 22 

How. Pr. 175, 1861; Bucki, 70 Hun, 598, 24 Sup. 378, 1893; Itzkowitz, 33 A.D. 244, 53 Sup. 356, 1898; Nelson, Divorce and Separation, vol. II, p. 799), and the action must be brought within the time limited by statute (Sturm, 80 Misc. 277, 141 Sup. 61, 1913).

Thus, the motion was denied where the wife failed to specify the time and place of the acts of cruelty charged, as required by Rule 280 (Mackintosh, 44 A.D. 118, 60 

Sup. 679, 1899; Brownstein, L. J., Bronx Co., Jan. 22, 1931). 

Her summons must be properly endorsed (Rudolph, 19 

Civ. Pro. Rep. 424, 12 Sup. 81, 1890). 

Lack of jurisdiction (IA2)

The motion will be denied for want of jurisdiction only when the defect appears clearly, as where the wife's complaint failed to make the necessary recital as to residence of the parties (Conrad, 123 A.D. 384, 107 Sup. 1093, 1908; 

Thomas, 216 A.D. 546, 215 Sup. 405, 1926; Kriegel, L. J., N. Y. Co., April 22, 1931). When the wife disputes the

claim of want of jurisdiction, and raises a substantial issue, the court will generally defer it for trial and award alimony in the interim (Mix, 1 Johns. Ch. 108, 1814; 

Gray, 143 N. Y. 354, 1894; Ensign, 54 Misc. 289, 105 Sup. 

917, 1907, aff'd 128 A.D. 882; Wade, 158 Sup. 555, 1916).

No marriage (IA3) 

The following comments are adequate: "To justify an order for temporary alimony, there must have been a marriage either valid in fact or by the parties supposed to be valid, by reason whereof they have entered upon those mutual property relations which govern matrimonial cohabitation. Further than this it need not be good in law. Of course, a marriage of some sort, adapted to produce and therefore presumptively producing the consequence thus stated, must, as a foundation for temporary alimony, be either admitted or proved, or there must be some entering upon a matrimonial relation. But as the legal validity of the marriage is not essential to this preliminary order for the woman's support, so neither are the proofs of it required to be so conclusive as on a final decree for permanent alimony." (Bishop, Marriage and 

Divorce, vol. II, p. 373; italics ours)

The reference to "property relations" signifies the husband's right to the possession and enjoyment of his wife's property, which under common law predicated her reciprocal right to be supported by him. The statement is therefore now qualified to the extent that the situation has changed. in an application for temporary alimony and the expenses of the action, although there may be in the answer a general denial of the existence at any time of the marital relation, the court has the power from the affidavits and other papers presented to it to pass upon the question for the purposes of the application, and it is not bound down to the allegations of the complaint and ፡፡

TEMPORARY ALIMONY 

41 the denial of the answer if other papers or proofs are submitted to it; and though the denial of the answer, if standing alone, would bring the case within the rule that where no marital relation is admitted or proven there is no right to alimony; yet if the matters contained in other papers or shown by legitimate proofs before the court make out in the judgment of the court a fair presumption of marriage, it has the power to grant alimony pending the action and expenses of the action." (Brinkley, 50 

N. Y. 184, 1872, p. 194; italics ours)

Thus, alimony was granted despite the husband's claim that there was no marriage in Vincent, 16 Daly, 534, 17 

Sup. 497, 1891; in Unger, 152 A. D. 828, 137 Sup. 730, 1912, where the alleged prior and subsisting marriage of the wife was by common law, and the court deemed the issue clearly deserving of a trial on the merits; and Lau, 140 Sup. 310, 1913, where the husband again claimed that the wife's marriage to him was bigamous on her part, and the court declared that the mere fact of a ceremonial marriage having taken place between them imposed upon him the burden of establishing its invalidity.

If the husband claims that the marriage has been dissolved, as by a foreign judgment, and the wife raises a serious question as to its validity, the issue must be deferred for trial and alimony allowed (Kramrath, A. D., 1st Dep't., December, 1930).

The allowance will be denied if the absence or invalidity of the marital relation appears beyond doubt (Blinks, 5 Misc. 193, 25 Sup. 768, 1893; Dye, 140 A. D. 

309, 125 Sup. 242, 1910; Collins, 71 N. Y. 269, rev'g 10 

Hun, 272, 1877, also 80 N. Y. 1, 1880, citing Mann, 75 

N. Y. 614, 1878). See also Herforth, 2 Abb. Pr. (N.S.) 

483, 1867 and Walsh, 4 Misc. 448, 24 Sup. 335, 1893, appeal dismissed 139 N. Y. 624.

Wife's bad faith (IA4)

This consists of such behaviour by the wife as to constitute a defence to her action, either in that it comprises a specific ground recognized in the law as an objection to equitable relief, or in her failure generally to come in with clean hands. Characteristic instances are: where she resided in the same house with the husband when the motion was made (Bruggeman, 191 A. D. 689, 182 Sup. 

87, 1920); where she cohabited with him (Mazzer, L. J., Bronx Co., Dec. 12, 1930); where the opposing papers in her divorce action showed very strongly conspiracy on her part (Pincus, 210 A.D. 100, 205 Sup. 425, 1924).

The allowance was refused in the wife's divorce action, where she had obtained a prior judgment of divorce against the defendant and which was void for want of jurisdiction, but on the strength of which he had remarried, in absolute good faith, and committed the adultery alleged (Ober, 54 Hun, 639, 7 Sup. 843, 1899). The circumstances would have to be such as to indicate beyond question not only that she was solely responsible for the developments leading up to the husband's offence, but that she had in some overt manner brought them about, in order that the probabilities of succeeding be in the husband's favor.

Separation agreements (IB5) 

The existence of a valid and subsisting separation agreement between the parties is a bar to temporary alimony. The parties having adjusted the amount to be paid for the wife's support, as well as the manner of payment, both to their own satisfaction and in accordance with the requirements of law, there is neither the occasion nor the propriety of an award by the court (Collins,

TEMPORARY ALIMONY 

43 

80 N. Y. 1, 1880; Desbrough, 29 Hun, 592, 1883; Taylor, 32 Misc. 312, 66 Sup. 561, 1900; Grube, 65 A.D. 239, 72 

Sup. 529, 1901; Gutmann, 178 Sup. 833, 1919; Rosenblatt, 209 A. D. 373, 204 Sup. 676, 1924, rev'g 123 Misc. 

100, 203 Sup. 798; Ascher, 213 A. D. 183, 210 Sup. 515, 1925; Blueglass, 127 Misc. 157, 215 Sup. 325, 1926; Smith, 127 Misc. 764, 217 Sup. 3, 1926; Schifferdecker, 219 A. D. 

740, 220 Sup. 229, 1927).

The agreement is also a bar to support for children. (Wachtel, L. J., Bronx Co., Jan. 22, 1931).

An agreement which is void for its violation of Section 

51 of the Domestic Relations Law, prohibiting contracts which assume to relieve the husband of the duty of supporting his wife, is ineffectual to bar alimony (Kaufman, 2 Brad. 519, 1912); or for any other reason which makes it defective on its face or which appears clearly from the motion papers (Hobby, 5 A. D. 496, 39 Sup. 36, 1896), as that the parties were living together when the agreement was made (Maney, 119 A.D. 765, 104 Sup. 541, 1907). If the defect is not patent and one which makes the agreement voidable, and the circumstances which the wife claims invalidate it are disputed by the husband, the agreement precludes an allowance (Aigner, 211 A. D. 834, 206 Sup. 877, 1924, rev'g 123 Misc. 101, 203 Sup. 780).

The court will not pass upon its sufficiency on the motion, as the wife has her remedy by way of an action to set it aside.

Such an action may be joined in the matrimonial complaint. The motion, of course, stands in the same position. It will be looked on with even less favor by the court if she sues for separation, and it appears that her purpose in having the agreement set aside is to make the voluntary and mutual parting of the parties an abandonment by the husband, or his participation in the execu-

tion of the agreement an act of duress towards her (Curtis, 29 Misc. 257, 61 Sup. 59, 1899).

If the husband has on a prior occasion denied the validity of the agreement, as in her action to recover the arrears, he may not urge it as a defence to the motion (Winton, 161 Sup. 405, 1916).

If the agreement has been breached by the husband, as by his failure to make the required payments, the institution of the matrimonial action by the wife constitutes her acquiescence in the breach, and the result is a mutual repudiation. The husband may not thereafter avail himself of the agreement in defence to her motion for alimony (Scheinkman, 64 Misc. 443, 118 Sup. 775, 1909; Murnane, 256 N. Y. 7, aff'g 230 A. D. 820, 244 Sup. 881, 1930; Kennedy, L. J., N. Y. Co., Dec. 19, 1930; De Matteo, id., Feb. 10, 1931).

The husband's default must be substantial, however, to constitute a breach. "Suffice it to say that in my opinion the authorities are ample to sustain the proposition that so far as the question of breach depends upon an issue of performance, until there has been such substantial failure to perform as amounts to 'a failure of consideration' or there has been a repudiation of the agreement or other acts amounting to a practical rescission on the part of the husband, the wife must look to the agreement, and cannot ignore it and obtain provision for her support in the form of alimony in any action she may bring to dissolve or modify the marital status" (Landes, 94 Misc. 486, 159 Sup. 586, p. 588, aff'd 

172 A.D. 758, 159 Sup. 230, 1916; 170 A.D. 898, 154 Sup. 

1129, 1915).

Even if the agreement has been violated by the husband, but a substantial lump sum has been paid by him in partial compliance, an award may be improper because the wife is likely to have enough of the money left to maintain herself (Smith, supra; IIE, ante).

TEMPORARY ALIMONY 

45 A controversial feature of this phase of the law-i.e., the effect of separation agreements on the right to alimony-, is the precise point at which the wife repudiates the agreement. An impression might be had that the agreement does not bar temporary alimony, from a recent decision that the application for or granting of alimony, and not the mere commencement of the action by her, constitutes the repudiation (Hirschthal, 134 Misc. 479, 235 Sup. 451, 1929). Nothing was said about violation of the agreement by the husband: it would follow then, if the case is to be taken literally, and by necessary inference, that the very fact of the motion being made disposes of the agreement, and that an award is by consequence in order in every case. "The rationale of the cases seems to be that some provision must be had for the maintenance of the wife, either by the award of alimony or by the provisions of the agreement, and that the husband should not be placed under double liability. The conclusion is therefore reached that where the wife, as here, did not ask for and obtain alimony, the terms of the separation agreement continue in force." (p. 452) 

The case quotes Randolph vs Field, 165 A. D. 279, 150 

Sup. 822, modifying 84 Misc. 403, 146 Sup. 247, 1914, to the effect that the commencement of the action may or may not constitute a repudiation; but in that case, the husband had been in default under the agreement when the separation action was begun, and in another part of the opinion the court expressly emphasizes the husband's breach as the determining factor in the situation, not the request for or granting of alimony. "Any question which might arise as to whether the defendant's refusal to pay, under the separation agreement, were such a material breach of the contract within the inten-

tion of the parties as to amount to a repudiation, is set at rest by the complaint of this plaintiff in her action for a separation. She there specifically alleged that the defendant 'ceased making payments thereunder and repudiated said agreement. It is equally clear that the plaintiff has assented and acquiesced in such repudiation. 

Confronted with the alternative of relying on the separation agreement for her support, or repudiating it and asserting her marital rights to be supported by her husband, she chose the latter course. If the separation agreement were, at the time she commenced her action for a separation, a valid and subsisting contract, upon which she relied for support, then she could not obtain support by way of alimony in such action, or successfully prosecute the same. She, however, obviated any question as to her right to bring such action by pleading the separation agreement and its repudiation by the defendant. The institution by her of that action was, I think, not only an assent to the repudiation by the defendant, but a repudiation of it upon her part. Her election was further indicated by her acceptance of a counsel fee of $250 and $20 a week alimony." (p. 824; italics ours) 

To the same effect see Lawsberg, 171 A.D. 354, 156 Sup. 

1050, 1916.

The allowance is therefore merely further proof of the wife's repudiation of the agreement. The Hirschthal case also cites Newport, 131 Misc. 851, 228 Sup. 313, 1928, for the proposition that both the action and the motion are needed to make out a repudiation: but there as well the husband had been in arrears. In Butler, 206 

A.D. 214, 201 Sup. 111, 1923, the court went so far as to uphold the agreement in the face of a temporary award, paid and accepted, where the husband had not been in default under the agreement, nor had in any other way violated it. The award was therefore held to be improper, and being so, it could not give sanction to any incidental transaction—even if it was the wife's expressed intent to terminate the contract. In short, it required the joint ac-

TEMPORARY ALIMONY 

47 tion of the parties to bring the agreement to an end, just as it required their joint action to effectuate it. 

See also Hofmann vs Nestel, 146 A.D. 305, 130 Sup. 

775, 1911 and Dess, L. J., Kings Co., Feb. 4, 1931.

A distinction should be drawn between the defence of separation agreement to the motion, as in the foregoing, and to the action; as where an allowance was refused because the separation agreement showed the likelihood to be that what the wife claimed to be a desertion by the husband was in reality a mutual parting (Powers, 33 

A. D. 126, 53 Sup. 346, 1898).

Payments made or property transferred under a separate agreement which is mutually abandoned cannot be recovered by the husband, whether alimony is requested or granted, or not (Dess, supra).

Settlements (IB6) The wife's waiver and release of all support for a consideration which is actually paid, pursuant to an agreement which is fair and legal, is a bar to temporary alimony. Where the settlement is made on the discontinuance of a matrimonial action, no alimony may be had in a subsequent action by the wife (Greenfield, 161 A.D. 

573, 146 Sup. 865, 1914). Payment of the agreed sum operates to discharge the husband's duty, exactly as a separation agreement does. 

The wife may have alimony, however, if she returns or tenders back the money or property received (Rose, 11 

Paige Ch. 166, 1844; Grube, 65 A.D. 239, 72 Sup. 529, 1901).

In an action to set aside a settlement, provided in a separation agreement, a temporary allowance was refused on the ground that the wife had relinquished her special

rights incidental to the marital relation, including the very remedy which she now sought (aside from the fact that the action was non-matrimonial) (IA, ante; Davis, 195 A.D. 430, 186 Sup. 805, 1921). 

Other allowance pending (IB7)

If alimony has been allowed in a prior action, it must be waived before a new allowance may be had (Davis, 150 

Sup. 636, 1914).

The pendency of a Police Court award may have one of several results with respect to the application for alimony. The court may make an allowance in lieu of it (Dushinsky, 107 A.D. 607, 94 Sup. 638, 1905; Urbanovsky, L. J. Bronx Co., Dec. 12, 1930; Emanuel, id., Kings 

Co., April 22, 1931); or in addition to it (Peckerman, 91 

Misc. 114, 154 Sup. 297, 1915); or may deny the motion because of it (Fernandez, L. J., Kings Co., Feb. 18, 1931; 

Klimkowski, id., Feb. 25, 1931), presumably on the ground that the matter belongs in the Police Court (IIB, ante). 

Other action pending (IB8)

The allowance will be denied if another action between the same parties is pending, the determination of which will dispose of the immediate issues; but without prejudice to a renewal in the event that the prior action is decided in the wife's favor (Federman, L. J., Kings Co., April 1, 1931). The pendency of an appeal from an incidental order in the same action is not a bar (Robertson, 1 Edw. Ch. 360, 1832).

Lack of corroboration, indefiniteness and incompetency of proof (IB9)

Corroboration is an absolute prerequisite to the granting of alimony in divorce actions brought by the wife

TEMPORARY ALIMONY 

49 (Cassese, 223 A.D. 843, 228 Sup. 172, 1928). Where the corroborating witness died, the motion was denied (Byitte, L. J., Kings Co., Dec. 30, 1930).

If for any reason, whether it be that the wife has submitted her own affidavit without corroboration, or that her proofs could never gain admission in evidence, the moving papers could not establish the charges by regular and proper methods, the motion must be denied (Hodge, 85 Sup. 1133, 1904).
