Thus, there is the further requirement that the wife's accusations of the husband's adultery be made clear, specific and definite. If they are vague and uncertain, or made on information and belief, they are insufficient (Whitney, 22 How. Pr. 175, 1861; Monk, 7 Rob. (N. Y.) 

153, 1867; Moriarty, 59 Sup. Ct. Rep. 279, 10 Sup. 228, 1890; Shaw, 5 Misc. 497, 26 Sup. 715, 1893; Wood, 61 

A.D. 96, 70 Sup. 72, 1901; Schweig, 112 A.D. 786, 107 Sup. 

904, 1907). The motion was denied where the moving papers failed to show any impropriety in the conduct of the husband and the co-respondents (Werner, 204 A.D. 

791, 198 Sup. 701, 1923). 

Husband's poverty (IB10)

The motion was denied where the husband was penniless and so ill as to be unable to work (Newman, L. J., N. 

Y. Co., Feb. 21, 1931). The prevailing rule, however, particularly where the husband sues, is that poverty is not a defence. He should either provide the wife with the means of maintaining herself during the action, or abandon it (Purcell, 3 Edw. Ch. 194, 1838; Hallock, 4 How. Pr. 

160, 1849; Browne, 9 Civ. Pro. Rep. 180, 1886; Nottingham, 209 A.D. 462, 204 Sup. 753, 1924; Phillips, L. J., Kings Co., Dec. 12, 1930), unless she has sufficient means of her own (Mandel, id., Feb. 18, 1931).

If he had to borrow in order to bring the action, he should borrow for the wife's defence, (Russo, id., Bronx 

Co., Dec. 3, 1930).

The husband's poverty may be an important consideration when the moving papers are weak (Carpenter, 19 

How. Pr. 539, 1860). 

Wife's bad faith (IB 11)

This is an objection to the wife's right to alimony independently of the merits of the action. The allowance was denied to the defendant wife in an action to annul brought on the ground that she had a husband living when she contracted the marriage, the impediment arising from the fact that the divorce judgment of her prior marriage, although actually granted, had not yet been entered (Weiss, L. J., Kings Co., Feb. 5, 1931). She conceded the fact, and the records bore it out; but it also appeared that she had withheld the fact of her prior marriage from the marriage clerk and had instead told him that she had never been married. Otherwise the oversight would have been discovered, and because of her misrepresentation, was not.

Clearly the wife could not succeed, even aside from her untruthful statement, except on the ground of bad faith by the husband and that was not raised. Nevertheless, the wife would be entitled to an allowance, in the discretion of the court, under the first of the three exceptions to the rule that alimony will be granted only on a showing of reasonable probability of success-viz., that she had been guilty of no wilful misconduct (IIE, ante). Her behaviour in this instance brought the case squarely within the rule, and there was no other disposition consonant with justice than to deny the motion.

TEMPORARY ALIMONY 

51 No alimony will be granted a defendant wife who admits collusion (Crocker, Sheldon 257, 1872). 

Prior divorce decree (IIA)

If the wife sets up, in defense to the husband's action, that they are no longer married by reason of a prior divorce judgment obtained by her as in a foreign jurisdiction-and the husband denies its validity, the motion for alimony may be granted even though she is in effect asserting that she is not the plaintiff's wife. This is because the husband affirms the marital status but seeks to avoid it, and he should, therefore, make provision for her in the meantime. The issue of the validity of the prior decree must in any event await trial (Starkweather, 29 

Hun, 488, 1883).

This situation, however, would not seem to be different in principle from that which obtains when the wife sues to annul the marriage. The husband by resisting the action affirms the marital status, and the wife by bringing the action repudiates it, exactly as where she pleads the dissolution of it in his action. But in annulment actions, as has been so often laid down by the courts, the plaintiff wife may not have an allowance (IC, ante).

If the wife has remarried and admits cohabitation with her second husband, the motion must be denied, regardless of the propriety of her motives (Bailie, 30 A.D. 461, 52 Sup. 228, 1898, rev'g 53 Sup. 866).

The motion is in order, even if the wife has remarried, if she denies the adultery (Dean, 48 Misc. 149, 96 Sup. 

472, 1905), or sets up an affirmative defense such as recrimination (Starkweather, supra). See Phillips, L. J., Kings Co., Feb. 19, 1931.

In a case where the prior foreign decree is procured in pursuance of an arrangement between the parties, and

1 the husband defaults knowing that the proceedings are futile but for the deliberate purpose of thereafter suing the wife for a divorce in the event of her re-marriage (Bailie, 53 Sup. 866, 1898) a situation would arise which is analogous to that in the Ober case (IIF, IA4, ante), where the husband relied on the sufficiency of the decree and remarried, and the wife was suspected of bringing about his predicament. An allowance would be in order, even though the wife was clearly guilty of committing adultery, because the husband's lack of good faith might defeat his action. 

Husband's divorce action (IIB)

Alimony will be granted the defendant wife if she specifically denies the charges on oath or sets up an affirmative defence and there is a reasonable doubt as to her guilt (Purcell, 3 Edw. Ch. 194, 1838; Hallock, 4 How. Pr. 

160, 1849; Strong, 1 Abb. Pr. (N.S.) 358, 1866; Fricke, 4 Misc. 382, 24 Sup. 483, 1893; Rublinsky, 24 Sup. 920, 1893; Cohen, 11 Misc. 704, 32 Sup. 1082, 1895; Israel, 28 Misc. 57, 59 Sup. 800, 1899, aff'd 46 A.D. 623; Brown, 83 Misc. 597, 145 Sup. 471, 1914, aff'd 161 A.D. 948; 

Fishbaugh, 161 Sup. 446, 1916; Nottingham, 209 A.D. 

459, 204 Sup. 750, 1924). "The rule is well settled that where the wife denies on oath the charge of adultery she is entitled to alimony, unless the evidence of her adultery is so preponderating and convincing as to render it most improbable that she will succeed at the trial." (Glaser, 36 Misc. 231, 73 Sup. 

284, 1901, p. 232).

This is another one of the exceptions to the rule requiring a reasonable probability of success (IIE, ante; 

Bissell, 3 How. Pr. 242, 1847).

Thus, the motion was granted even though the oppos-

TEMPORARY ALIMONY 

53 ing papers were very strong (Boesenberg, 50 A.D. 622, 1900; Eisenbrock, 187 A. D. 85, 175 Sup. 67, 1919), and where the husband had already obtained judgment against the co-respondent for criminal conversation (Williams, 3 

Barb. Ch. 628, 1847); and even without consideration of financial circumstances (Wood, 2 Paige Ch. 108, 1830).

When the question is close, the fact that the husband has no means to pay any allowance will be considered (Griffin, 23 How. Pr. 189, 1861; Kock, 42 Barb. 515, 1864).

The motion was denied for the wife's failure to deny the charges on oath (Osgood, 2 Paige Ch. 621, 1831); to categorically deny them (Gronbach, 218 A.D. 848, 218 

Sup. 643, 1926); to make any refutation of the charge other than to state that she was not the woman observed (Stearns, 33 A.D. 630, 53 Sup. 348, 1898).

For the rule granting the allowance on the wife's affirmative defence see Clark, 7 Rob. 284, 1867 and Starkweather, 29 Hun, 488, 1883. 

G. Denials of the motion 

Legal basis

The refusal of alimony to a wife for her failure to make the necessary prerequisite showing, such as insufficiency of means, is not a matter of discretion but the settled rule of equity (Collins, 80 N. Y. 1, 1880). 

Res adjudicata

The denial precludes a further application for the same relief (Glasser, L. J., Kings Co., April 28, 1931; Rosenbaum, id., April 30, 1931). The denial of a motion for alimony and counsel fees bars another motion for alimony alone, unless the second application may be sus-

tainable as one for a modification for change of circumstances (Simonds, 57 Hun, 290, 10 Sup. 606, 1890). 

Support for children

Provision for children is granted even though alimony is refused (Hok, L. J., N. Y. Co., Dec. 16, 1930), and even though the refusal is because of the wife's failure to show a reasonable probability of succeeding and insufficiency of means (Trost, id., Kings Co., Dec. 9, 1930). Conditions 

The denial may be conditioned upon the husband's continuing the acts which he had set up in opposition to the motion, such as the payment of household expenses (Walsh, L. J., Kings Co., Dec. 9, 1930), the implication being that a renewal would be in order in the event of his default. Renewals

The motion may be renewed, just as in any case, when the denial is not on the merits (Horter, 177 A.D. 827, 164 

Sup. 889, 1917). It was denied without prejudice for the wife's failure to offer corroboration in her divorce action (Capes, 173 A.D. 142, 159 Sup. 367, 1916; Smith, L. J., Kings Co., Nov. 18, 1930), for insufficient corroboration (Berry, id., N. Y. Co., Dec. 9, 1930), for her failure to make the adultery charges definite (Schweig, 112 A.D. 

786, 107 Sup. 904, 1907), for making them on information and belief (Monk, 7 Rob. 153, 1867) and for lack of reasonable probability of success in general (Worden, 3 

Edw. Ch. 387, 1840; Simmons, L. J., N. Y. Co., Nov. 22, 1930). 

Leave to renew may be expressly conditioned, as upon the husband's failure to make the payments under a وتی جاتی ہوا

TEMPORARY ALIMONY 

55 separation agreement which had been the reason for the denial of the motion (Schifferdecker, 219 A.D. 740, 220 

Sup. 229, 1927).

Technicalities usually applicable to renewals are disregarded (Ensign, 54 Misc. 289, 105 Sup. 917, 1907, aff'd 

128 A.D. 882). 

H. Motion costs

A motion for temporary alimony is not one of those in which costs are specified by statute, and therefore, no more than ten dollars may be allowed, besides necessary disbursements for printing and referees' fees (Ward, 22 

Sup. 903, 1892; C. P. A. Sec. 1505). 

I. Incidental practice Rearguments

In one case the order granting the allowance was vacated in Special Term because the filing of supplemental pleadings had so changed the situation as to remove any likelihood of the wife's succeeding (Weiss, 135 Misc. 264, 238 Sup. 36, 1929); but the proper practice, it would seem, would be by way of reargument. Resettlements 

See Werner, 155 A. D. 928, 140 Sup. 1105, 1913, for an instance of a resettlement on appeal and certain features of the procedure incidental thereto. Appeals

The order granting an allowance is reviewable (Leslie, 6 Abb. Pr. (N.S.) 193, 1869; Patterson, 4 A.D. 146, 38 

Sup. 637, 1896), but only on the ground of abuse of dis-

cretion (Griffin, 23 How. Pr. 189, 1861; Aldrich, 26 Sup. 

344, 74 Hun, 638, 1893).

It is not reviewable by the Court of Appeals unless the lower court had no power to make the allowance, as where the wife's complaint was insufficient (Kennedy, 73 N. Y. 369, 1878).

The husband's right to appeal is absolute and is not impaired by his wilful disobedience of the order (Ascher, 213 A.D. 183, 210 Sup. 515, 1925).

The order refusing alimony is reviewable, but also only for discretionary abuse (Pettee, 64 Hun, 636, 19 

Sup. 311, 1892). The order was reversed when Special

Term had made as a condition of its refusal the husband's undertaking to support the wife (Mossa, 123 A.D. 400, 107 Sup. 1044, 1908). The Appellate Division has the same power to make the allowance as Special Term (Weigand, 103 A.D. 42, 92 Sup. 679, 1905; Parker, 140 

A.D. 899, 125 Sup. 161, 1910).

The allowance was set aside when it appeared that it was based upon an agreement between the wife and her attorney to give him a share of the alimony (Van Vleck, 21 A.D. 272, 47 Sup. 470, 1897).

The appeal must be taken during the action, because the order and all incidents of it abates with the action. 

But an exception is made when the allowance is grossly inadequate (Tibbets, 230 A.D. 66, 243 Sup. 217, 1930). "An improper pendente lite order for alimony and counsel fee, improper because grossly inadequate or plainly insufficient, survives a final judgment in a matrimonial action in so far as concerns the power of the Appellate Division to review such an order. The Appellate Division is a court of discretion, as is the Special Term, and, if the latter order was palpably unjust, the Appellate Division can make an order that should have been made in the first instance." (p. 218)

TEMPORARY ALIMONY 

57 

J. Temporary alimony on appeals from the judgment

The wife may have temporary alimony pending appeals from the final judgment, whether taken by her or by the husband. The appeal makes the action pending (IIC, ante; McBride, 55 Hun, 401, 8 Sup. 448, 1890, appeal dismissed 119 N. Y. 519), and Section 1169 providing for temporary allowances (as well as Section 1170) becomes applicable.

Exactly as where the application is made before judgment, the wife must show a reasonable probability of succeeding (on the appeal) and insufficiency of her own means. Thus, her motion was denied where her papers failed to show that she was unable to support herself, or to disclose the husband's means (Poillon, 75 A.D. 536, 78 Sup. 323, 1902).

The motion may be made at any stage of the appeal, whether it be pending review by the Appellate Division, the Court of Appeals or the United States Supreme 

Court (Haddock, 109 A. D. 502, 96 Sup. 522, 1905). The procedure is by application to Special Term to continue the allowance if one was made before judgment, or for a new one if one was not. The Appellate Division may grant it where it is improperly refused in Special Term (Haddock, supra; also 75 A.D. 565, 78 Sup. 304, 1902; 

Di Lorenzo, 78 A.D. 577, 79 Sup. 566, 1903).

When the appeal is from a judgment which includes a provision for alimony, the giving of a bond pending the husband's appeal does not stay a temporary allowance.

As was said in the Di Lorenzo case, supra, the motion for alimony is not a proceeding of such a nature as to enforce the judgment appealed from, within the meaning of the statute, and it is not, therefore, stayed. Nor is the husband's contention that he may be obliged to pay alimony

twice sound. The award is only temporary and may be considerably less than the final award, which was stayed, and the wife may be required to stipulate that sums paid to her be credited on the amount directed to be paid by the final judgment. This is the procedure which is customarily followed (Bauman, 209 A.D. 238, 204 Sup. 265, 1924). 

K. Stipulations

Alimony may be had on consent (Kinkade, L. J., N. Y. 

Co., Feb. 14, 1931).

The court upheld a stipulation providing that alimony be awarded, that the parties live apart, that the payments required by the order be made in full discharge of the husband's duty to support the wife and that in the event of his default, she be free to take steps for the enforcement of the order (Ronan, 32 Misc. 467, 60 Sup. 799, 1900); the agreement of the parties that the action be discontinued and that the husband make a certain provision for the wife's support, with leave to her to recontinue the action and move for alimony in the event of his default (Van Gieson, 26 A.D. 347, 49 Sup. 781, 1898); their agreement to reconciliate, and in the event of a failure of the reconciliation for the wife to renew the motion for alimony (which the court augmented by providing an allowance for the support of the child in the interim) (Downing, L. J. Kings Co., May 13, 1931). 

L. Modification

AUTOMATIC TERMINATION—ARREARS 

(1) Termination of the action

The termination of the action abates both the order and arrears (Wood, 7 Lans. 204, 1872; In re Thrall, 12

TEMPORARY ALIMONY 

59 

A.D. 235, 42 Sup. 439, 1896, aff'd 153 N. Y. 644; Pachter, 130 Misc. 769, 225 Sup. 56, 1927, rev'g 129 Misc. 302, 221 

Sup. 202; Zelkin, L. J., Bronx Co., April 24, 1931). When the jurisdiction to render a judgment is ended, no jurisdiction can remain as to matters purely ancillary to that object. Nor is there any survival of accrued arrears, for the right to temporary alimony is coincident with the existence of the action and by virtue of its pendency, in the same sense that the right to alimony in general is by virtue of the existence of a marriage. The action being uncertain as to duration and ultimate disposition, the right to the interlocutory payments is not vested. 

The statement made in one case that the husband may be precluded from discontinuing his action for his non-compliance with the temporary order, because it would be an impairment of the wife's rights (Leslie, 10 Abb. Pr. (N.S.) 64, 1871) is not to be taken as an authoritative dictum that the allowance is anything but provisional, interlocutory and indeterminate.

But the action is not deemed terminated for the purpose of abating the order unless and until judgment is entered. Thus, the order survives the finding by a jury against the defendant wife in a divorce action (Germond, 1 Paige Ch. 83, 1828; Stanford, 1 Edw. Ch. 317, 1832; 

Moncrief, 15 Abb. Pr. 187, 1862; Lusk, 31 Misc. 312, 65 Sup. 401, 1900), a decision unfavorable to the wife but before trial in Special Term (Horn, 73 Misc. 14, 130 

Sup. 591, 1911) and the filing of the referee's report in the husband's favor (Beadleston, 23 W'kly Dig. 365, 1886).

The rule has been qualified as to cases where adultery is the sole issue (Horn, supra).

In actual practice, however, it will be found that the courts are constrained to relieve the husband of the

order when the wife has been convicted of adultery, even though no judgment has been entered and regardless whether there were other issues. Thus, her motion to enforce payment of arrears was held in abeyance ten days to permit the husband to submit a decree (Dietz, 136 Sup. 341, 1912), and was denied outright (Calvin, L. J., Kings Co., Feb. 11, 1931).

For an instance of temporary arrears being preserved in the judgment see Lubow, 195 Sup. 31, 1922.

If the wife's action is dismissed, the fact that it was for her default instead of on the merits puts the order in no better position (Hayes, 150 A.D. 842, 135 Sup. 225, 1912, aff'd 208 N. Y. 600, rev'g 74 Misc. 533, 134 Sup. 

482, 1911). The restoration of the case reinstates the order. Arrears due when the judgment of dismissal is entered are thereafter recoverable; arrears accruing between the judgment and the restoration of the action are not (Fauls, 153 A.D. 367, 138 Sup. 459, 1912).

Dismissal after trial was held in one case (Shepard, 99 A.D. 308, 90 Sup. 982, 1904) not to preclude recovery of arrears, and, more particularly, that if the husband had already been adjudged in contempt for the arrears, the order of adjudication survived the dismissal. But the decision was held to be irreconcilable with the Thrall case, supra, in the Hayes case, supra. It may therefore be said that the adjudication fares no better than the temporary order itself.

Recovery of arrears was allowed following the dismissal of the husband's action, because of his appeal (Gelb, L. J., N. Y. Co., Jan. 17, 1930). (2) Reconciliation of the parties

Reconciliation between the parties is deemed an abandonment of the action. An application to vacate the

TEMPORARY ALIMONY 

61 temporary award is in order; but in one case it was held that the order automatically lost all effect and that a formal order was therefore unnecessary (Quinn, 133 Misc. 

266, 231 Sup. 329, 1928). This would make it advisable for the wife to discontinue the action where a reconciliation has taken place, if she desires to resume proceedings for the recovery of alimony (Rosenblatt, L. J., N. Y. Co., Dec. 18, 1930). (3) Death of the parties

The reason for the termination of the order, on the death of either party, is, of course, that the action has come to an end (White, 224 A.D. 355, 231 Sup. 146, 1928). Accrued arrears are beyond the wife's reach on the husband's death, or of her personal representatives on her death (In re Hudes' Estate, 128 Misc. 362, 219 Sup. 485, 1926). 

MODIFICATION ON MOTION 

(1) To terminate the order

There would not seem to be any impropriety in a formal motion to terminate the allowance on the happening of any one of the contingencies which effect an automatic termination (Millady vs Stein, 19 Misc. 652, 44 

Sup. 408, 1897). It is absolutely required when the husband has won an interlocutory judgment (Poss, 164 A.D. 

213, 149 Sup. 587, 1914; Lunenfeld, 137 Misc. 419, 242 

Sup. 279, 1930).

Temporary alimony may be suspended and discontinued because of the wife's misbehavior. (White, L. J. 

Kings Co. February 5, 1931).

The motion was granted, for the wife's adultery, where her misconduct predicated the husband's subsequent action for divorce. No automatic modification followed

even from the conviction for adultery of her co-respondent, in another action (Ronan, 32 Misc. 467, 66 Sup. 799, 1900). 

It is necessary to distinguish the cases in which the motion is granted as a matter of course, viz., where there has been a formal adjudication of the wife's adultery, and the cases in which there has not, and in which, therefore, the husband must present substantial proof by affidavits. 

Even in the first class of cases, however, the court is invested with discretion. Thus, the fact that the husband was destitute and unable to comply with the order was a determining factor with the court in vacating the allowance where the jury had convicted the wife of adultery (Lusk, 31 Misc. 312, 65 Sup. 401, 1900).

The situation is the same where no formal adjudication or other formal determination has been had of the wife's adultery, in cases where her misconduct is alleged to have taken place after the allowance was made, and those in which it took place before the allowance. If the husband urged her misconduct in opposition to the motion for alimony, or knew of it but withheld it, he may not thereafter raise it under the familiar and well-settled rules of motion practice. If he does not discover it, however, until after the allowance is made, he may move to modify the order without urging the misconduct as a defence to the action, and even though it was committed before the action was commenced (Glaser, 36 Misc. 231, 73 Sup. 284, 1901).

A foreign judgment of divorce in favor of the husband, if it is valid here, is ground for vacating the order (Weaver, 96 Misc. 476, 160 Sup. 642, 1916).

The court may suspend payments for the wife's bad faith, as where she caused a delay of the trial for a year by moving for a jury trial (Sigel, 19 Sup. 906, 1892).

TEMPORARY ALIMONY 

63 But the husband's motion was denied, where the wife had failed to move the case for trial, because he could have done it himself (Ulbricht, 89 Hun, 479, 35 Sup. 324, 1895).

The motion to vacate was granted following the reversal in the Appellate Division of the wife's separation judgment (Deisler, 65 A.D. 208, 72 Sup. 560, 1901).

The motion is in order when the wife commences another action, as for divorce following one for separation (Nichols, 5 N. Y. W’kly Dig. 398, 1877).

The authority of the court to modify the order, either by striking it out, as in the foregoing, or by reducing or increasing the amount, as taken up in the following section, is specifically conferred by Section 1169. It will not be exercised unless the party desiring the modification makes regular application for it (Benedix, 226 A.D. 

815, 234 Sup. 542, 1929).

A reversal of the order granting the motion to vacate the allowance reinstates the arrears (Wallace, 140 A.D. 

800, 125 Sup. 561, 1910). (2) Motion to reduce and increase

Reductions and increases may be had on a showing of change of circumstances (Strauss, 60 Hun, 580, 14 Sup. 

671, 1891). Facts must be presented which were not before the court on the original motion (Manson, L. J., Queens Co., Feb. 2, 1931). Otherwise Special Term would be reviewing its own decision (Gross, 222 A.D. 816, 226 Sup. 280, 1928). Thus, the motion will be in disfavor if it follows very soon after the allowance is made (Goldberg, 184 Sup. 923, 1920).

The wife's interference in the husband's employment, making it difficult for him to earn, was rejected as a

ground for a reduction (Kunze, 53 Sup. 938, 1898). But the application may be entertained for the wife's bad faith, as where the allowance was reduced for her delay in moving the case for trial (Bass, L. J., N. Y. Co., Dec. 

30, 1930). 

The modification may be conditioned. The increase was granted on condition that the wife proceed at once with trial (Barnes, L. J., Kings Co., Feb. 3, 1931); the reduction granted if the husband noticed the case and paid the calendar fee (Stone, id., N. Y. Co., Jan. 19, 1931).

The court's refusal to modify may also be conditioned, as where a reduction was denied on condition that the plaintiff wife consent to an early trial (Feinberg, L. J. 

Kings Co., Dec. 12, 1930).

The motion is not a reargument, and need not be heard by the same judge who granted the alimony. It is not improper, however, to refer it to him (Newell, 27 Misc. 

117, 57 Sup. 403, 1899). 

M. Preferences

Section 138 of the Civil Practice Act, subd. 18, pro vides for preferences in divorce actions when alimony has been allowed. The section is exclusive and does not apply to separation actions (Seligman, 52 Misc. 9, 100 

Sup. 770, 1906). But preferences may be had in any matrimonial action by reason of its special circumstances (Kruppenbacher, L. J., Queens Co., Nov. 13, 1930). 

N. Penalties for arrears 

Disqualifications of husband

Various penalties may be imposed upon the husband for his failure to pay temporary alimony, aside from con-

TEMPORARY ALIMONY 

65 tempt, sequestration and other remedies of enforcement. 

They may be divided into two groups, bars to affirmative action by the husband and affirmative steps by the wife. 

A husband in default is not entitled to any affirmative relief (Nielson, L. J., Kings Co., March 19, 1931). Thus, he was not permitted to appear at the inquest for the purpose of contesting alimony (Musolino, id., Kings Co., Dec. 12, 1930); refused the right of visitation (D'Angelo, id., Bronx Co., Nov. 26, 1930); denied a preference in a divorce action (Fennessy, 111 A.D. 181, 97 Sup. 602, 1906), and a dismissal of the wife's action for failure to prosecute (Fink, L. J., Kings Co., Jan. 27, 1931).

He may not enter judgment (Goldenthal, L. J., Kings 

Co., Nov. 26, 1930). It does not matter that the wife went through the trial of his divorce action without urging the objection that he was in arrears, because she had the right to seek vindication (Gray, 162 A.D. 586, 148 Sup. 24, 1914). Where he had already entered a decree, her motion to strike it out was granted (Bates, 145 

Sup. 411, 1913).

He is not disqualified, however, from taking steps to reinstate himself in the court's good grace. "... the court can refuse the benefit of proceedings to a party in contempt, when asked as a favor, and can prevent him from taking progressive steps against his adversary, but it has no power to stay him in his proceedings, by motion or appeal, where the object was to rid himself of the alleged contempt, or show that the order which he did not obey was erroneous." (Hovey vs Elliott, 167 U.S. 

409, 1897, p. 437, citing Brinkley, 47 N. Y. 40)

The arrears are no bar to his motion to strike out the allowance for the wife's adultery (Ronan, 32 Misc. 467, 66 Sup. 799, 1900); nor the motion to reduce it where he is unable to make the payments, for which see the discussion on enforcement by contempt (Chapter V).

Affirmative steps by the wife The husband can be stayed (Naveja, 110 Misc. 279, 179 Sup. 881, 1920; Schmuckler, L. J., Kings Co., March 

21, 1931); but not for arrears in another action (Tafel, 169 A.D. 417, 155 Sup. 164, 1915).

Her motion to stay her own action was denied (Friedman, 216 A.D. 348, 215 Sup. 47, 1926). It was granted, however, because of the imminence of trial and the gross inequity of permitting the husband to profit by his disobedience of the order by forcing the wife on when she would be without sufficient means to properly present her proofs (Harney, 110 A.D. 20, 96 Sup. 905, 1905; Moncrief, 155 Sup. 592, 1915).

The husband's answer may not be stricken out for his default, because it would be in violation of the "due process" clause of the Federal Constitution (Hovey vs 

Elliott, supra; Sibley, 76 A.D. 132, 78 Sup. 743, 1902). 

But see Brisbane, 34 Hun, 339, 1884, and Quigley, 45 Hun, 23, 1887; and Knott, 6 A.D. 589, 39 Sup. 804, 1896, which permitted the striking out of the husband's appearance. 

If he is in default for failing to answer, or for any other reason unable to or disqualified from answering, he may be permitted to come in and defend on payment of arrears (Sarans, L. J., N. Y. Co., Nov. 14, 1930). Payment of arrears may be made the condition of the denial of the wife's motion to dismiss his complaint for failure to prosecute (Weidman, id., Kings Co., Dec. 10, 1930).

But his complaint may not be stricken out for the arrears (Naveja, supra).

In order that the wife may avail herself of these remedies, whether by objection to affirmative steps on the husband's part, or by affirmative action on her part, it is neither necessary that the order awarding the alimony

TEMPORARY ALIMONY 

67 be personally served, if he knows about it and is evading service (Knott, supra), nor that he be actually adjudged in contempt (Gray, 162 A.D. 586, 148 Sup. 24, 1914).

The motion papers for a stay may be served outside the state if the order awarding the alimony was served here (Harney, supra).

O. Status, significance and effect of the order 

Temporary alimony is not an exclusive remedy, in so far as the wife's right to support is concerned (Mayper vs Harlan, 125 Misc. 123, 210 Sup. 257, 1925, rev'd as to other points 215 A.D. 767). She has the choice of resort to either this special remedy or to her husband's common law obligation, and her failure to avail herself of the one does not prejudice the other. At most, the wife's refusal to ask for temporary alimony may commend itself to the court's favorable consideration (Haddock, 75 A.D. 565, 78 Sup. 304, 1902).

The fact that the allowance is refused does not mean that it will be refused in the judgment, nor does the temporary award necessitate a permanent one. 

The allowance does not determine the rights of the parties (Peo. ex rel Commissioners of Charities vs Cul len 153 N. Y. 629, 1897). The denial of the motion leaves the parties in statu quo (Elder vs Rosenwasser, 238 N. Y. 427, 1924). The wife is free to apply to the

Family Court for an allowance (Peo. ex rel Heinle vs 

Heinle, 115 Misc. 469, 188 Sup. 399, 1921).

The allowance measures the husband's liability for the wife's support (Elder vs Rosenwasser, supra). The subject, as well as other immediately allied matters, is taken up in greater detail in the following chapter, in the discussion on special allowances.

COUNSEL FEES, EXPENSES AND SPECIAL ALLOWANCES III A. Explanatory note-classification of cases—authority 

The allowances under consideration in this chapter are somewhat varied, but they have this in common-they are all designed to compensate third parties for services rendered for or necessitated by the wife, and, like alimony, are supported by the summary order of the court. 

They are of two kinds. The first is occasioned by proceedings or developments in the action and consists of counsel fees and miscellaneous fees and disbursements, such as minutes, printing, etc. The second is occasioned by matters outside the action and is referred to here as special allowances. They are for emergency needs, principally medical and legal service.

The authority for all these allowances is Section 1169 of the Civil Practice Act. Counsel fees and disbursements are provided for by that part of it which specifies the payment of sums to the wife to enable her to carry on or defend the action; special allowances by the provision for the support of the wife. The word "expenses" in the caption of the statute is taken to signify every item of expenditure necessitated by the wife for her prosecution or defence of the suit and which, in turn, justifies re-imbursement by the husband (Nottingham, 209 A.D. 459, 204 Sup. 750, 1924). 68

There is no difference in principle between a general allowance for suit fees and a specific one to meet a particular expense (Schloemer, 49 N. Y. 82, 1872). 

B. Counsel fees-in general 

Similar to alimony

Counsel fees, like alimony, is a provisional remedy (Kellogg vs Stoddard, 89 A.D. 137, 84 Sup. 1015, 1903), and the right to it is statutory or incidental to the statute (Lake, 194 N. Y. 179, 1909).

It is strictly pendente lite and for future services only (Winton, 31 Hun, 290, 1883; Stillman, 199 A.D. 735, 192 Sup. 360, 1922). Cases contra (Graves, 2 Paige Ch. 

62, 1830; Green, 40 How. Pr. 465, 1870) are not to be followed. 

Pendente lite

Counsel fees may be had only on motion (Williams, 53 Hun, 636, 6 Sup. 645, aff'd 130 N. Y. 193, 1891). It may not be had after trial (Pountney, 56 Hun, 647, 10

Sup. 192, 1890) even though the motion papers were served and the original return day was before the trial (Winkemeier, 11 A.D. 199, 42 Sup. 586, 1896); or in the judgment (Atherton, 82 Hun, 179, 31 Sup. 977, 1894) because that would make it for past services (Stevens, 125 

Misc. 451, 211 Sup. 192, 1925, mod'd 214 A.D. 785, 211

Sup. 913). Thus, it may not be had after settlement (Cipro, 161 Sup. 408, 1916), or reconciliation (Conklin, 201 A.D. 170, 194 Sup. 685, 1922). But see Chase, 65 

How. Pr. 308, 1883, and Le Compte, 3 Bradb. 588, 1913. 

See also Straus, 67 Hun, 491, 22 Sup. 567, 1893; 

Poillon, 75 A.D. 536, 78 Sup. 323, 1902; Bishop, 165 A.D. 

954, 150 Sup. 660, 1914.

For future services only

The allowance is proper only for future services (Greenwald, L. J., Bronx Co., Feb. 27, 1931). It does not matter whether the services, if already rendered, have been paid for or not (Nottingham, 209 A.D. 462, 204 Sup. 

753, 1924; Mabson, 130 Misc. 248, 223 Sup. 789, 1927, rev'd on other points 221 A.D. 844). Exceptions

There are exceptions to these rules. The limitation of counsel fees to the pendency of the action and future services may be removed by the agreement of the parties. "Such an agreement is in effect an agreement by the defendant to pay the sum necessary to enable the plaintiff to maintain her action, the amount to be determined by the court when the final judgment in the action is entered. 

The husband having here made that agreement and those acting for the wife having acted upon it, it is too late, after the court has fixed the amount and decreed its payment, to object to any award upon the ground that the motion should have been made when the husband made the agreement, where the counsel for the wife had acted upon the stipulation and postponed the application for a counsel fee until after the services had been rendered." (Page, 124 A.D. 421, 108 Sup. 864, 1908, aff'd 

195 N. Y. 540, pp. 867, 868) 

Counsel fees will be allowed after trial if there are services still to be rendered and the circumstances warrant it (McBride, 53 Hun, 448, 6 Sup. 447, 1889; Cipro, 161 Sup. 408, 1916). It will be granted in the judgment if the temporary order so provided (Mercer, 73 Hun, 192, 25 Sup. 867, 1893) or may be deferred for consideration at the trial, as a condition to its denial on the temporary application (Cook, L. J., Bronx Co., April 28, 1931); and the husband may not object if he failed to appeal from or move to re-settle the order (Browning, 247 N. Y.

579, 1828). See also Beauley, 199 A.D. 279, 191 Sup. 397, 1921. 

The allowance was made on the discontinuance of the action where the fact that the parties had reconciled and intended to discontinue was communicated to the court when their agreement that counsel fees be allowed was entered into (Louden, 65 How. Pr. 411, 1883).

The reconciliation of the parties was held not to be a discontinuance of the action so as to bar an allowance of counsel fees, where the husband had agreed to pay the wife's attorney if she would return to him, but had failed to do so and had interposed an answer instead, and it did not matter that by reason of the reconciliation the wife would have no chance of succeeding in the action. His promise and her reliance thereon required that he be compelled to perform it (Smith, 35 Hun, 378, 1885, aff'd 

99 N. Y. 639).

Counsel fees will be allowed for past services when it appears that the wife will be unable to go on unless her attorney is paid for them (Stampfer, 11 Sup. 588, 1890; 

Miller, 158 A.D. 766, 144 Sup. 278, 1913; Tranter, 189 

A.D. 714, 178 Sup. 521, 1919; Beauley, supra). Her attorney's refusal to proceed unless paid is not such a showing (Emerson, 26 Sup. 292, 1893, aff'd 143 N. Y. 

633). The mere fact that final judgment remains to be entered is insufficient to justify an allowance for that purpose (Schroter, 57 Misc. 199, 107 Sup. 1065, 1907).

The fact that the wife's means were substantially reduced by having to pay her attorney does not warrant a larger allowance for his services than he would ordinarily be entitled to, because that would in effect be a compensation for past services (Conklin, 188 Sup. 141, 1921).

In deciding an application for past services, the court will inquire into this question-does the work already

done represent the most important part of the attorney's function? If it does the motion will be denied, as where it consisted in defending an issue which was already passed upon (Beadleston, 103 N. Y. 402, 1886).

The temporary order may expressly provide for payment of past services, by reason of the special circumstances. The services must be specified; but if unexpected developments arise, such as new issues by reason of the husband's interposition of a counter-claim, the order will not preclude a further application (Guttas, 131 

Misc. 278, 227 Sup. 456, 1928, aff'd 224 A.D. 810, judgment rev'd 226 A.D. 847).

Counsel fees for past services and after trial were allowed in an annulment action brought by the husband and decided in the wife's favor (Griffin, 47 N. Y. 134, 1872). 

Allowance for children

The court may allow counsel fees for the protection of the interests of the child of the parties. This occurs in cases where the legitimacy of the child is at issue and an attorney is appointed to represent it. 

C. Determination of the motion for counsel fees-consideration before the court

The underlying principles are in the main the same as in alimony. For that reason, and also for the several citations on counsel fees which are given there, reference should be had to the discussion on the determination of the motion for alimony (IIE, ante).

Requirements The wife must show that the services of counsel are necessary for her protection and that she is without

1 means to pay for them (Lake, 194 N. Y. 179, 1909; Myron, 167 A. D. 895, 151 Sup. 671, 1915; Stillman, 199 A.D. 735, 192 Sup. 360, 1922). 

Merits of wife's action

The fact that the husband's defence seems very strong does not bar an allowance (Brennan, 19 W'kly Dig. 342, 1884). It is different where it is clear that she has no case (Uhlman, 51 Super. Ct. (J & S) 361, 1885), or that her complaint is defective (Wood, 8 Wend. 357, aff'g 2 Paige 

454, 1831). 

Husband's poverty

The husband's poverty is no defence to an application for counsel fees, particularly where he sues. He must pay for the wife's defence or abandon the action (Cohen, 11 Misc. 704, 32 Sup. 1082, 1895). The suggestion that was made in this case that the allowance was justified by the probabilities of success in the wife's favor is not supported by the authorities. That consideration is pertinent only to alimony (IIE, ante). 

Husband's divorce action 

The husband must pay counsel fees if the wife denies the adultery under oath, or urges an affirmative defence, unless her guilt appears beyond a reasonable doubt (Osgood, 2 Paige Ch. 621, 1831; Dean, 48 Misc. 149, 96 Sup. 

472, 1905; Masey, 57 A.D. 315, 68 Sup. 994, 1901).

Separation agreements 

They do not bar counsel fees. The agreement generally contemplates ordinary support and maintenance, not unusual and unforeseen necessities (Miller, 43 How. Pr. 125, 1872; Collins, 80 N. Y. 1, 1880; Grube, 65 A. D. 239, 72

Sup. 529, 1901; Gutmann, 178 Sup. 833, 1919; Blueglass, 127 Misc. 157, 215 Sup. 325, 1926; Smith, 127 Misc. 764, 217 Sup. 3, 1926; Schifferdecker, 219 A.D. 740, 220 Sup. 

229, 1927). But see Murnane, 256 N. Y. 7, aff'g. 230 

A.D. 820, 244 Sup. 881, 1930 and Schreiber, L. J., N. Y. 

Co., Feb. 27, 1931.

Family Court allowance 

It does not bar counsel fees (Klimkowski, L. J., Kings 

Co., Feb. 25, 1931). 

Annulment actions

The plaintiff wife may not have counsel fees (Jones vs Brinsmade, 183 N. Y. 258, 1905, rev'g 104 A.D. 619; 

Bartlett, 1 Clarke Ch. 322, 1841; Herron, 28 Misc. 323, 59 Sup. 861, 1899; Sweet, 132 Misc. 386, 230 Sup. 317, 1928; Wachtel, L. J., Bronx Co., Jan. 22, 1931; Bonilla, id., N. Y. Co., April 8, 1931). But see Pandolfi, id., Kings 

Co., Jan. 27, 1931. 

Merits of husband's action

It is no reason for denying the wife counsel fees that the husband cannot succeed (and that the wife will require little assistance, if any, to defeat the action), as where his complaint in an action for separation was defective for non-compliance with Rule 280 (Bond, L. J., Queens Co., Dec. 27, 1930). 

Discontinuance by husband

Counsel fees may be denied where the husband states that he intends to discontinue the action, with leave to renew in the event that he does not (Freedman, L. J., N. Y. Co., Feb. 7, 1931).

Exceptions

The allowance is sometimes made even though the wife fails to show the facts which are ordinarily prerequisite. 

This occurs principally in two classes of cases, viz., where the husband counterclaims (De Meli, 5 Civ. Pro. Rep. 

306, 67 How. Pr. 20, 1884; Fishbaugh, 161 Sup. 446, 1916), and where the husband's allegations seriously impugn the wife's character or the validity of the marital relation (Mann, 75 N. Y. 614, 1878, cited in Collins, 80 

N. Y. 1, 1880; Emanuel, 190 A.D. 917, 179 Sup. 919, 1919; 

Frohlich, 199 A.D. 936, 191 Sup. 926, 1921). 

D. Particular services warranting counsel fees

The allowance may be had not only to enable the wife to carry on or defend but for particular proceedings and developments. They are either motions or appeals.

The distinguishing feature of these allowances is that they are decided on their own merits, without reference to the granting or denial of the application for counsel fees, if one was made.

An allowance was granted to enable the wife to resist the husband's motion for a stay (Goldfarb, L. J., Kings 

Co., Nov. 15, 1930) and his motion to set aside the referee's report dismissing his action (Donnelly, 63 How. Pr. 

481, 1882); and on her proceedings to punish the husband for contempt where he had been persistently delinquent and it appeared that her rights would be jeopardized if no provision was made to defray her legal expenses (Potters, 133 Misc. 26, 231 Sup. 33, 1928).

The allowance is improper after judgment (Moussette, 229 A.D. 744, 241 Sup. 434, 1930; Wright, 139 

Misc. 423, 248 Sup. 317, 1931; Olney, L. J., Kings Co., April 22, 1931). It is improper after a judgment of di-

vorce because the husband's duty to provide for his wife has been terminated by the severance of the marital relation (Bishop, 165 A.D. 954, 150 Sup. 660, 1914; 210 

A.D. 3, 205 Sup. 542, 1924; Parker, 189 A.D. 603, 179 

Sup. 51, 1919). "The final judgment in this case wholly separates the parties and dissolves the relation of husband and wife, and it has been entered and no appeal has been taken therefrom. The action is not pending. We may assume, perhaps, that if a motion is made in an action to set aside a judgment entered therein by reason of some irregularity or fraud affecting the jurisdiction of the court over the parties or the subject matter that the incidental power of the court over matrimonial actions and parties might, when a necessity therefor is shown, justify an order for counsel fees. In such a case a wife could consistently insist that the marriage relation had not been dissolved. 

Where a judgment is absolutely void for want of jurisdiction it is a form merely and does not affect the substantial relation between the parties. The facts disclosed on this motion do not admit of a claim on the part of the plaintiff that she is now the wife of the defendant. The court had jurisdiction of the parties and of the subject matter and the judgment entered is not void but binding upon the parties and upon the court, so long as it remains undisturbed. The application of the plaintiff to have the judgment set aside can only be granted if at all by reason of a fraud wholly apart from anything relating to the inducement which led the plaintiff to bring the action. 

It is not a case that comes within the language of the statutory provision relating to counsel fees and the relation of husband and wife being wholly severed the court has no inherent jurisdiction to award counsel fees as an incident to its general statutory jurisdiction in matrimonial actions." (Lake, 194 N. Y. 179, pp. 185, 186)

Thus, the court was divested of all prerogative conferred by the statute because the action was terminated, and of inherent authority because the parties were no longer married.

It is an open question, however, whether counsel fees may be had after a judgment of separation (Horn vs 

Schmalholz, 150 A. D. 333, 134 Sup. 652, 1912). It was granted to oppose the husband's motion to modify the decree (Weingarten, L. J., Bronx Co., June 14, 1923) and his appeal from the order denying his motion to open a default judgment (McIlvane, id., Kings Co., Dec. 10, 1930). But see Prehn, id., N. Y. Co., Dec. 11, 1930.

Allowances on appeals vary. If they are from orders, they will be entertained independently of the merits of the action, for they have to do with particular proceedings or developments, not the general carrying on or defending the suit. The wife must show, in order to obtain counsel fees on an appeal from an interlocutory order, that the relief which was granted or refused, to her detriment, is substantial, as that the court had refused to confirm the referee's report in her favor (Nottingham, 209 A. D. 462, 204 Sup. 753, 1924). It must appear, of course, that the order is reviewable. Where it refused the wife an adjournment and dismissed the case, an allowance was denied (Hayes, 141 A. D. 35, 125 Sup. 652, 1910).

An allowance on an appeal from the judgment comes squarely within the provision for expenses to carry on or defend, as specified by Section 1169, for the action is deemed pending. Counsel fees may be had by the wife whether the judgment is for divorce or separation, (annulment if she is defendant), and whether she is appellant or respondent. The suggestion that the allowance is disapproved when sought by the wife to resist the husband's appeal on the ground that it would tend to hamper or restrict his remedy (Winton, 31 Hun, 290, 1883) is not to be followed.

The wife must show substantially the same facts to obtain the allowance as in the action proper (Gould, 201 1552 2 12

A. D. 127, 194 Sup. 122, 1922; McCracken, 213 A. D. 768, 210 Sup. 881, 1925).

It must appear that the appeal is actually pending. 

Where the husband succeeded in modifying the judgment dismissing his divorce action by having the costs imposed against him stricken from it, the service of a notice of appeal by him, made theretofore, was held to be ineffectual to start an appeal running. A new judgment had taken the place of the old one, a new notice of appeal was therefore necessary, and counsel fees was denied (Thurston, 136 Sup. 340, 1911).

When the wife is appellant, she must show both errors of law and fact (Gansz, 59 Sup. 955, 1899).

The allowance may be had on all successive appeals, as from the Appellate Division to the Court of Appeals (Page, 139 Misc. 102, 248 Sup. 493, 1931).

When the husband appeals, his stay of judgment does not preclude an allowance for counsel fees, just as it does not preclude alimony (Haddock, 109 A. D. 502, 96 Sup. 

522, 1905). For an instance of an action by the wife's attorney on the husband's appeal bond, see Gordon vs

United States Fidelity and Guarantee, 76 Misc. 203, 134 

Sup. 891, 1912.

The Appellate Division has the same power to make the award as Special Term (Haddock, 75 A. D. 565, 78 

Sup. 304, 1902). 

See Halsted, 11 Misc. 592, 32 Sup. 1080, 1895; Di Lorenzo, 78 A.D. 577, 79 Sup. 566, 1903. 

Additional allowances-in general

In every one of the foregoing instances, an additional allowance of counsel fees may be had if one was already made, if it is otherwise proper. The wife must show that the first allowance is not enough to cover the additional

services and that the husband has sufficient means to pay for them (Kittle, 8 Daly, 72, 1878). This is beside the usual requirements that the services are necessary to protect her rights, that they are future, not already rendered and that she lacks sufficient funds of her own (Stampfer, 58 Super. Ct. 587, 11 Sup. 588, 1890; McCarthy, 137 N. Y. 

500, 1893; Loffredo, 233 Sup. 814, 1929).

Additional counsel fees were deemed proper in the following cases, the proceedings being identified with the determination of the action, as distinguished from the motions and appeals which are unrelated to it: where new issues arose by reason of the husband's amending the pleadings (Beauley, 199 A.D. 279, 191 Sup. 397, 1921); where he moved for a new reference of the issue in a divorce action brought by the wife, after the court refused to confirm the first report made in his favor (Bauer, 42 

Misc. 557, 87 Sup. 607, 1904); where the jury disagreed on the husband's issue of the validity of the marriage and a retrial was necessitated (McCarthy, supra); where the jury disagreed and there had been a formal understanding between respective counsel that the sum paid as counsel fees was to discharge the husband's liability until the result of the action was reached (Van Wormer, 57 Hun, 496, 11 Sup. 247, 1890). 

E. Motion practice-the order

This contemplates an application for counsel fees made independently of one for alimony. If alimony has been requested, the moving papers should refer to the other application for the particulars of the action, etc. (IIC, ante). If it has not been requested, and no proceedings have as yet been had in which the wife has set forth the facts of the case, it would be advisable to state them

briefly on the motion for counsel fees. In any event, the wife's affidavit should state sufficient facts upon which an amount can be fixed (Gernsback, L. J., N. Y. Co., Nov. 

22, 1930). An affidavit by her attorney is unnecessary.

The court is familiar with the amount of work which will be necessitated and its value, without any statement from him (De Llamosas, 2 Hun, 380, appeal dis'd 62 

N. Y. 618, 1875).

The form of the notice of motion is to be found in the 

The form of order for the notice of motion for alimony (IIC, ante). the order is embodied in the form of the payment of alimony (IID, ante).

The order must make the money payable to the wife, and it is customary to require payment at the office of her attorney (Quinn, 133 Misc. 266, 231 Sup. 329, 1928; 

Kellogg vs Stoddard, 89 A. D. 137, 84 Sup. 1015, 1903; 

Kamman, 167 A. D. 423, 152 Sup. 579, 1915; 167 A. D. 

426, 152 Sup. 581). 

Counsel fees are usually made payable in two instalments, one-half following service of the order and the balance on or before the day of trial. As a result, when the husband has failed to pay the balance when the case is called, the attorney must request an adjournment if he desires to secure his remedy, as it may be defeated if he goes on and suffers an adverse decision. For this reason, it is advisable that the order specify that the balance is to be paid when the cause first appears on the trial calendar (Bodka, L. J., Bronx Co., Jan. 16, 1931).

This gives him a sufficient opportunity to take necessary steps for the collection of the balance. A memorandum would be appropriate for this purpose when the order is submitted.

Where monies have already been paid to counsel and the fact has been communicated to the court and the

allowance is based in part upon it, it is preferable that the order provide for a sum to be paid as would have been allowed in the first instance, and the husband to be given credit for the amount paid (Heyman, L. J., Bronx Co., Jan. 19, 1931). This will be fair to the husband, on the one hand, by indicating the full measure of his obligation for counsel fees; and to the attorney, on the other hand, in the event that he brings civil action to recover for his services. 

Except as indicated in the foregoing, the proceedings on a motion for counsel fees are identical with one for alimony. 

F. Miscellaneous features of counsel fees

Nature and effect of the remedy The allowance of counsel fees measures the husband's liability for the services of the wife's attorney in the action (Klein vs Dula, 217 A.D. 473, 217 Sup. 221, 1926).

Like alimony, the remedy is not exclusive. The wife's failure to demand counsel fees leaves her attorney free to sue civilly for necessaries (Naumer vs Gray, 28 A.D. 529, 51 Sup. 222, 1898; Elder vs Cochrane, 209 A.D. 665, 205 

Sup. 292, 1924; Mayper vs Harlan, 125 Misc. 123, 210 

Sup. 257, 1925, rev'd on other points 215 A.D. 767, 242 

N. Y. 537).

The allowance covers all work which counsel may perform, including motions to punish for contempt. If the services are deemed to be unusual and not reasonably contemplated by the fee allowed, the only recourse of the attorney is by way of an application for a further allowance (Turner vs Woolworth, 153 A.D. 293, 137 Sup. 1071, 1912).

1 11 444 直接​製​豆 ​16 712 171 1 1 41 Amount

The underlying principles are the same as in alimony (IE, IIE, ante). The means of both parties are proper considerations (Waterman, 147 A.D. 464, 131 Sup. 741, 1911; Le Roy, 175 A.D. 120, 161 Sup. 503, 1916; Conklin, 186 Sup. 191, mod'd 196 A.D. 607, 188 Sup. 141, 1921; 

Fisher, 223 A.D. 19, 227 Sup. 345, 1928, aff'd 250 N. Y. 

313). The husband's income tax returns may be examined (Mabson, 130 Misc. 248, 223 Sup. 789, 1927, rev'd on other points 221 A.D. 844, 224 Sup. 844).

The fact that the merits of the wife's defence do not impress the court may militate against a large award (Gutmann, 178 Sup. 833, 1919); as will the fact that the husband does not defend (Kennedy, L. J., N. Y. Co., Dec. 

19, 1930); or that an allowance was had in a prior action (Davis, 150 Sup. 636, 1914).

The wife is entitled to an allowance for only one attorney (Uhlman, 51 Super. Ct. (J & S) 361, 1885). 

See Goldfarb, L. J., Kings Co., Nov. 15, 1930. Restitution

Counsel fees paid must be returned in the event that the order or judgment awarding it is set aside, in distinction from alimony (Laurie, 9 Paige Ch. 234, 1841; Pincus, 211 A.D. 128, 206 Sup. 599, 1924); but not by counsel because it is not payable to him (Grauer, 2 Misc. 98, 20 

Sup. 854, 1892), also holding that in determining whether restitution should be ordered a consideration of the circumstances of the reversal of the order granting the allowance is proper. Restitution was not ordered, however, where the allowance was reduced (Uhlman, supra).

Arrears The husband may be refused a discontinuance unless he pays the counsel fee (Blevins, 131 Misc. 315, 226 Sup. 

553, 1928); but not where the wife consents to the discontinuance (Miller, L. J., Queens Co., March 25, 1931).

The attorney may not have a lien against alimony for his counsel fee, even if the alimony unpaid has been reduced to a fine, under well-settled rules preserving the fund to the wife; but a lien may be had to secure monies lent to her (Turner vs Woolworth, 153 A.D. 293, 137 Sup. 

1071, 1912). See also In re Brackett, 114 A.D. 257, 99 Sup. 

802, 1906, aff'd 189 N. Y. 502; Conklin, 201 A.D. 170, 194 

Sup. 685, 1922, aff'd 234 N. Y. 546; Paul, L. J., Kings Co., March 11, 1931. 

Counsel fees are irrecoverable after the wife's death (Millady vs Stein, 19 Misc. 652, 44 Sup. 408, 1897), or after the husband's death (Kellogg vs Stoddard, 89 A.D. 

137, 84 Sup. 1015, 1903); or following a reconciliation of the parties, which is as effectual to defeat the attorney's remedy as a formal order of discontinuance (Naumer vs 

Gray, 41 A.D. 361, 58 Sup. 476, 1899). The order, arrears and all incidents of counsel fees abate with the termination of the action, exactly as in the case of temporary alimony (IIK, ante).

The attorney's civil action does not lie to enforce the order, for two reasons. Remedies of enforcement, as in the case of alimony, lie only in the action itself, and the action may not be brought by the attorney because the fee is not payable to him (Cohn vs Howe, 156 Sup. 448, 1915).

Modification The allowance of counsel fees may be modified by being increased or reduced at the instance of the respective

parties, on a showing that the husband's circumstances have changed. This situation would come about only where the fee was payable in instalments and there was a balance still due. Although the motion is not a reargument, it is proper to refer it to the judge who made the allowance (Leal, L. J., Kings Co., Jan. 2, 1931). 

Other compensation to counsel

The wife should inform the court in her moving affidavit if she has paid any fee to her attorney, and he should do it on her failure to do so. It should be noted, in this connection, that the wife's refusal to pay him his fee does not justify her delay in submitting a final judgment of divorce, and that her motion for a decree will be accordingly denied (Kellogg, 166 Sup. 417, 1917). Appeals

The order granting or refusing counsel fees is reviewable (Morrissey, Appellate Division, 2nd Department, January, 1931). The Appellate Division has the same power to make the award as Special Term (Parker, 140 A.D. 899, 125 Sup. 161, 1910); and on review may modify the award (Miller, 158 A.D. 766, 144 Sup. 278, 1913). The wife's attorney was permitted to appeal from an order setting aside his counsel fee, on the ground that the remedy was a substantial right to him (Louden, 65 How. Pr. 411, 1883), in connection with which see Chase, 65 How. Pr. 

308, 1883. Bankruptcy 

Counsel fees, like alimony, is neither provable nor dischargeable in bankruptcy (In re Emil J. Smith, 30 Civ. 

Pro. Rep. 95, 1899, 3 Am. B'k'ptcy Rep. 67).

G. Expenses other than counsel fees-suit money

Counsel fees, as was stated in the introductory paragraphs of this chapter, is only one of several disbursements or expenses which are entailed by the wife in maintaining her action or defence and which must be defrayed by the husband. They are available by application on notice, and may be had only pendente lite (McQuien, 61 

How. Pr. 280, 1881; Percival, 14 N. Y. St. Rep. 255, 1888).

The order granting or denying them is reviewable (Morrissey, supra). 

Subpoena fees

They will be allowed only if the evidence sought will materially aid the wife's case or defence (Conklin, 196 

A.D. 607, 188 Sup. 141, 1921, mod'g 186 Sup. 191). 

Referee's fees 

They are allowable (Schloemer, 49 N. Y. 82, 1872; 

Heatherton, 1 N. Y. W'kly Dig. 27, 1875; McQuien, supra; Halsted, 11 Misc. 592, 32 Sup. 1080, 1895; Fisher, 223 A.D. 19, 227 Sup. 345, 1928, aff'd 250 N. Y. 313), regardless whom the findings may favor (Early, 8 Civ. 

Pro. Rep. 68, 2 How. Pr. (N. S.) 239, 1885). The motion was denied in Kaufman, 122 Misc. 219, 203 Sup. 510, 1924. 

Minutes, printing expenses and appeals in general

The court has always had the power under Section 1169 of the Civil Practice Act to order the husband to pay the wife's expenses of printing on appeal. Dicta to the contrary (Fagan, 39 Hun, 531, 1886) are not to be followed. 

The wife was allowed the minutes in Gansz, 59 Sup. 

955, 1899, on her motion for counsel fees on appeal, for the purpose of showing that prejudicial errors were made;

but denied in Herrmann, 88 A.D. 76, 84 Sup. 736, 1903, because they were shown to be merely useful, not necessary. See also Miller, 158 A.D. 766, 144 Sup. 278, 1913; 

Hock, 149 Sup. 1027, 1914; Van Valkenburgh vs Bishop, 164 Sup. 86, 1917.

The wife must satisfy the court that she has a reasonable probability of succeeding, if her application is made for suit money on an appeal. In applying for printing expenses, it is customary to annex the printer's estimate to the moving papers. An allowance was granted in Halsted, 11 Misc. 592, 32 Sup. 1080, 1895, and Solomon, L. J., Kings Co., Feb. 24, 1931. In the case of cross-appeals, where the husband represents that he will perfect the record, the court may grant expenses of printing the briefs and deny them as to the case on appeal, without prejudice to a renewal in the event that the husband fails to file it within a certain time (Martindale, id., N. Y. Co., Feb. 16, 1931).

The court may make a general allowance to cover disbursements on appeal (Solisch, L. J., N. Y. Co., Feb. 28, 1931), or in addition to counsel fees (McCracken, 213 

A.D. 768, 210 Sup. 881, 1925; Baruso, L. J., Bronx Co., March 4, 1931; Berkowitz, id., Kings Co., April 25, 1931).

An allowance for disbursements on appeal will not be vacated when the order or judgment which is appealed is upheld or set aside in the husband's favor (Glasser, id., April 25, 1931). 

Travelling expenses 

They were allowed in Miller, 158 A.D. 766, 144 Sup. 

278, 1913. 

Detective's fees 

There is no authority for their allowance (Conklin, 196 

A.D. 607, 188 Sup. 141, 1921, mod'g 186 Sup. 191).

Blanket awards

The court may allow a sum without specifying any fee or disbursement other than the expenses in general of maintaining the wife's action or defence (Shafton, L. J., N. Y. Co., Nov. 28, 1930).

H. Expenses outside the action-special allowances 

The allowances taken up here are somewhat extraordinary. Whenever the health or liberty of the wife is imperilled, the husband may be called upon to provide her with means of securing or protecting herself, by ordinary application on notice made by her and in the same summary method generally that is pursued for alimony. 

The remedy is equally available for children if they are in the wife's custody. The mere pendency of the action, no matter who sues or for what relief, affords her a remedy for the enforcement of the husband's duty that is far more drastic and effective than the usual civil channels. 

The application is judged by itself. It is possible that alimony and counsel fees may have been denied on the ground that the wife had failed to show even a vestige of merits, that the action, if brought by her, was prompted by the basest motives, or, if brought by the husband, by the most praiseworthy ones: the special allowance may be granted. Nor is it necessary that the services required are prospective. Even if they have already been rendered, if it appears that the wife will receive no further assistance unless they are paid for and that her situation or condition demands it, an award will be made. 

The only specification in the statute which authorizes such an allowance is the provision for the support of the wife and children in Section 1169 of the Civil Practice Act, which would seem to be included in the word "alimony"

1 403 in the caption. There is nothing in the reported cases to justify the allowance, other than the circumstance that the remedy is uniformly granted.

The only similarity of the allowance to counsel fees or any other expense or disbursement, aside from the incidental procedure, is that it is conditioned upon the wife's inability to pay for the services herself. The moving papers must therefore disclose the financial circumstances of the wife in the greatest particular. 

The application may be made by way of modification of the order granting or denying alimony or counsel fees (Elder vs Rosenwasser, 238 N. Y. 427, 1924).

Special allowances are generally either for medical or legal services. That does not mean, of course, that only physicians and lawyers may benefit by them. They may be, for example, for hospital treatment and nurses, or for bail premiums respectively. 

Medical services

The allowance may be general, as to defray doctors' and hospital bills (Lechner, Appellate Division, 2nd Depart ment, January, 1931) or specific, as to pay the expenses of the wife's confinement (Rosen, L. J., Kings Co., Jan. 23, 1931; Mathat, id., April 21, 1931; Balabanich, id., Queens 

Co., April 8, 1931), or to pay the expenses for the medical care of the wife necessitated by her pregnancy (Liss, id., Kings Co., Feb. 19, 1931). When the confinement is impending, the court may allow the sum in anticipation of it (Stoelzel, id., Kings Co., Jan. 31, 1931) or may defer the application until it is imminent (Hoffman, id., N. Y. Co., Feb. 11, 1931).

An allowance for medical services may be made simultaneously with one for counsel fees and expenses (Briggs, id., Kings Co., April 6, 1931) or on the order for alimony and

counsel fees (De Candio, id., Nassau Co., March 23, 1931). 

The allowance may be made payable in weekly instalments, as one for the children's medical expense (Schwartz, id., Bronx Co., April 9, 1931).

The husband may be permitted to offer proof that the bill is excessive (Gorsuch, id., Nassau Co., March 23, 1931).

The order is reviewable. The Appellate Division may make the award if Special Term refuses it (Lechner, supra). 

Legal services

Before a special allowance may be had for services rendered to the wife in a matter outside the action, it must appear that the husband is responsible for them as necessaries. That is, no recovery may be had in this summary method unless an action could lie civilly, by the third party directly against the husband. This requires amplification as far as legal services are concerned. 

Special allowances are in the nature of an exception to the rule that the payment of alimony discharges the husband's liability for the wife's debts. "As a provision for the wife, alimony judicially decreed is within this principle; if regularly paid, it protects the husband against her debts, and the further effect of the decree is to render it conclusively sufficient in amount. 

And this doctrine extends even to the mere order of the court for temporary alimony during the pendency of a divorce suit, it matters not to the husband's protection whether in fact the amount was adequate to the wife's needs or not. If it is not paid, the order will furnish him no protection. And where, in consequence of his neglect to pay, he has been compelled to discharge debts contracted by her, his only remedy is to apply for a reduction of the alimony." (Bishop, Marriage and Divorce, vol. II, p. 340)

14 7 Nevertheless, the allowance of alimony does not discharge the husband's duty to provide for his wife's sudden, unforeseen and urgent needs.

Nor is the special allowance barred by the principle that the husband's responsibility for legal services is discharged by the award of counsel fees, since counsel fees, if paid, measures the extent of the duty (Damman vs 

Bancroft, 43 Misc. 678, 88 Sup. 386, 1904) and terminates the common law obligation (Turner vs Woolworth, 153 

A.D. 293, 137 Sup. 1071, 1912). The principle applies only to services rendered in the action. In the same way that alimony does not cover unusual necessities, counsel fees does not cover outside services; unless the services were before the court when the award was made (Klein vs Dula, 217 A.D. 473, 217 Sup. 221, 1926). 

The latter point was the issue in Elder vs Rosenwasser, in which case alimony had been refused pending the matrimonial action. A civil action was brought thereafter against the husband by an attorney for services rendered to the wife in connection with matters outside the action between the spouses. The Appellate Term reversed the judgment for the plaintiff (121 Misc. 181, 200 Sup. 620, 1923). "While it is the husband's duty to furnish his wife with necessaries, and where he wrongfully fails to do it, the third party furnishing them may recover, yet where the parties have submitted their differences to the court, and under the statute, the court has made, or has refused to make, a provision for maintenance, the adjudication determines the liability of the husband, whatever it may be. 

Here the court said that it was not the duty of the husband to take care of his wife whatever the reason was, the order was the measure of the husband's liability." (pp. 629, 630, italics ours)

The decision was sustained by the Appellate Division (208 A.D. 745, 202 Sup. 924), but reversed by the Court of Appeals (238 N. Y. 427, 1924). "It (the denial of temporary alimony) was a temporary order made in the discretion of the court pending litigation. It did not finally determine the rights of the parties. 

It was not a final decree, and, therefore, could not have that effect. The wife was not obliged to resort to the court for support pending the action; to do so was discretionary with her. If she did not apply, the husband's common-law liability to provide her with the necessaries of life would still continue. It would not terminate if he were in the wrong until the final decree making her an allowance had taken its place. . . . The 

Appellate Division it seems to us was clearly in error in stating that the court had decided 'that it was not the duty of the husband to take care of his wife.'" (pp. 431, 432) 

The lower court had cited Simpson vs Dutcher, 123 Sup. 

340, 1910, where the third party was refused a civil recovery from the husband of monies which she had loaned the wife after a motion for alimony had been denied. 

It was said there that to uphold the action would be enabling the wife to accomplish indirectly what could not be done directly. The wife could avoid the effect of the denial of alimony by pledging her husband's credit to the same amount as any sum which might be awarded as alimony. But in view of the final ruling in Elder vs 

Rosenwasser the case of Simpson vs Dutcher would seem to be repudiated. 

See also Catlin vs Martin, 69 N. Y. 393, 1877, Hays vs 

Ledman, 28 Misc. 575, 59 Sup. 687, 1899 and Mayper vs 

Harlan, 125 Misc. 123, 210 Sup. 257, 1925, rev'd on other points 215 A.D. 767.

THE JUDGMENT 

IV This chapter takes up permanent alimony and the various features of the final judgment which are related to, identified with and affect the allowance in general. 

A. The Civil Practice Act-sections applicable 

Six sections of the Civil Practice Act are applicable to permanent alimony-1140, 1155, 1159, 1164, 1170 and 1175. Sections 1159 and 1170 are taken up in the discussion on modification.

Section 1140 

Custody and maintenance of children. If a marriage be declared a nullity or annulled, the court, by the judgment or by subsequent order may award the custody of the child of the marriage to either party as the interests of the child may require, and may make provision for its education and maintenance out of the property of either or both of its parents if the marriage shall have been declared a nullity and out of the property of the guilty parent if the marriage shall have been annulled.

The allowance is neither alimony (treating support for children as alimony), nor in the nature of alimony. 

It is limited to the property of the parents, for which reason it is immediately distinguishable from all the other allowances specified by the several sections, and affords the sole instance where the wife may be charged with the responsibility for the maintenance of children. 92

THE JUDGMENT 

93 

See Nealon 195 A.D. 694, 187 Sup. 295, 1921; Beischer, 132 Misc. 576, 230 Sup. 278, rev'd 226 A.D. 454, 235 Sup. 

652, 1929.

Section 1155 

Maintenance and support of wife and children in action for divorce by wife. The court in the final judgment dissolving the marriage in an action for divorce brought by the wife may require the defendant to provide suitably for the education and maintenance of the children of the marriage, and for the support of the plaintiff, as justice requires, having regard to the circumstances of the respective parties; and by order upon the application of either party to the action, and after due notice to the other to be given in such manner as the court shall prescribe at any time after final judgment whether heretofore or hereafter rendered may annul, vary or modify such a direction; but no such application may be made by a defendant unless leave to make the same shall have been previously granted by the court by order made upon or without notice as the court in its discretion may deem proper after presentation to the court of satisfactory proof that justice requires that such an application should be entertained. 

With respect to divorce judgments obtained by the wife, the section is a re-statement of Section 1170. It specifies virtually nothing which is not to be found there. 

It is even less emphatic, providing that the court". may require the defendant to provide . . . for the support of the plaintiff," while Section 1170 says it "must give . . . directions .. for the support of the plaintiff." 

Section 1164 

Maintenance of wife and children in action for separation. 

Where an action for separation from bed and board is brought by the wife, the court, in the final judgment of separation, may give such directions as the nature and circumstances of the case require. In particular, it may compel the defendant to provide suitably for the education and maintenance of the children of the marriage and for the support of the plaintiff, as • • •

justice requires, having regard to the circumstances of the respective parties. And the court, in such an action, may render a judgment compelling the defendant to make the provision specified in this section where, under the circumstances of the case, such a judgment is proper, without rendering a judgment of separation.

As in the case of Section 1155, this section, except for the last sentence, re-states Section 1170 with particular application to the wife's judgment of separation. Also the allowance is made discretionary instead of mandatory. 

The last sentence disposes of the rule which formerly prevailed (Douglas, 5 Hun, 140, 1875; Curnen, 155 A.D. 

536, 140 Sup. 805, 1913) that alimony may not be had without an affirmative decree to the wife. This does not mean, however, that alimony may be had in every case where the decree is withheld. Neither alimony nor support for children is proper if the complaint is dismissed for failure of proof or the wife's misconduct (Kamman, 167 A.D. 423, 152 Sup. 579, 1915, mod'g 151 Sup. 226; 

Robinson, 146 A.D. 533, 131 Sup. 260, 1911, rev'g 69 Misc. 

438, 125 Sup. 1064; Sothern, 184 A.D. 952, 170 Sup. 1114, 1918; Chamberlin, 193 A.D. 784, 184 Sup. 464, 1920; 

Northrup, 129 Misc. 616, 221 Sup. 493, 1927), or even if there is no dismissal, if the wife fails to prove the facts entitling her to a separation (Davis, 75 N. Y. 221, 1878).

It is only where the decree is not granted to the wife because the interests of justice will be best served, as where it is not actually necessary for her protection or well-being, or where she has condoned the defendant's misbehaviour although not to the extent of warranting a dismissal, that alimony will be allowed (Le Compte, 3 

Bradb. 588, 1913; Allers, 194 A.D. 96, 185 Sup. 440, 1920;

Robinson, supra). There would seem to be no objection

THE JUDGMENT 

95 to an allowance where the wife has satisfactorily established her case and announced in open court that she has reconsidered her original intention and does not desire a decree. But see Marangiello, App. Div., 2nd Dep't., December, 1930.

Alimony is proper in a judgment of limited separation (Bedell, 1 Johns. Ch. 603, 1815; Meehan, L. J., N. Y. Co., Dec. 10, 1930).

Section 1175 In an action brought for judgment annulling a marriage, or divorcing the parties and dissolving a marriage . . . interlocutory judgment . . . must be entered within fifteen days after the party becomes entitled to file or enter the same. .. The interlocutory judgment, in the discretion of the court, may provide for the payment of alimony or for the support and maintenance of the children of the marriage until the interlocutory judgment becomes final or until entry of the final judgment. The allowance provided by the interlocutory judgment is not permanent alimony. It is a special, distinct provision, created by this section of the Civil Practice Act. 

Thus, where the interlocutory decree provided for the payment of "permanent alimony," it was held that the specification was to be taken literally. No recovery of instalments could be had until after the final judgment, because they did not accrue until then (Shaw, 154 A.D. 

324, 138 Sup. 999, 1912)..

It has been held to be an open question whether the final decree may provide for alimony in the absence of a provision for it in the interlocutory decree. The safest practice is to incorporate it in the interlocutory decree (Byrnes, 126 A.D. 619, 111 Sup. 72, 1908). This serves also to settle the matter of alimony by the time the ninety days have expired.

It is no ground for refusing the wife alimony in the in-

1 # 44 11 terlocutory decree that she may never be granted a final decree (Shurman, 148 Sup. 947, 1914). 

B. Particular considerations 

Allowance discretionary

Permanent alimony, as well as temporary alimony, is discretionary. A judgment may be rendered in favor of the wife without an allowance (Avery, L. J. Bronx Co., January 30, 1931). 

Annulment actions

Under no circumstances may alimony be had in the judgment of annulment, regardless which spouse sues. 

There is nothing in the statute about it, and the courts have declined to assume the prerogative as they have in the case of temporary alimony where the wife is defendant. 

Actions by husband

Permanent alimony may not be granted in actions by the husband, regardless of the outcome (Waring, 100 N. Y. 

570, 1885; Lonsdale, 41 A.D. 224, 58 Sup. 532, 1899; 

Byrnes, 126 A.D. 619, 111 Sup. 72, 1908). 

Wife's counterclaim

The wife may have alimony in the judgment on her counterclaim (Allers, 194 A.D. 96, 185 Sup. 440, 1920).

Requirement of a valid judgment Judgments for alimony may not be had independently of a matrimonial decree (Dailey, 9 Misc. 511, 30 Sup. 337, 1894), or on consent of the parties without application

THE JUDGMENT 

97 to the court, no more than judgments of divorce or separation; and payments made thereunder may not be applied subsequently to alimony chargeable to the husband. 

Forfeiture of the right to support The judgment may not relieve the husband of the duty to support the wife, regardless of the outcome of the action (Chamberlin, 193 A.D. 784, 184 Sup. 464, 1920). 

Husband's right to contest alimony

The husband may resist the wife's demand for permanent alimony even though he concedes her right to a decree (Levene, 165 A.D. 953, 150 Sup. 708, 1914; Liebowitz, L. J., Queens Co., Feb. 26, 1931). Cases contra, as that the husband's default precludes him from opposing the request for alimony (Graves, 2 Paige Ch. 62, 1830) are not to be followed. 

C. Agreements before judgment 

Stipulations in the action

The agreement of the parties that permanent alimony be fixed at a certain sum is not improper or deemed an inducement to the separation or divorce, exactly as in the case of agreements fixing temporary alimony (Werner, 153 A.D. 719, 138 Sup. 633, 1912).
