It is not controlling, however, unless it appears that it was designed to fix the amount of permanent alimony (Carpenter, 130 Misc. 698, 225 Sup. 431, 1927).

It must be fairly entered into. It was set aside where made after the referee's report had been handed down, recommending a larger sum and there was nothing to warrant a lesser one (Hamlin, 129 Misc. 263, 221 Sup. 

249, 1927, aff'd 223 A.D. 810, 227 Sup. 819; also, 224 A.D. 

168, 230 Sup. 51, 1928).

Separation agreements 

The decree may adopt the provision in a separation agreement fixing the amount of alimony to be awarded in the event a judgment is rendered, exactly as in the case of an ordinary stipulation made for that purpose, whether the agreement is made before or pending suit (Van 

Horn, 196 A.D. 472, 188 Sup. 98, 1921; Hammerstein vs 

Equitable, 156 A.D. 644, 141 Sup. 1065, 1913, aff'd 209 

N. Y. 429; Levy, 149 A.D. 561, 133 Sup. 1084, 1912).

Where the agreement merely provides for the wife's support, it bars alimony in the final or interlocutory judgment (Galusha, 116 N. Y. 635, 1889; Grissler, 209 

A.D. 480, 205 Sup. 119, 1924), unless it provides that it is to terminate on the divorce of the parties (Atherton, 155 N. Y. 129, 1898). See Reynolds, 169 A.D. 917, 153

Sup. 680, 1915. If the amount agreed upon is clearly inadequate, the court may make an allowance for alimony in addition to it (Collins, 80 N. Y. 1, 1880).

Settlements and agreements in lieu of alimony An agreement that the wife take certain funds or property in lieu of alimony is proper and will be upheld. Inadequacy is not a ground for setting it aside in the decree, as distinguished from legal insufficiency (Cain, 188 

A.D. 780, 177 Sup. 178, 1919). The rule is the same whether the arrangement is made by separation agreement, or otherwise. It is ineffectual, however, to bar support for children (id).

The wife may enforce that provision of the agreement giving her leave to apply for alimony in the event the husband defaults on the settlement (Hammerstein vs

THE JUDGMENT 

99 

Equitable, 156 A.D. 644, 141 Sup. 1065, 1913, aff'd 209 

N. Y. 429).

D. References 

References to determine the amount of alimony to be allowed are proper (Cooledge, 1 Barb. Ch. 77, 1845; 

Graves, 2 Paige Ch. 62, 1830; Forrest, 25 N. Y. 501, 1862; 

Fleischer, 224 A.D. 46, 229 Sup. 77, 1928). The better practice, however, is for the court to take proof of the means of the parties at the trial (Le Page, 208 A.D. 458, 204 Sup. 260, 1924).

The proceeding is similar to one had on a temporary application. The referee's findings are not controlling, and the court may make a different allowance (Wallack, L. J., N. Y. Co., Jan. 30, 1931). "To the extent that provision was made for an inquiry into the defendant's financial condition and the proper amount of alimony to be allowed, the reference was not to hear and determine, but rather to inform the conscience of the court the court alone having the power in its discretion to award alimony and provide for the maintenance and custody of children (secs. 1170, 1171 C.P.A.). It follows that the court is not precluded by the referee's findings of fact or conclusions of law in regard to the amount of alimony but may treat them merely as advisory, and may review the testimony and grant alimony according to its independent judgment" (Johnstone, 130 Misc. 243, 223 

Sup. 744, 1927, pp. 745, 746) 

E. Provisions of the judgment

The form The provision in the judgment requiring the payment of alimony reads the same as the temporary order (IID, ante), except that the statement that the allowance is

* 4 118 pending the action is omitted and that payments are made payable directly to the wife or at the office of her attorney. 

When payments commence

Alimony may be made payable from the date of commencement of the action (McCarthy, 143 N. Y. 235, 1894). It is not generally made payable from that day, however, unless there was no temporary alimony (Burr, 7 Hill, 207, 1843, p. 222; Barber, 21 How. (U.S.) 582, 1858). 

Reservation of alimony for future

The judgment may reserve the question of alimony for future consideration (Matthews, 128 Misc. 425, 219 

Sup. 341, 1926), or may provide that the omission of an allowance is without prejudice (Kellogg, 122 Misc. 734, 203 Sup. 757, 1924).

Payments in lieu of and in addition to alimony The judgment may provide for the payment of specific funds in lieu of alimony, pursuant to the parties' agreement (Hammerstein vs Equitable, 156 A.D. 644, 141 Sup. 

1065, 1913, aff'd 209 N. Y. 429), in which connection it should be noted that the wife's right to permanent alimony is sufficiently valuable to make the waiver of it an adequate consideration for the husband's agreement to transfer certain assets to her (id., 209 N. Y. 429).

The judgment may make pecuniary provision for the wife by regular periodic instalments, but different from alimony, as where an agreement was incorporated in the decree, on consent, that the wife be supported for life, as part of the settlement of litigation over property rights (Goldfish, 193 A.D. 686, 184 Sup. 512, 1920, aff'd 230 N. Y. 

606). Such an allowance, and all others of its character

THE JUDGMENT 

101 which the judgment, in view of its equitable origin and nature, may contain, must be distinguished from alimony. 

Thus, where the provision is made as alimony, it will be set aside or refused recognition (Doe, 52 Hun, 405, 5 Sup. 

514, 1889; Allen vs Farmers', 18 A.D. 27, 45 Sup. 398, 1897). As the Court said in the Goldfish case, supra, in refusing to give the provision the incidents and characteristics of alimony: "That was not a provision such as is made in a divorce action for support and maintenance, but was an agreement on the part of the defendant, made to avoid litigation between him and the plaintiff with respect to their property, and in settlement of the plaintiff's claim of ownership of certain specified property." (p. 515)

The judgment may require the husband to reimburse the wife for expenditures made by her for her support, pending the action (Percival, 124 N. Y. 637, 1891). 

Other agreements

The judgment may incorporate all the miscellaneous agreements of the parties affecting alimony and support, including separation agreements and ordinary stipulations (Werner, 153 A.D. 719, 138 Sup. 633, 1912; Van Horn, 196 

A.D. 472, 188 Sup. 98, 1921) and for the support of children (In re Narelle's Estate, 136 Misc. 781, 243 Sup. 754, 1930). Also, an agreement not to request a modification (Levy, 149 A.D. 561, 133 Sup. 1084, 1912). The final judgment may confirm an agreement incorporated in the interlocutory decree (Crawford, 129 Misc. 683, 221 Sup. 

551, 1927). 

Adjudication of settlement

The judgment may not embody a determination of the validity of a settlement made in lieu of alimony, if the

agreement is valid on its face and subsisting (Grissler, 209 

A.D. 480, 205 Sup. 119, 1924). But the court may decide if the allowance or settlement is adequate, and if it is not, permit the agreement to stand and make an award in addition to it (Collins, 80 N. Y. 1, 1880; IVC, ante). 

Provisions of other judgments

The judgment may not pass upon the status of the alimony provision in another judgment. Thus, it is improper to include a provision in an interlocutory decree rendered in the husband's favor, striking out the allowance in a prior separation judgment to the wife, the proper practice being to modify the latter (Burton, 150 A.D. 790, 135 

Sup. 248, 1912).

Conditions-other pecuniary arrangements The amount of the allowance may be conditioned expressly or by inference, as where the judgment provided that the wife be allowed to live in the husband's house and that he pay the carrying charges (Malone, L. J., Queens 

Co., Jan. 28, 1931). It is improper to require him to pay the rent for the wife and children if he is willing that they occupy his house (Rosenwasser, 117 Misc. 123, 190 Sup. 

774, 1921).

The husband may be directed to reimburse the wife for her legal expenses (Beauley, 199 A.D. 279, 191 Sup. 397, 1921), and the wife to join in a deed to the husband's property on a good price being obtained and a proper adjustment of her dower rights being effected (Rosenwasser, supra). 

Provision for children

The judgment may make separate provision for alimony and for the support of children (Wetmore, 27 Misc. 

700, 59 Sup. 586, 1899).

THE JUDGMENT 

103 The judgment may require the husband to support the child without specifying any sum (Ehrich, 211 A.D. 490, 207 Sup. 219, 1924). In such a case, it would seem, the provision would not assume the character of alimony until the wife had obtained an order, on the husband's failure to make provision for the child, directing him to reimburse her for expenditures made for that purpose.

The decree may provide for an unborn child (People vs Pfister, 134 Misc. 696, 236 Sup. 589, 1929). 

Invalidity of the alimony provision

The invalidity of the provision for the payment of alimony, whatever the reason, does not, of course, affect the validity of the judgment (Robinson, 123 Misc. 80, 204 Sup. 

329, 1924; IG, ante). 

F. Automatic termination of the allowance 

Death of parties—arrears

The judgment ends on the death of either party (Johns, 44 A.D. 533, 60 Sup. 865, 1899, aff'd 166 N. Y. 613; Faversham, 161 A. D. 521, 146 Sup. 569, 1914, appeal dis'd 230 

N. Y. 662; Hunter, etc., 197 A.D. 678, 189 Sup. 831, 1921; 

Rice vs Andrews, 127 Misc. 826, 217 Sup. 528, 1926).

The judgment ends on the husband's death even though it provides that alimony is payable as long as the wife lives (Field, 66 How. Pr. 346, 15 Abb. Pr. (N. C.) 434, 1884).

Nor may the court continue payments after the husband's death. The situation is discussed at length in 

Wilson vs Hinman, 182 N. Y. 408, rev'g 99 A.D. 41, 90 

Sup. 746, 1905. Three reasons are stated for the immediate and instantaneous abatement of the allowance- (1) that alimony is a personal obligation, not a debt, (2)

that there are very few accumulated incomes and that most men depend on their earnings for their livelihood, and if their earnings cease it is only fair that the alimony which is based thereon should also cease and (3) that the wife has her share in the husband's estate after his death, and if he should choose to cut her off, it would be unreasonable to give her superior rights because she has been legally divorced or separated from him. It does not matter that he has given security to enforce payments. "This section (authorizing the court to require security of the husband) does not purport or assume to grant to the wife alimony for any longer period nor impose upon the husband or his estate any greater obligation than that awarded by the previous provisions of the decree; it is merely security for the performance of the obligation already imposed that the court is authorized to require. 

It would be an unnatural construction to give it any other effect. The security required might not be in the shape of a lien on any specific property, but merely the personal obligation of sureties. In that case it would hardly be contended that the obligation of the sureties would extend beyond that of their principal." (p. 414)

The case may be taken to refute all suggestions that the court has power to make the alimony continue after the husband's death (Johns, supra; Galusha, 116 N. Y. 635, 1889, mod❜g 43 Hun, 181; Goldfish, 193 A.D. 686, 184 Sup. 

512, 1920, aff'd 230 N. Y. 606).

It states, however, that alimony may be made to survive the husband's death by agreement of the parties, the only way it can be done. The allowance may be predicated upon a separation agreement which has been incorporated in the judgment, and if it provides that the alimony is to survive, it will be given that effect. If, however, it specifically limits the alimony to the husband's lifetime, or says nothing about its duration, the alimony does not survive

THE JUDGMENT 

105 (In re Bell's Estate, 121 Misc. 795, 200 Sup. 776, 1923; 

In re Junge, 125 Misc. 707, 212 Sup. 119, 1925).

In a case where there is a possibility that the agreement intended that the alimony survive, the judgment will not be given that effect unless it unequivocally makes that specification. It is not enough for the judgment to provide that payments are to be made during the wife's life (Johns, supra; Barnes vs Klug, 113 Sup. 325, 129 A.D. 

192, 1908) and to the extent that the Goldfish, case supra, suggests the contrary, it is not to be followed. In such a case, also, the inference of survivorship which may arise from a reading of the agreement is conclusively rebutted by the fact that the judgment makes provision for the wife, on the husband's death, other than alimony; as where the decree required him to pay the premiums on a life insurance policy for the wife's benefit (Johns, supra), and to assign a policy to her (In re Bell's Estate, supra). 

On the wife's death, liens to secure payment are unenforceable (Faversham, 161 A.D. 521, 146 Sup. 569, 1914, appeal dis'd 230 N. Y. 662). The husband cannot recover property transferred to the wife in her lifetime in consideration of her waiver of alimony (Hammerstein vs 

Equitable, 156 A.D. 644, 141 Sup. 1065, 1913, aff'd 209 

N. Y. 429). The wife, on the other hand, following the husband's death, may enforce against his estate his agreement to make such a transfer or conveyance (Wilson vs 

Hinman, 182 Ñ. Y. 408, rev'g 99 A.D. 41, 90 Sup. 746, 1905).

Arrears due when the husband dies, both of alimony and support for children, may be recovered by the wife from his estate (Faversham, supra; In re Brace's Estate, 105 

Misc. 178, 173 Sup. 636, 1918; In re Glickman's Estate 

132 Misc. 785, 230 Sup. 671, 1928).

Arrears due when the wife dies may be recovered by her personal representatives, and even from the husband's estate if he dies subsequently (Van Ness vs Ransom, 215 

N. Y. 557, 1915). "But alimony is not a personal claim in the sense that a cause of action for slander or assault is personal. It is personal in a sense that it is a provision for her maintenance and support, and cannot be diverted from that purpose. It takes the place of the husband's liability which ended with the divorce. If there had been no divorce the husband's liability would have continued while the marital relation existed, and liability on the judgment should continue to the same extent. Therefore, the alimony sued for in this action, which accrued prior to the death of the wife, was not a personal claim that died with her, but a right which survives in favor of her personal representatives. To hold otherwise would be to defeat the object of the law and seriously impair the value of the decree in the wife's favor by depriving her of the credit which she would have to obtain means of support." (p. 560) 

The effect is thus to enable recovery by and for the advantage of persons other than the wife. It is to be noted, also, that the accumulations are inheritable on the ground that they are an ordinary contractual indebtedness, nothing being said in the cases that there is any requirement to reduce them first to an ordinary money judgment, on which, for example, execution might be issued. This, in turn, means that the wife, or her representatives if she is dead, would have superior remedies by reason of the fact that the husband had died, since they could sue civilly to enforce the obligation, without being obliged to docket the arrears, which latter cannot in any event be done, even if there was such a requirement, as a judgment cannot be entered against a deceased debtor. In the lifetime of both parties, the wife would be limited to docketing and execution, and the other remedies specified by statute 

(Chapters V, VI, post).

THE JUDGMENT 

107 It should be noted, also, that the exemption of alimony from discharge in bankruptcy extends to a money judgment for arrears (In re Williams, 208 N. Y. 32, 1913). 

This case cites Wetmore vs Markoe, 196 U. S. 68, 25 Sup. 

Ct. 735, 1904, and quotes the following: "The precise question, therefore, is, is such a judgment as the one under consideration here a debt within the meaning of the act? (Bankruptcy Act). The mere fact that a judgment has been rendered does not prevent the court from looking into the proceedings with a view of determining the nature of the liability which has been reduced to judgment." (p. 72) 

Thus, alimony is divested of its personal character when it is necesary to assure its recovery at the instance of the wife or her agents. 

Husband's incompetency 

The husband's incompetency does not terminate the judgment (In re Bell's Estate, 121 Misc. 795, 200 Sup. 776, 1923). 

Subsequent divorce judgment

The alimony provided by a judgment of separation has been held not to terminate automatically upon the entry of a judgment of divorce (Burton, 150 A.D. 790, 135 Sup. 

248, 1912; Harris, 197 A.D. 646, 189 Sup. 215, 1921) and that a formal motion to modify must be made. 

This is not quite compatible, however, with the distinction which is drawn between divorce and separation judgments. "Of course, alimony awarded on the dissolution of a marriage differs in one element from that on a separation; in the latter case the decree merely defines the continuous duty still existing on the part of the husband to support

the wife, while in the former the marital obligation is terminated, and the sole liability of the husband springs from the decree." (Wilson vs Hinman, 182 N. Y. 408, 1905, pp. 

410, 411)

It would seem, therefore, that alimony provided by a separation decree terminates instantaneously upon the entry of a judgment of divorce, regardless in whose favor (Scheinwald, App. Div., 2nd Dep't, November, 1930). 

Specified contingencies

Where the decree specifically provides that the alimony is to end on the happening of a certain event, such as the wife's re-marriage, the allowance terminates automatically on that contingency (Sleicher, 251 N. Y. 366, 1929, rev'g 

224 A.D. 529, 231 Sup. 538, and aff'g 228 Sup. 711). The annulment of the second marriage reinstates the allowance, but does not revive arrears accruing after the second marriage and prior to its annulment (id.). 

G. Termination and modification by waiver and release 

Divorce judgments

The allowance provided by a judgment of divorce may be waived for or without consideration (Shinkle, L. J., Kings Co., March 20, 1931). The waiver will not preclude recovery of the alimony, however, unless an order of modification is entered, in accordance with Section 1170 of the Civil Practice Act. An exception to this rule is made where the waiver is by way of release for a consideration pursuant to an executed agreement, in which case no order of modification is necessary (Van Ness vs Ransom, 164 

A.D. 483, 150 Sup. 251, 1914, aff'd Parsons vs Macfarlane, 220 N. Y. 605; Ostrin vs Posner, 127 Misc. 313, 215 Sup. 259, 1925).

THE JUDGMENT 

109 

Separation judgments 

Alimony provided by a judgment of separation can be waived only for a consideration. The parties still being husband and wife, the arrangement might contravene 

Section 51 of the Domestic Relations Law. It has been held, also, that in addition to this requirement, there must be an order of modification (Gewirtz, 189 A.D. 483, 178 

Sup. 738, 1919; Ostrin vs Posner, supra). The latter case cites five cases for the proposition that an order is necessary, even though an adequate release has been effected. 

They are: Jones, 90 Hun, 414, 35 Sup. 877, 1895; Hobby, 5 A.D. 496, 39 Sup. 36, 1896; Gewirtz, supra; Hallow, 200 

A.D. 642, 193 Sup. 460, 1922; Bailey, 119 Misc. 433, 196

Sup. 340, 1922. Of these, however, a reconciliation was claimed by the one of the parties to have taken place in the Jones, Hobby and Bailey cases, and there, of course, Section 1165 of the Civil Practice Act made an order re voking the decree requisite. In the other two cases, Section 1170 was invoked, expressly or by implication. There does not seem to be any reason for not making an exception in the case of separation judgments as far as Section 1170 is concerned, as is made in the case of divorce judgments (Van Ness vs Ransom, supra).

The Ostrin case does make a distinction for this purpose between the two classes of judgments, on the ground that in a separation the parties are still married and Sec tion 51 of the Domestic Relations Law is applicable. But that circumstance is pertinent to the matter of consideration. The Van Ness case is cited as an authority for the distinction: but no distinction is actually mentioned there. Releases 

The wife may always release alimony (Pye, 167 A.D. 

951, 152 Sup. 564, 1915) and support for children (Gould,

18 Misc. 334, 42 Sup. 147, 1896). Her acceptance of a gross sum in lieu of alimony is an executed release (Van 

Ness vs Ransom, supra). If the settlement is executed, but notes are given to secure payment, the husband's default does not revive the duty (id.). But she can repudiate the agreement by returning the unpaid notes (Hoffstaedter, L. J., N. Y. Co., Dec. 9, 1930). Consideration

The husband's agreement to pay the arrears of alimony under a separation decree and the mutual promise to live apart are insufficient to support a waiver because he is already under the duty of paying the arrears and the parties are already separated by law (Gewirtz, supra). 

His agreement to support the child awarded to her is insufficient (Ostrin vs Posner, supra); but it will relieve him of the responsibility for its support under the decree, if the parties are divorced (id.). 

A release given on the wife's re-marriage is vitiated by its annulment, both as to the original allowance and intervening instalments. The only right which she waived, being the wife of another man at the time, was as to the existing indebtedness, and that, of course, is insufficient (Sleicher, 136 Misc. 604, 240 Sup. 362, 1930).

The husband's discontinuance of his appeal from the judgment, and of his action to establish property rights against the wife, pursuant to their agreement that the alimony be reduced, were held to be a sufficient consideration (Aldrich, 220 A.D. 555, 222 Sup. 56, 1927, appeal dis'd 247 N. Y. 563).

Where the settlement is attacked by the wife for want of consideration or any other ground, or its existence denied, in her action to recover the alimony, the fact that

THE JUDGMENT 

111 she failed for many years to take steps to enforce the judgment is a circumstance favoring its sufficiency or existence (Van Ness vs Ransom, supra). 

Proceedings to vacate settlements

A motion to set aside a settlement of alimony is improper if it is valid on its face and subsisting (Pye, supra). An action must be brought. The same applies to agreements modifying the allowance. A decree setting aside a settlement or modification of alimony may not include a judgment for arrears, because the judgment of divorce is deemed one for the payment of money within the terms of the statute requiring that ten years shall have elapsed from the date of entry before proceedings for its recovery may be taken (Farquhar, 172 A.D. 242, 158 Sup. 194, 1916). 

H. Modification on motion 

(1) THE STATUTE 

Two sections of the Civil Practice Act are applicable, 1159 and 1170. The latter will be taken up first because it is more general.

Section 1170. Custody and maintenance of children, and support of plaintiff in action for divorce or separation. . 

The court, by order, upon the application of either party to the action, or any other person or party having the care, custody and control of said child or children pursuant to said final judgment or order, after due notice to the other, to be given in such manner as the court shall prescribe, at any time after final judgment, may annul, vary or modify such directions, or in case no such direction or directions shall have been made, amend it by inserting such direction or directions as justice requires for the custody, care, education and maintenance of any such child or children or for the support of the plaintiff in such final judgment or order or orders. But no such appli-

cation shall be made by a defendant, or any other person having the care, custody and control of said infant or infants, unless leave to make the same shall have been previously granted by the court by order made upon or without notice as the court in its discretion may deem proper after presentation to the court of satisfactory proof that justice requires that such an application should be granted.

For a discussion of the history of modification in the 

New York statute, see Sistare, 218 U. S. 1, 1909.

Application of the section-reservation of alimony Section 1170 does not apply to divorce judgments in effect at the time of its passage (Goodsell, 82 A.D. 65, 81 

Sup. 806, 1903; Crawford, 129 Misc. 683, 221 Sup. 551, 1927). It was not intended to apply to them, and even if it did, it is doubtful if it would be constitutional, despite the fact that it is remedial merely (Walker, 155 

N. Y. 77, 1898, rev'g 21 A.D. 219, 47 Sup. 513; Livingston, 173 N. Y. 377, 1903, aff'g 74 A.D. 261, 77 Sup. 476).

The original statute from which Section 1170 is derived made no provision for modification. In time, it was amended to provide for it, but only where alimony had already been allowed. If no award had been made, there was no way of having it inserted. The section as amended was held to apply to existing judgments if there was at least a reservation of the question of alimony, without any sum being fixed (Hauscheld, 33 A.D. 296, 53 

Sup. 831, 1898; Peo. ex rel Hauscheld vs Dunn, 159 N. Y. 

570, 1899, aff'g 38 A.D. 640). But see Werner, 153 A.D. 

719, 138 Sup. 633, 1912.

Today it is not necessary that there be either an allowance or a reservation of the question of alimony. The point is therefore important only in so far as judgments are concerned which may still be subsisting from a time prior to the amendment.

THE JUDGMENT 

113 The section has always been applicable to separation judgments in effect at the time (Tonjes, 14 A.D. 542, 43 

Sup. 941, 1897).

The interlocutory, as well as the final decree, is subject to modification (Griffin, 219 A.D. 370, 220 Sup. 39, 1927). Annulments

Neither this section nor any other in the Civil Practice Act makes provision for modification of the allowance for children specified in Section 1140. It may therefore be concluded that the court has no power to alter the allowance. 

(2) CHANGE OF CIRCUMSTANCES 

The court will modify the allowance on proof of change of circumstances. The underlying principles are the same as in the case of temporary alimony (IIK, ante). The proof of change must be clear (Matzke, 185 A. D. 533, 173 

Sup. 244, 1918). Support for children may be modified (Baron, L. J., N. Y. Co., Jan. 9, 1931). 

Individual cases

The following were accepted as proper considerations in determining change of circumstances: the possibility of augmented income to the husband (Faye, 131 Misc. 

388, 226 Sup. 729, 1928); the wife's possession of certain personalty, including the household furniture (Carpenter, 130 Misc. 698, 225 Sup. 431, 1927); the husband's expenses incurred by the wife's illness (Davis, 78 A.D. 500, 79 Sup. 621, 1903); the wife's re-marriage (even before 

Section 1159 made a modification mandatory), in which case the court may inquire into the means of the second

husband (Earle, 164 A.D. 713, 150 Sup. 173, 1914, aff'd 

222 N. Y. 589); her inheritance of a legacy (Holmes, 4 

Barb. 295, 1848). 

The following were not accepted: the husband's anticipatory earnings (Carpenter, supra); expenditures incurred by the wife for the support of her adult children and grandchildren (Parker, 189 A.D. 603, 179 Sup. 51, 1919); the husband's re-marriage (Levy, 149 A.D. 561, 133 Sup. 1084, 1912).

The fact that the motion comes soon after the judgment will count against a modification (Brennen, L. J., Kings 

Co., May 19, 1931). 

Wife's re-marriage

The fact that the allowance terminates automatically on the wife's re-marriage, where the decree provides that it is to continue until that event, does not bar a formal motion to modify the judgment. The termination of the allowance (IVF, ante) is a legal consequence which does not appear from the decree. On the husband's death, a charge upon his estate might result, and his representatives would have to establish the fact of the wife's remarriage under possibly difficult conditions (Goldfish, 193 

A.D. 686, 184 Sup. 512, 1920).

References The court frequently refers the matter to inquire into the claim of a change in circumstances (Kabatchnick, 26 

A.D. 292, 49 Sup. 612, 1898; Bishop, 210 A. D. 226, 205 

Sup. 539, 1924). Appeals

The order granting or denying an increase or reduction is reviewable (Davis, 78 A. D. 500, 79 Sup. 621, 1903; 

Goldner, App. Div., 2nd Dep't, December 1930).

THE JUDGMENT 

115 Limited modification A modification may be had for a specified period of time (Davis, supra). 

(3) EQUITABLE CONSIDERATIONS 

Reductions and increases may be granted for purely equitable reasons, entirely unrelated to pecuniary circumstances, as where the wife disobeyed the court by sending the child to a private school instead of a military school (Parkinson, 222 A.D. 838, 226 Sup. 454, 1928). For a discussion of the authority of the court to intervene on the wife's behalf where she has been subjected to fraud or over-reaching, see Hamlin, 129 Misc. 263, 221 Sup. 249, 1927, aff'd 223 A.D. 810, 227 Sup. 819. 

(4) MOTION TO INSERT ALIMONY 

The old rule

There is a long line of cases holding that alimony may not be inserted in the judgment where there was neither a provision for it of some sort or at least a reservation of the question. They are not to be followed any longer, now that the section has been amended (IVH1, ante). In order to avoid any confusion, however, it is well to recognize them. They are: Kamp, 59 N. Y. 212, 1874, rev'g 

5 J & S 241; Erkenbrach, 96 N. Y. 456, 1884; Cullen, 55 Sup. Ct. Rep. 346, 1888; Chamberlain, 63 Hun, 96, 17 

Sup. 578, 1892; Gould, 18 Misc. 334, 42 Sup. 147, 1896; 

Noble, 20 A.D. 395, 46 Sup. 820, 1897; Hauscheld, 33 

A.D. 296, 53 Sup. 831, 1898; Salomon, 101 A.D. 588, 92 

Sup. 184, 1905; Koehl, 92 Misc. 579, 156 Sup. 234, 1915.

The explanation for the old rule is that the judgment is final not only as to the matters actually passed upon, but

those which could have been litigated in the cause and thus adjudicated. This principle is still sound and fully applies to alimony judgments, with the exception which the express letter of the statute has made. 

Individual cases

The following are characteristic instances of modification by insertion of an allowance in the judgment: where the parties had made a pendente lite agreement for the wife's support with leave to her to move for alimony in the event of his default (Hammerstein vs Equitable, 156 

A.D. 644, 141 Sup. 1065, 1913, aff'd 209 N. Y. 429); where they had made a separation agreement and he had defaulted (Ryskind, 230 A.D. 481, 246 Sup. 789, 1930); where they had made a stipulation for support, in contemplation of the judgment, and it was inadvertently omitted from the decree (Lester, 102 Misc. 630, 169 Sup. 

267, 1918, mod'd 183 A.D. 939, 169 Sup. 1101). 

Validity of the agreement

The agreement which is sought to be inserted in the judgment, as the basis for the allowance, must be valid. 

If it is illegal because it is an inducement to a separation, it will be rejected (Werner, 153 A.D. 719, 138 Sup. 633, 1912). 

Provisions other than alimony

The decree may be modified at the wife's instance to provide for allowances in the nature of but different from alimony. This includes expenses of the child's illness (Finn, 222 A.D. 34, 225 Sup. 336, 1927), counsel fees (Prehn, L. J., N. Y. Co., Dec. 11, 1930), and pendente lite expenses in general (Gellert, 219 A.D. 737, 219 Sup. 820, 1926, aff❜g 128 Misc. 146, 218 Sup. 555).

THE JUDGMENT 

117 It may not be modified to provide for the payment of arrears, because of the vested property nature of instalments of permanent alimony (Sistare, 218 U. S. 1, 1909). 

Nor does it make any difference that alimony was withheld because of the parties' agreement, under which the husband has defaulted, and that the judgment provides that the wife may at any time "apply at the foot of this judgment for further relief in the premises" (Crawford, 129 Misc. 683, 221 Sup. 551, 1927). 

Support for children may be inserted (Anderson vs 

Cullen, 16 Daly, 15, 8 Sup. 643, 1890; White, 154 A.D. 

250, 138 Sup. 1082, 1912; Erkenbrach, supra). This is so even where the child has been awarded to the wife with the duty on her part to support it, if she shows reduced circumstances (Earle, 158 A.D. 552, 143 Sup. 841, 1913).

On such a motion, the husband may not raise the question of legitimacy (Dornfeld, 200 A.D. 38, 192 Sup. 497, 1922). 

Motion by child

The child may move for modification to provide for his support providing both parties are alive (Rice vs Andrews, 127 Misc. 826, 217 Sup. 528, 1926). 

Motion by husband

The husband was not permitted to amend the judgment by inserting a stipulation for the wife's support with a provision in it that she could move for a modification in the event of his default, where his purpose was to thereafter apply to strike it out for her re-marriage. The objections were that by the terms of the stipulation the remedy was available to the wife, not the husband, and that it was not necessary for him to pursue such a circuitous course (Werner, 153 A.D. 719, 138 Sup. 633, 1912).

(5) MOTION TO STRIKE OUT ALIMONY 

Wife's re-marriage

The motion to strike out the allowance was in order where the wife's re-marriage (even without the application of Section 1159) had so improved her circumstances as to obviate the necessity of any allowance (Comstock, 49 Misc. 599, 99 Sup. 1057, 1906). 

Entry of divorce judgment

The alimony provided by a separation decree will be stricken out on the husband's motion if he wins an interlocutory decree (Burton, 150 A.D. 790, 135 Sup. 248, 1912) or a foreign decree (Richards, 87 Misc. 134, 149 Sup. 1028, 1914, aff'd 167 A.D. 922); or if the wife obtains a divorce (Harris, 197 A.D. 646, 189 Sup. 215, 1921), in which case she is estopped from setting up its invalidity (Gibson, 81 

Misc. 508, 143 Sup. 37, 1913). 

Default judgments

The husband may be required to pay the arrears before the default judgment against him is opened and the alimony provision vacated (Weidner, 85 Hun, 432, 32 Sup. 

894, 1895). 

Support for children

The motion to strike out the allowance for children is in order when they have reached their majority or have been adopted (Halsted, 228 A.D. 298, 239 Sup. 422, 1930; 

Halperin, L. J., N. Y. Co., Jan. 23, 1931; Gross, 110 Misc. 

278, 179 Sup. 900, 1920). See also Kellogg, 122 Misc. 734, 203 Sup. 757, 1924.

A settlement of both alimony and support for children, made in contemplation of the wife's re-marriage, does not

THE JUDGMENT 

119 justify a motion to strike out, because the alimony may be vacated when she does re-marry, and the provision for the children when they are adopted. Otherwise, and until then, they must be supported by the husband (Shinkle, L. J., Kings Co., March 20, 1931).

The husband may move to strike out the allowance for support of children on a showing that the wife is not applying the fund to the purpose for which it was intended by the court; as where the judgment required her to send the child to a military school and he was sent instead to a private school (Parkinson, 222 A.D. 838, 226 Sup. 454, 1928). 

Wife's misconduct after divorce judgment

The husband may not modify the judgment of divorce for the wife's subsequent immorality because they are no longer married and she owes him no duty (Forrest, 3 

Bosw. 661, 1859; Hayes, 220 N. Y. 596, 1917, aff❜g 175 

A.D. 941).

Wife's adultery after separation judgmenther behaviour in general as an item in determining the amount

It is an open question whether the alimony in a separation decree may be stricken out for the wife's subsequent adultery. It was done in one case where the husband had urged as an additional ground his reduced circumstances (Avellino, L. J., Kings Co., Feb. 7, 1931). In the Burton case, supra, the court wrote:- "Of course, the language of the court in People ex rel 

Comm'rs of Charities v Cullen, 153 N. Y. 636... that, after a judgment of judicial separation, the wife 'was no longer bound to perform any of the duties of a wife' is not

14 to be strained to the implication that the wife when living apart could commit adultery without endangering a judicial provision for her maintenance, which rests upon the existence of her status as a wife." (p. 249) 

The statement is obiter dicta, however, and is negatively put. It cannot be taken, however, as a suggestion that the husband could rid himself of the alimony by bringing a divorce suit because that would hardly require a statement. It may be interpreted, therefore, as meaning a modification.

The expressions of other jurisdictions are few in number and do not seem to agree (Sloan vs Cox, 4 Haywood (Tenn.) 75, 1817; Cariens, 40 S. E. Rep. 335, 50 W. Va. 

113, 1901).

Two outstanding decisions in our own forum may be studied in this connection. In the Forrest case, supra, the court, after reviewing the authorities in so far as divorce judgments are concerned, takes up, incidentally, the situation with respect to separation judgments, and observes that the cases which hold that "where a wife, who is entitled to jointure or the benefit of a settlement, but finds it necessary to invoke the aid of a court of equity to obtain the benefit of the trust or the execution of the agreements, she will not be defeated in that court by proof of her adultery, or that she is then living in adultery, have an analogous and forcible bearing upon the present question. If the Court of Chancery could exercise its discretion in view of such moral considerations as the condition of such a complainant would suggest, it would seem not unreasonable to refuse to enforce a settlement in favor of a woman, who, though still in law a wife, was living in flagrant and profligate disregard of the obligations which formed the inducement to the settlement. . . . Not so. The Court of Chancery will interfere at the instance of the wife, and enforce the specific execution of marriage articles, although the wife is living separate from the husband in a state of adultery; and so,

THE JUDGMENT 

121 also, to compel the performance of articles binding the husband to her maintenance on an agreement to separate although she then be living in adultery." (pp. 669, 672, italics ours)

The analogy between the situations which prevail as to divorce and separation judgments is further pointed out in the observation that the independent state which the wife obtains by being divorced is not the only consideration opposed to a modification. Conceding that her misconduct would neither be countenanced nor condoned and that the natural tendency would be to reduce or vacate the allowance, the court proceeds:- "But this would plainly be extreme, for more than one reason. Possibly her unprotected and destitute condition may be itself the cause of her decline from virtue; and if not so, still she is not to be left to degradation as her only resource." (p. 673)

In any event, it would seem that the wife's subsequent misconduct is not such a "change of circumstances" as to bring it within the cases which have made the change the condition to modifying the alimony or striking it out. 

Pecuniary circumstances have been determining factors, not moral character. In this connection, the other case referred to may be taken up. In Goodsell, 82 A.D. 65, 81 

Sup. 806, 1903, the court denied the husband's application for a re-hearing on the question of alimony following a divorce judgment in favor of the wife, on the ground that her adultery alleged to have been committed before the trial was no longer a matter which could be taken up in determining either the propriety or amount of the allowance. It was conceded, however, that ordinarily the wife's behaviour is a proper consideration in determining the amount.

""The circumstances of the parties,' as used in these statutory provisions, with reference to the original decree, is quite comprehensive, and not only to the pecuniary circumstances and the requirements of the parties in view of the manner in which they had lived, but also authorized and required a consideration of the conduct of each; and, if the wife was to some extent responsible for the misconduct of the husband, or in fault, although this did not justify his acts, yet it might be considered in determining the amount of alimony that she should receive." (p. 808) 

See also Peckford, 1 Paige Ch. 274, 1828, holding that the wife's indiscretions and other misbehaviour are proper considerations in determining the amount of alimony to be allowed; and Burr, 7 Hill, 207, 1843, p. 218, that the husband's extreme ill-treatment of the wife may serve to make the allowance larger than would otherwise be made. The principle is somewhat analogous to the one which justifies a larger award of alimony so as to include re-imbursement to the wife for expenses incurred by her as a result of the husband's misconduct, despite the fact that alimony is not determined in the same way as damages for torts (Comstock, 49 Misc. 599, 99 Sup. 1057, 1906).

The foregoing settles the matter as far as personal characteristics constitute "circumstances of the parties"; but the Goodsell case held that the wife's adultery was an exception. It was not a phase of behaviour as could play any part in the issue because it would necessarily defeat a decree, and alimony, in the first instance.

Thus, the wife's adultery was expressly ruled out as an item to be acknowledged in fixing the alimony. The question arises, however, whether the decision is controlling on the instant situation, because there the adultery was alleged to have been committed before the judgment. 

Is the fact that the wife has broken her vows after a judgment of separation a circumstance which can impair the

THE JUDGMENT 

123 alimony at most, while if it occurred prior would defeat the judgment itself? Such a situation would seem to be anomalous. If the alimony can be stricken out, the decree may be upset as well, and none of the cases or commentators go as far as that.

The weight of opinion opposes such a modification. If nothing else, it would be irregular at least to permit the setting aside of a determination arrived at after a formal inquiry by legal evidence by a lesser quality and degree of proof. Nor would the objection be cured by having testimony taken, for the proceeding would then assume the aspect of a new trial, as far as the alimony was concerned, and it neither purports to be that, nor in the majority of cases would be warranted as such by procedural specifications. 

(6) SECTION 1159 

The statute-application 

Section 1159. Modification of judgment or order in action for divorce brought by wife. When an action for divorce is brought by a wife, and a final judgment of divorce has been rendered in her favor, the court, by order upon the application of the defendant on notice, and on proof of the marriage of the plaintiff after such final judgment, must modify such final judgment and any orders made with respect thereto by annulling the provisions of such final judgment or orders, or both, directing payment of moneys for the support of the plaintiff. 

The section is inapplicable to existing judgments (Krauss, 127 A.D. 740, 111 Sup. 788, 1908).

It is construed as the expression of the legislative intent that the wife should not be supported by two men. 

It therefore affects separation agreements as well (Schley vs Andrews, 225 N. Y. 110, rev'g 171 A.D. 952, 156 Sup. 

1144, 1919).

It finally disposes of the conception, once held in some favor, of alimony as a penalty (Shepherd, 1 Hun, 240, 1874, aff'd 58 N. Y. 644). 

Date of modification

The modification takes effect as of the date of the order (Cary, 217 N. Y. 670, aff'g 168 A.D. 939, 1915; Mowbray, 136 A.D. 513, 121 Sup. 45, 1910; Cappiello, L. J., N. Y. Co., Jan. 2, 1931). But see Liss, id., Nov. 14, 1930. 

Scope of the section

On this motion, the court will not entertain the husband's application to modify the judgment for impropriety of the allowance, nor will vacate any allowance other than alimony or in the nature of alimony (Goldfish, 193 A.D. 

686, 184 Sup. 512, 1920, aff'd 230 N. Y. 606). Nor is the section authority for the vacating of a transfer of property made by the husband to the wife in contemplation of a divorce, on her re-marriage (West vs Burke, 165 A.D. 

667, 151 Sup. 329, 1915, aff'd 219 N. Y. 7). 

Support for children 

Support for children will not be stricken out (Tooker, 137 Misc. 158, 241 Sup. 623, 1930). 

Husband's death pending motion

If the husband dies pending the motion, his estate may step in and continue the proceedings (Hunter, etc., 197 

A.D. 678, 189 Sup. 831, 1921). 

(7) OBJECTIONS TO THE MOTION TO MODIFY 

The husband's agreement not to move for a modification did not preclude him from making the motion (Levy, 149

THE JUDGMENT 

125 1 

A.D. 561, 133 Sup. 1084, 1912), but the wife's agreement was enforced (Finn, 222 A.D. 34, 225 Sup. 336, 1927).

The wife may make the motion even though the judgment provides for modification on "joint petition” (Anderson, 110 Misc. 123, 179 Sup. 865, 1920).

It is no objection to the husband's motion that he made it difficult for the wife to collect the alimony (Kiralfy, 36 

Misc. 407, 73 Sup. 708, 1901; Comstock, 49 Misc. 599, 99 

Sup. 1057, 1906).

The husband will be refused a modification if he is in arrears (Richards, 87 Misc. 134, 149 Sup. 1028, 1914, aff'd 

167 A.D. 922); but not if the modification is under Section 

1159 (Skidmore, 160 A.D. 594, 145 Sup. 939, 1914; Linton vs Hall, 86 Misc. 560, 149 Sup. 385, 1914; Kalisky, L. J., Kings Co., May 7, 1931), one reason being that the modification cannot impair the wife's right to the arrears (Mowbray, 136 A.D. 513, 121 Sup. 45, 1910).

Arrears are not an objection to a motion to reduce for change of circumstances if the husband is unable to pay them. The subject is taken up in the chapter on enforcement by contempt (V).

His remarrying within the prohibited three months may be an objection (Allaire, 172 A. D. 923, 157 Sup. 1116, 1916).

It may be noted in this connection that the husband was denied leave to re-marry, following a divorce judgment against him, because of his arrears (Matter of Calafiura, L. J., N. Y. Co., May 8, 1931).

The fact that the alimony provision which is sought to be modified was incorporated in the parties' contract made part of the judgment is not a valid objection to a modification (Kunker, 230 A.D. 641, 246 Sup. 118, 1930).

(8) OVERPAYMENTS 

In the event of a modification, the husband will be given credit for overpayments (Brice, 225 A.D. 453, 233 Sup. 

366, 1929). See Griffin, 219 A.D. 370, 220 Sup. 39, 1929. (9) PROCEDURE

The court will not entertain an application to modify except on formal motion (Parkinson, 222 A.D. 838, 226 Sup. 

454, 1928).

When the motion is under Section 1170, leave must be first obtained to make the motion, as the section itself specifies. An ex parte order is sufficient. The affidavit submitted with the order need not be as complete or comprehensive as the affidavit on the motion itself. A bare, prima-facie showing of the change of circumstances or other ground is enough. 

An order to show cause is not the "leave of the court" (Preston, 227 A.D. 670, 236 Sup. 27, 1929). A denial of the motion for the failure to obtain leave is generally without prejudice (Workin, 225 A.D. 790, 232 Sup. 922, 1929; Proag, L. J., Kings Co., Feb. 23, 1931). In one case the motion was denied but leave was granted under the prayer for other and further relief (Peters, id., N. Y. Co., Feb. 13, 1931). 

References on the motion are common (Shurman, 148 

Sup. 947, 1914; Salmon, L. J., Kings Co., Dec. 19, 1930).

The order directing a reference will be set aside on appeal if the appellate court decides that it would be useless in view of the applicant's failure to show any change of circumstances (Levene, 165 A.D. 953, 150 Sup. 708, 1914). 

I. Appeals

The husband may appeal from the judgment if it makes an objectionable provision for alimony (Cowles, 29 A.D.

THE JUDGMENT 

127 

476, 51 Sup. 1057, 1898). The Appellate Division can modify the allowance (Goetz, 217 A.D. 31, 216 Sup. 434, 1926). 

The giving of a bond stays the alimony. If the judgment requires the husband to give security for the enforcement of the alimony, that must be done, too, before the stay is effective (Galusha, 108 N. Y. 114, 1888).

The judgment may be entered nunc pro tunc as of the day of the argument of the appeal, if the husband dies thereafter, in order to preserve the wife's rights in the alimony (Bell, 181 U. S. 175, 1901).

J. Status, significance and effect of permanent alimony and the final judgment with respect to support in general 

Nature of permanent alimony

Instalments accruing under the final judgment are the wife's vested property, in distinction from temporary alimony (Krauss, 127 A.D. 740, 111 Sup. 788, 1908). The wife may compel the husband to pay the interest on arrears (Wright, 139 Misc. 423, 248 Sup. 317, 1931).

The judgment is entitled to recognition and enforcement in every other state, except when the allowance is completely subject to the discretion of the forum where it was granted. This is not the case in New York (Sistare, 218 U. S. 1, 1909).

The judgment is not a lien on the husband's property. 

He is not precluded from disposing of it and third parties take it free and clear of the wife's claim, unless the husband was in arrears at the time and made the transfer to defeat recovery, and the third parties knew of it (Sidway, 156 A.D. 375, 141 Sup. 391, 1913).

Divorce to wife with alimony

The allowance measures the extent of the husband's liability for the wife's support. She has no other remedy than through the judgment. The husband is not responsible for her debts, regardless of their nature or the circumstances which predicate them. Third parties dealing with the wife have no recourse except to the alimony. 

Divorce to husband, or to wife without alimony

The wife has forfeited her right to support, either by the judgment or the common law. The judgment may not be modified to provide for special allowances because the parties are no longer married, in addition to the fact that the absence of an allowance is an adjudication that the wife for one reason or another was not entitled to any (where the judgment is in her favor). 

Judgment granting or denying wife separation, with alimony

The allowance measures the husband's liability for the wife's support. "Where the divorce court takes jurisdiction for the purpose of legalizing a separation of spouses, judicial action upon the wife's support changes the state of the case. 

Alimony now becomes the regular standard of allowance for necessaries; and hence the payment of alimony, even if actually insufficient for the wife's maintenance, will discharge the husband from further liability for her sup port." (Schouler, Marriage, Divorce and Separation, 6th 

Ed., Vol. 1, p. 131)

The husband's common law duty is thus limited and made specific. Nevertheless, he is liable to third parties for the wife's emergency needs, such as predicate what

THE JUDGMENT 

129 have been referred to here as special allowances (IIIH, ante). The judgment may be modified to provide for them, or the husband may be sued for necessaries. The husband was held to be liable to the wife's attorney for his services in increasing the alimony (Horn vs Schmalholz, 150 A.D. 333, 134 Sup. 652, 1912; Hauser, 154 Sup. 

1072, 1915).

The wife may not recover directly from the husband for expenditures for necessaries made by her with funds drawn from the alimony, because they are not from her "separate estate" (Hauser, supra).

The allowance is not a bar to alimony in a subsequent action for divorce brought by either spouse, but the wife must first waive the prior allowance (McDonough, 26 

How. Pr. 193, 1863). See Saltzman, 228 A.D. 788, 239 

Sup. 381, mod'd 228 A.D. 819, 239 Sup. 865, 1930. 

Separation to wife without alimony

The husband's common law obligation continues (Elder vs Rosenwasser, 238 N. Y. 427, 1924). The wife cannot maintain a prosecution for non-support (Peo. ex rel 

Comm'rs vs Cullen, 153 N. Y. 629, 1897, as interpreted by Elder vs Rosenwasser, supra). 

Separation to husband

The situation is exactly the same as a divorce to the husband, or to the wife without alimony. Her right to support is forfeited (Palmer, 1 Paige Ch. 276, 1828; Perry, 2 Barb. Ch. 311, 1847). It is not revived by the institution by her of an action for divorce. "There can be no question but that the rights and obligations of the parties in relation to the marriage were determined by the judgment which separated the defendant from the plaintiff as to bed and board forever. Such a

judgment of separation is entirely inconsistent with the continuance of an obligation of the husband to support the wife, and ends the obligation assumed by the husband upon the marriage to support his wife. While the marriage was not annulled, the mutual obligations of the parties to the marriage on the part of the wife to live with her husband, and correspondingly on the part of the husband to live with or support his wife, were determined. The parties were legally separated, and the reciprocal obligations of the husband and wife of consort and support were terminated. There was, therefore, a valid adjudication that thereafter the wife was under no obligation to live with her husband, and the husband was under no further obligation to support his wife. So long as that judgment remained in full force and effect, the Court could not in any subsequent action enforce an obligation to support which had been determined by the adjudication between the parties. The subsequent commission of adultery by the husband, while giving to the wife the right to a judgment dissolving the marriage, did not in any way revive the obligation of the husband to support the wife either before or after the judgment." (Byrnes, 126 A.D. 619, 111 Sup. 

72, 1908, p. 74) Dismissal

The parties are left in statu quo. Whatever rights the wife had prior to the action remain unchanged.

If the wife's action is dismissed on the merits, the husband may recover counsel fee paid (Benesch, 106 Misc. 

395, 173 Sup. 629, 1918). 

Support for children

Regardless of the outcome of the case, the husband's duty to support the children continues (Gellert, 128 Misc. 

146, 218 Sup. 555, 1926, aff'd 219 A.D. 737, 219 Sup. 820); but the wife was not permitted to recover for the child's maintenance, where she had been denied a separation or an allowance (Dumarest, 208 A.D. 704, 202 Sup. 218, 1923), and where no provision for the children had been made in

THE JUDGMENT 

131 her divorce judgment, on the ground that the absence of an award was a determination that the husband was not liable to the wife for their necessaries (Rich, 88 Hun, 566, 34 Sup. 854, 1895). The Rich case, however, has been held to be no longer authoritative (Manice vs Randolph, 221 

A.D. 570, 224 Sup. 496, 1927, rev'g 129 Misc. 172, 220 Sup. 

664). See also Laumeier, 237 N. Y. 357, 1924, rev❜g 206 

A.D. 246, 200 Sup. 606.

Separation agreements 

The separation agreements survives the decree unless it is a divorce in the husband's favor, or if it specifies that it is to endure while the parties are married following a divorce to either party (Cain, 188 A.D. 780, 177 Sup. 178, 1919; Benesch, supra; Boate, 114 Misc. 321, 187 Sup. 321, 1921, aff'd 115 Misc. 689, 190 Sup. 914; .Shurman, 148 

Sup. 947, 1914).

It survives the decree if it specifies that it is to endure until the husband's death (Galusha, 116 N. Y. 635, 1889, mod❜g 43 Hun, 181).

ENFORCEMENT: 

Contempt 

V A. Nature and basis of the remedy 

Enforcement in general

The expression "remedy" as used in this and in the following chapter signifies the method of assuring to the wife recovery, both of instalments already accrued and in advance of their accrual. There are only three methods of collecting arrears contempt, sequestration and execution (White, 224 A.D. 355, 231 Sup. 146, 1928).

Every allowance, both of alimony and in the nature of alimony, viz., support for children, counsel fees, expenses and special allowances, is enforceable by contempt. It must, however, be incorporated in an order or judgment, as in the case, for example, of referees' fees (Heatherton, 1 N. Y. W'kly Dig. 27, 1875). 

The statute

The remedy of contempt in matrimonial actions is pro vided by Section 1172 of the Civil Practice Act. The sta tute is the sole authority (Stanley, 116 A.D. 544, 101 Sup. 

725, 1906). 

Enforcement by contempt proceedings of judgment or order in action for divorce or separation. Where the husband, in action for divorce or separation, or for the enforcement in this 132

ENFORCEMENT: CONTEMPT 

133 state of a judgment for divorce or separation rendered in another state, makes default in paying any sum of money as required by the judgment or order directing the payment thereof, and it appears presumptively, to the satisfaction of the court, that payment cannot be enforced by means of the sequestration of his property or by resorting to the security, if any, given as prescribed by statute, the court, in its discretion, may make an order requiring the husband to show cause before it at a time and place therein specified why he should not be punished for his failure to make the payment; and thereupon proceedings must be taken to punish him, as prescribed by article nineteen of the judiciary law for the punishment of a contempt of court other than a criminal contempt, and where the judgment or order directs the payment to be made in instalments, or at stated intervals, failure to make such single payment or instalment may be punished as therein provided, and such punishment, either by fine or commitment, shall not be a bar to a subsequent proceeding to punish him as for a contempt for his failure to pay subsequent instalments, but for such purpose he may be proceeded against under the said order in the same manner and with the same effect as though such instalment or payment was directed to be paid by a separate and distinct order, and the provisions of section seventy-two of the civil rights law are hereby superseded so far as they are in conflict herewith. Such order to show cause may also be made without any previous sequestration or direction to give security where the court is satisfied that they would be ineffectual. No demand of any kind upon the husband shall be necessary in order that he be proceeded against and punished for failure to make any such payment or to pay any such instalment.

From the language of the section, four characteristics of matrimonial contempts appear-(1) that foreign judgments are enforceable by contempt, (2) that the contempt is civil, not criminal, (3) that one proceeding does not bar further proceedings in the same action and (4) that no demand need be made as a prerequisite to the proceeding. 

Except for these, the remedy is the same which is prescribed by the several sections of the Judiciary Law, Article nineteen (752 et seq.). Once the proceeding is in-

stituted by order to show cause, the Judiciary Law becomes controlling (Stahl, 59 Hun, 621, 12 Sup. 854, 1891).

It is evident, also, that the statute contemplates appropriation of property, if that can be had, before resort to contempt (Lansing, 4 Lans. 377, 1871; Sandford, 44 Hun, 563, 12 Civ. Pro. Rep. 183, 1887).

For a discussion of matrimonial contempts see In re 

Sims, 57 Hun, 433, 11 Sup. 211, 1890. For the power of the court to punish matrimonial contempts independently of the statute see Ford, 10 Abb. Pr. (N.S.) 74, 1871. 

The right to the remedy

The statute assures the remedy to the wife. Provisions in the order or judgment granting the alimony tending to limit or impair the right cannot have that effect (Ryckman, 34 Hun, 235, 1884, aff'd 98 N. Y. 639). But the right is discretionary, not absolute (Uttal, 140 A.D. 255, 125 

Sup. 2, 1910; Barrett, 221 A.D. 710, 225 Sup. 174, 1927).

B. Considerations before the court on the motion to punish-classification of defences

Generally speaking, there are two questions before the court on a motion to punish the husband for contempt. 

They are (1) is the husband in default? and (2) is the default wilful? (Merrifield, 151 A.D. 931, 136 Sup. 87, 1912; 

Thompson, 197 A.D. 228, 188 Sup. 785, 1921). The determination of these questions rests, in turn, upon a host of more specific considerations, and for the purpose of presenting them adequately, the following outline of defences and objections is given. 

I ON THE MERITS 

A. No arrears 

1. Payment

ENFORCEMENT: CONTEMPT 

135 

2. Indirect payment 

3. Provision in lieu of alimony 

4. Void mandate B. Impropriety of contempt 

5. Wife's laches 

6. Wife's waiver 

7. Wife's bad faith 

8. Wife's misconduct 

9. Reconciliation 

10. Disposition of the cause 

11. Divorce after separation 

12. Wife's re-marriage

II DILATORY 13. Ineffectiveness of an adjudication 

14. Husband's financial responsibility 

15. Husband's inability to comply 

16. Lack or insufficiency of service of the order or judgment 

17. Lack or insufficiency of service of the order to show cause 18. Defect in the moving papers 

19. Prematurity of the motion

Payment (IA1) If the wife denies the husband's claim of payment, the issue is usually referred (Endler, L. J., Bronx Co., Dec. 12, 1930; Goodman, id., Kings Co., Dec. 23, 1930).

If the husband offers proof of payment which is on its face conclusive, such as receipts, and the matter rests there, the motion may be denied forthwith (Richiusa, id., N. Y. Co., March 20, 1930; Proag. id., Kings Co., Dec.

16, 1930), or the wife may be called upon to rebut it (D'Agnese, id., Bronx Co., March 7, 1931).

The court may adjudge the husband in contempt for the amount which is admitted to be due and refer the issue as to the balance (Wodiska, id., N. Y. Co., Dec. 13, 1930), or give the wife the choice of accepting such a disposition or having the entire matter referred (Werth, id., Jan. 17, 1931).

On a defence of payment, where an issue arises as to the terms specified in the order or judgment, that construction will be given to them which is borne out by the actions of the parties; as where the husband was required to pay $20 per week at the rate of $40 bi-monthly, in which case the provision was interpreted to mean $960 per year, instead of $1040, where he had been paying the former amount and the wife had been accepting it, for six years (Mooney, 10 

Misc. 386, 31 Sup. 118, 1894). 

Indirect payment (IA2)

As a general rule, payment must be made in the manner specified by the order or judgment. If the husband is required to forward the instalments to the office of the wife's attorney, he does not comply by making the payments directly to the wife, and they are not, therefore, applicable to alimony. The explanation is, at least in separation actions, that the remittances may be held with some reason to be in addition to the alimony awarded and independently of it, particularly where the allowance is for or includes support for children.

The husband would be under the burden of proving that the payments were made by him, and accepted by the wife, as alimony. This might be shown by the nature and status of the proceedings had, as well as the amounts of the re-

ENFORCEMENT: CONTEMPT 

137 mittances and the intervals at which they were made. If the judgment is divorce, for example, and there are no children, and the husband forwards the money in the sums and at the times required, but defaults only in failing to send it to the proper place, it would be clear enough that it was sent as alimony; and the wife would be taken to have accepted it as such. 

In actual practice, however, the courts are constrained to uphold indirect payments as being made in pursuance of the order or judgment. This is especially true where the manner of payment which is pursued tends to carry out the court's wishes as to the disposition of the fund. Thus, where the judgment directed the husband to pay twenty dollars per week for the wife and child, the wife to pay the boy's car fares to school, and the wife failed to do so, the husband was permitted to deduct from his remittances to the wife a sum sufficient to pay for that item and send it directly to the boy (Brill, 148 A.D. 63, 131 Sup. 1030, 1911). 

Provision in lieu of alimony (IAS)

A more contentious question is whether the husband. may provide for the wife's support as an alternative to paying the alimony, to be distinguished from paying it indirectly, as set forth in the preceding paragraphs. On the one hand, it may be said that supporting the wife is clearly not an observance of the court's direction, whether it is done by advancing monies to or purchasing necessaries for her, because it is obviously designed to take the place of the alimony. On the other hand, it serves to discharge the very purpose of the alimony-viz., to maintain the wife, and her consent and acceptance should add the needed sanction.

It would seem that judges hold to the latter view. Thus,

they were disposed to deny the wife's motion where the husband had supported one of the two children (Barrett, 221 A.D. 710, 225 Sup. 174, 1927); where he paid a judgment obtained against the wife for necessaries (Steinthal, L. J., N. Y. Co., Dec. 19, 1930); where the parties had resumed marital relations and he had supported the wife (Seligman, id., Nov. 14, 1930); where he had continued making payments under the order of the Family Court (Backrack, id., Kings Co., Dec. 1, 1930). In such a case, however, the husband will merely be given credit for the amount which he paid, as where he made the payments pursuant to a separation agreement (Hoffstaedter, id., N. Y. Co., May 7, 1931), and will be admonished to thereafter adhere to the terms of the order or judgment (Slavatsky, id., Kings Co., March 17, 1931). See Schatzberg, 229 A.D. 214, 241 Sup. 684, 1930, appeal dismissed 225 

N. Y. 635.

Void mandate (IA4) 

The husband may raise the question of the validity of the judgment or order on a contempt motion. He may show that the mandate is a nullity for want of jurisdiction, or for want of power in the court to allow alimony because of the absence of personal service. He is not obliged to move to set the proceedings aside.

The same is true if his objection is that the order makes an improper requirement. The motion will be denied where the fund in payment of which the husband had defaulted was made payable to the attorney, instead of at his office (Noland, 29 Hun, 630, 1883; Quinn, 133 Misc. 266, 231 Sup. 329, 1928). But the motion was granted where the counsel fee was provided by the judgment and was allowed after trial, application for it having been properly made; and it did not matter that the remedy of execution

ENFORCEMENT: CONTEMPT 

139 was provided to enforce the award (Mercer, 73 Hun, 192, 25 Sup. 867, 1893). To the extent that an improper requirement may be corrected by a motion to modify, this class of defences may more properly belong in the dilatory group (II18, post). 

Wife's laches (IB5)

The motion may be denied for the wife's unreasonable delay in bringing it on or in serving the order or judgment (Ostrin vs Posner, 127 Misc. 313, 215 Sup. 259, 1925; 

Harris, L. J., N. Y. Co., Dec. 10, 1930). But laches is no defence if the wife has had to make motions to punish on several prior occasions, and it may never defeat the remedy in so far as support for children is concerned (Tooker, 137 

Misc. 158, 241 Sup. 623, 1930). 

Wife's waiver (IB6)

This is to be taken in conjunction with the cases cited under the heading of "Provision in lieu of alimony" (IA3, ante), more particularly, those in which the wife accepted the provision made by the husband. It is often difficult to distinguish between cases of that class and of the one under present discussion, because both possess common features. 

It is important, however, to draw the line between them, because in cases where the husband's conduct is in question -whether he paid or not-the issue is the arrearage itself; but where it is the wife's conduct-whether the default is justified—the arrearage is conceded. The importance of this distinction, in turn, will be taken up in the discussion on the effect of the denial of a contempt motion (VC, post).

Waiver is the wife's acquiescence in the husband's default, to the extent of relieving him of liability for punishment for it. The waiver is of the right to the remedy of 1

contempt, not to the alimony due or other remedies of enforcement. It comes about when the wife has accepted lesser sums than those required by the court's mandate, or accepted provision for her support in lieu of alimony, or tolerated the husband's total default, for so long a period of time or under such circumstances in general as to show that she is satisfied with the situation (Goodsell, 94 A.D. 

443, 88 Sup. 161, 1904; Thompson, 197 A.D. 228, 188 Sup. 

785, 1921; Knapp, 196 Sup. 235, 1922; Sax, 130 Misc. 696, 224 Sup. 634, 1927; Goodman, L. J., Kings Co., Dec. 23, 1930).

Thus, in every case of provision made in lieu of alimony, the husband may urge the further defence of waiver if the wife accepted the payments or consented to the expenditures by the husband. It is possible, therefore, where the court refuses to hold that there has been payment, sufficient to discharge the alleged arrears, to make out a case of waiver, not as substantial a defence in ultimate effect but just as efficacious to hold off commitment for the time being.

In one case, it was held that expenditures made by the former husband for the wife's support, following a judgment of divorce, and accompanied by their resumption of marital relations, constituted a provision for the wife in lieu of alimony to the extent of effecting a modification of the decree and discharging his obligation arising thereunder (Brodsky, L. J., Kings Co., Dec. 30, 1930). Passing by the question whether a sufficient consideration was paid to support a settlement, the facts are worth noting because they show how closely knit are the various objections to contempt motions available to a husband who concedes a literal default, and what extreme caution, in consequence, must be exercised to recognize and identify them. The facts of the case could make out an indirect

ENFORCEMENT: CONTEMPT 

141 payment if it could be shown that the parties treated the expenditures as payments of alimony; or a provision in lieu of alimony, if they did not; or a waiver.

If it should be determined that the expenditures constituted an indirect payment, they could not possibly predicate a modification, since the judgment would be thereby affirmed. If, on the other hand, they are construed as a waiver, they may have that effect. This is therefore an instance when the husband might be benefited ultimately by a determination that the transaction was a waiver instead of a payment-i. e., that he was in arrears.

The principle, finally, which underlies the denial of a contempt motion for the wife's waiver is an equitable one. 

It is that the wife has forfeited her right to so drastic a remedy as the imprisonment of the husband when it appears that she has condoned his delinquency or encouraged him in it. Her rights have been possibly prejudiced but she cannot pretend, thereby, to be aggrieved. 

Wife's bad faith (IB7)

The motion will be denied if the wife has made it impossible or difficult for the husband to pay the alimony, as where she interfered with his employment (Kunze, 53 

Sup. 938, 1898).

It may be denied if it appears that she is herself in default for disobedience of the court's direction. Her refusal to permit the husband to see the child is not such a disobedience unless it is by the terms of the order or judgment made a condition to withholding from the wife relief by contempt (Schweig, 112 A.D. 787, 107 Sup. 905, 1907; 

Fleming, L. J., Queens Co., Nov. 26, 1930).

Her unreasonable delay in prosecuting the action is a defence, as where she failed to notice it for trial for five

years (Sax, 130 Misc. 696, 224 Sup. 634, 1927); but not if the husband's default left her without means to defray legal expenses (Lynch, L. J., N. Y. Co., Jan. 23, 1931). 

Wife's misconduct (IB8)

The wife's misconduct such as would constitute a defence to her action, or would warrant a modification of the order, may defeat the motion to punish (Cotter, L. J., N. 

Y. Co., Jan. 21, 1931). In one case, however, the motion and the husband's application to strike out the allowance for the wife's adultery were both granted (Ronan, 32 Misc. 

467, 66 Sup. 799, 1900).

Reconciliation (IB9) The parties' reconciliation is a defence to the motion (Rosenblatt, L. J., N. Y. Co., Dec. 18, 1930; Luxenberg, id., Feb. 2, 1931). For a complete statement, see Lawrence, 202 A. D. 844, 194 Sup. 951, 1922. 

Disposition of the cause (IB10)

When the action is determined in the husband's favor, either in the granting to him of affirmative judgment, or the dismissal of the wife's complaint, temporary arrears are irrecoverable by contempt, even though no formal judgment or order of modification has been entered. The allowance continues, but in the light of the developments, and in the exercise of the court's discretion, the remedy is refused. Thus, the motion was denied on the granting to the husband of an interlocutory decree (Calvin, L. J., Kings Co., Feb. 11, 1931). It must appear, however, that the husband has paid regularly prior thereto (Lunenfeld, 137 Misc. 419, 242 Sup. 279, 1930).

ENFORCEMENT: CONTEMPT 

143 Divorce after separation (IB11) Where the allowance is provided by a separation judgment, and a divorce judgment follows, the motion may be held up to permit the husband to apply to modify the first decree by striking out the alimony provision (Gibson, 81 

Misc. 508, 143 Sup. 37, 1913).

The motion was granted with respect to arrears of alimony under a separation judgment which had been superseded by a divorce judgment, the motion having been made after the divorce judgment (Scheinwald, App. Div., 2nd Dep't., Nov. 1930). 

Wife's re-marriage (IB12)

It would seem that arrears accruing after the wife's remarriage (following a divorce judgment in her favor) are unenforceable by contempt, but not support for children (Tooker, 137 Misc. 158, 241 Sup. 623, 1930).

Ineffectiveness of an adjudication (IB13) 

Under the familiar principle that equity will not do a useless thing, the motion may be denied if the husband is beyond the reach of the court (Wulff, 151 A.D. 22, 135 

Sup. 289, 1912).

Husband's financial responsibility (IB14) The husband's possession of sufficient and available assets to satisfy the arrears is a bar to the motion (Rahl, 14 N. Y. W’kly Dig. 560, 1882; Conklin, 125 A.D. 280, 109 

Sup. 189, 1908). 

Husband's inability to comply (IB15)

Formerly the rule was that the husband's poverty was not a defence to the motion (Ryckman, 34 Hun, 235, aff'd 

98 N. Y. 639, 1884; Delanoy, 19 A.D. 295, 46 Sup. 106, 1897; Young, 35 Misc. 335, 71 Sup. 944, 1901; Compton,

125 A.D. 859, 110 Sup. 775, 1908; Cahzin, 112 Sup. 525, 1908). The explanation for the rule was that the granting of the allowance was not only a determination that it was proper but that it was within the husband's means. 

Both matters were res adjudicata and could not thereafter be raised by him collaterally. He could apply for a reduction, of course, on a showing of change of circumstances, but until one was had it was deemed that he was continuously ably to comply, and his default became, as a result, wilful and contumacious.

Gradually the rule was relaxed, and the courts began to accept the husband's capacity for compliance as a consideration in deciding the motion (Strobridge, 21 Hun, 288, 1880; Noland, 29 Hun, 630, 1883; Holtham, 6 Misc. 

266, 26 Sup. 762, 1893; Burdick, 183 A.D. 488, 171 Sup. 

247, 1918). 

Today, the tendency, with rare exceptions (Abrams, L. J., Bronx Co., March 20, 1931) is to deny the motion for the husband's poverty or inability to make the payments, if it appears that he has complied to the limit of his ability (Mesch, L. J., Bronx Co., Dec. 1, 1930; Sokol, id., Dec. 30, 1930; Leal, id., Kings Co., Jan. 2, 1931; Murray, id., Jan. 6, 1931; Clare, id., Bronx Co., Jan. 20, 1931; 

Bieczynski, id., Kings Co., Feb. 24, 1931; Friedman, id., Queens Co., March 31, 1931; Green, id., Kings Co., May 

11, 1931).

In line with this trend, the motion was denied as to arrears accruing during the period of the husband's incarceration for a prior default (Haber, id., Bronx Co., May 

15, 1931). 

Dilatory defences—availability of defences in general

Dilatory defences are taken up in the discussion on the procedure incidental to contempt motions.

ENFORCEMENT: CONTEMPT 

145 It should be noted that of the defences discussed in the foregoing, all but the first four (No arrears) are peculiar to contempt proceedings, with exceptions and variations as specifically noted.

C. Effect of a denial of the motion-status of arrears and of the right to apply for a reduction The importance of determining whether a defence which is urged and sustained on a motion to punish is dilatory or on the merits, and if on the merits whether it goes to the arrears or the propriety of contempt, becomes manifest when the question is the effect of the denial of the motion. If the objection is dilatory, the denial is without prejudice. A renewal is in order and the court usually makes that specification (Boyd, L. J., N. Y. 

Co., Nov. 13, 1930).

If the denial is on the merits and on the ground that no arrears are found to be due, the wife is forever precluded from taking further steps for their recovery, by contempt, sequestration, execution or otherwise. If it is based upon the particular ground that the order or judgment makes an improper requirement the rule is subject to the exception, as already noted, that the motion is renewable to the extent that the order or judgment may be modified.

If the denial is on the merits but for impropriety of contempt, a different situation arises. If the defence which is upheld is the wife's acts or conduct (Nos. 5-10, outline) the only effect is to bar her from further contempt proceedings for the recovery of the same arrears. 

She may proceed by other remedies, including equitable ones, even though she had been refused relief in the first instance for equitable reasons. This is because the relief

which is sought in the subsequent proceeding is purely to collect the debt, not punishment of the husband. 

Nevertheless, it would not be an abuse of discretion on the part of the court to deny her subsequent application, e. g., to sequester, for security, etc.,-because of the previous denial of the contempt motion. The prior ruling would serve to afford a precedent on the length to which the court could go without exceeding its prerogative.
