In no event, however, does the denial of the motion preclude legal remedies. Thus, the wife was permitted to recover the arrears by execution after contempt had been denied for her laches (Ostrin vs Posner, 127 Misc. 

313, 215 Sup. 259, 1925).

If the denial, finally, is for impropriety of contempt, but for one or more of the reasons other than the wife's acts or conduct (Nos. 10-15, outline), the wife may renew the motion on a showing that the conditions or circumstances which constituted the objection have changed. 

She could come in again if the disposition of the cause in the husband's favor had been set aside by reversal or otherwise; or her re-marriage had been annulled; or the husband had returned to the jurisdiction; or he had become divested of his available property; or he was no longer impecunious and his default in view of the same was wilful.

It will be found occasionally that more than one defence is sustained. In such a case, the final effect of the denial will depend upon that one of the defences which most substantially impairs the wife's rights. Thus, if there are three objections urged and upheld, as follows-prematurity of the motion, laches and indirect payment—, the result, obviously, is that the wife is thereafter precluded from recovering the alleged arrears by any remedy

ENFORCEMENT: CONTEMPT 

147 at all, equitable or legal. See Goodsell, 94 A.D. 443, 88 

Sup. 161, 1904; Knapp, 196 Sup. 235, 1922; Sax, 130 

Misc. 696, 224 Sup. 634, 1927.

The foregoing discussion has been confined to the effect of a denial on the wife's position. Turning to its effect on the husband's status, more particularly on his right to request a reduction, it seems that a different situation prevails from that of earlier times. 

Under the rule that the husband's default for non-payment of alimony barred him from affirmative relief, he was refused a modification of the allowance (Bortner, L. 

J., Kings Co., April 25, 1931; Simon, id., Lillienfeld, id., N. Y. Co., May 19, 1931). No distinction was made between cases of ordinary defaults and formal adjudications in contempt.

It followed, as a result, that when the motion was denied on the ground that no alimony was due, the husband was free to apply for a reduction, and his cross-motion made for that purpose was in order. His motion was entertained even if the denial of the contempt motion was for equitable reasons, on the theory that this ruling was in effect a determination that his default was excusable. Thus, the wife's motion was denied for the husband's poverty and his cross-motion to reduce granted (Burdick, 183 A.D. 488, 171 Sup. 247, 1918).

The wife, therefore, exposed herself to the hazard of having the husband's disqualification removed by bringing the contempt motion. Ordinarily, if she did nothing and the husband moved for a reduction, it would have to be denied for his arrears, regardless whether the default was justifiable or not. This feature of the situation has now been eliminated by a drastic change in the attitude of the courts. Motions to reduce are being granted not only where the default is excused (Friedman, L. J., Queens Co.,

March 31, 1931), but where it is condemned by an adjudication in contempt, if the husband shows that he cannot further comply with the order or judgment (Wilson, id., N. Y. Co., Jan. 2, 1931; Alleyne, id., Jan. 7, 1931; Tauber, id., Bronx Co., March 11, 1931; Ribler, id., March 28, 1931; Minnix, id., Kings Co., May 6, 1931), and while serving a sentence for contempt (Bail, id., N. Y. Co., Feb. 

14, 1931; Morris, id., Kings Co., Dec. 10, 1930). The reduction may be granted without prejudice to previous contempt proceedings (Schmeidt, id., Bronx Co., April 4, 1931); and if the motion is denied for irregularity, etc., the adjudication in contempt may be declared to be without prejudice to it (Goldman, id., N. Y. Co., Jan. 17, 1931).

The husband's motion may be granted on condition that he give a bond to secure the arrears as an alternative to adjudicating him (Bunce vs Willard, id., Kings Co., Dec. 

31, 1930).

This change in the policy of the courts, at least as far as the First Department is concerned, is formally affirmed in Jacobs, 138 Misc. 590, 245 Sup. 338, 1930. The statement is there made that the rule precluding the husband from relief by modification if he is in default has been relaxed when it is impossible for him to comply. The intimation is that the inability to comply would have to be severe enough to constitute a good defence to a motion to punish. The case cites as an authority Staples, 206 A.D. 

196, 200 Sup. 583, 1923, but there the point is that poverty is a ground for release from imprisonment. 

This is a complete departure from the position which has traditionally characterized the courts in their dealings with delinquent husbands, and comes about concurrently with the liberalizing of their views as to the propriety of poverty defences on contempt motions. It has not won, however, complete unanimity. There are judges

ENFORCEMENT: CONTEMPT 

149 who still adhere to the old rule and limit the husband's claim of poverty to contempt motions (Cole, L. J., N. Y. 

Co., Jan. 29, 1931).

When the husband's claim of inability to comply as an objection to the contempt motion, and of change of circumstances as a ground for a reduction, is controverted and there are questions of fact, the practice is to refer the whole matter (Jacobs, id., Bronx Co., Nov. 13, 1930; 

Visic, id., N. Y. Co., Dec. 30, 1930; Leal, id., Kings Co., Jan. 2, 1931).

In conclusion, it may be said that the determining considerations on the motion to reduce are the same whether it is made alone or counter to the contempt motion: except, that if it is made alone, the court may not go into such equitable circumstances as would defeat a motion to punish for impropriety of contempt, viz., the wife's laches, waiver, bad faith, etc., the reason being that they are strictly collateral to the issue. 

D. Procedure 

When motion may be made

The motion may be deferred if other proceedings are pending which make such a course advisable, such as a reference on a motion to modify the allowance (Goodsell, 94 

A.D. 443, 88 Sup. 161, 1904), or the fact that there is already an outstanding order of commitment for another default (Russell, L. J., N. Y. Co., Feb. 21, 1931) or that the husband is serving a sentence for contempt (Mesch, id., Bronx Co., Dec. 1, 1930). The court may permit a renewal after the other order has been executed or otherwise disposed of (Russell, supra), or towards the close of the term of imprisonment (Lynch, id., N. Y. Co., Dec. 10, 1930); or grant the motion and direct that payments need

not be made until after the husband's release (Morris, id., Kings Co., Dec. 10, 1930). The motion is not barred, however, by the mere existence of another adjudication in contempt (Scheinwald, App. Div., 2nd Dep't., Nov. 1930).

In this connection, it may be noted that the motion may be denied because of the imminence of trial (Schatts, id., Kings Co., Jan. 23, 1931). The propriety of such a course, however, would seem to be supportable on the theory of laches-i.e., the fact that the wife withheld making the motion until the last minute (VB, IB5, ante)—, rather than the prospect of an early disposition of the cause. 

Moving papers

The papers in support of the motion to punish consist of an order to show cause, an affidavit by the wife, an affidavit or other proof of service of the order or judgment and a copy of the order or judgment. 

The moving papers, as well as the subsequent proceedings by the wife, should disclose, in a word, the necessity of invoking the remedy of contempt, i.e., that payment is unenforceable by other remedies (Sandford, 44 Hun, 563, 12 Civ. Pro. Rep. 183, 1887). 

The order to show cause

At a Special Term, Part 2, of the Supreme 

Court of the State of New York, held in and for the County of New York, at the County 

Courthouse, in the Borough of Manhattan, City of New York, on 25th day of June, 1931

JUSTICE MARY SMITH

Plaintiff Defendant ..

On reading and filing the annexed affidavits of Mary Smith, duly sworn to the 20th day of June, 1931, and of Henry Jones, duly sworn to the 20th day of May, 1931, showing due service upon the defendant of the order awarding temporary alimony and counsel fees herein (or, the judgment of divorce, or separation), and on all the other pleadings and proceedings heretofore had herein, and it appearing to the satisfaction of the Court that the defendant has given no security and that sequestration proceedings have not been instituted because they would be ineffectual and that the rights and remedies of the plaintiff have been impaired, impeded, prejudiced and defeated, and on motion of Frank Graham, Esq., attorney for the plaintiff, LET the defendant, William Smith, or his attorney, show cause before this Court, at a Special Term, Part I thereof, at the Courthouse, in the Borough of Manhattan, City of New 

York, on the 28th day of June, 1931, at 10 o'clock in the forenoon on said day or as soon thereafter as counsel can be heard, why an order should not be made and entered herein adjudging the defendant to be in contempt of Court for his failure to pay the temporary alimony and counsel fees awarded to the plaintiff herein and punishing him therefor, and for such other and further relief as may be just; and it is further 

ORDERED that service of a certified copy of this order, together with copies of the papers upon which it is granted

made upon the defendant or his attorney on or before the 26th day of June, 1931, be deemed due and sufficient notice. J.S.C. 

The order to show cause is the only way of instituting the proceeding, even though the order or judgment makes a different specification (Stahl, 59 Hun, 621, 12 Sup. 854, 1891). An ex parte order of commitment is absolutely void (Stewart, 127 A.D. 724, 111 Sup. 734, 1908). The suggestion that once the court determines that neither security nor sequestration is availing that commitment may be had without notice (Isaacs, 61 How. Pr. 369, 1881) is not to be followed.

Enter The order to show cause must be a court order (Weich, 59 Misc. 238, 110 Sup. 201, 1908; Quinn, 133 Misc. 266, 231 Sup. 329, 1928). But a judge's order is valid if the judge who signed it was then holding a term of the court and was authorized to sign court orders (Aiken, 96 Misc. 

561, 160 Sup. 876, 1916).

The requirement that the order to show cause recite that payment is unenforceable by resort to security or sequestration, and that the default caused the wife damage is absolute and unqualified (Goldman, 103 Misc. 

700, 171 Sup. 26, 1918; Mahan, 50 Sup. Ct. (J & S) 92, 1884). 

Wife's affidavit (caption) 

STATE OF NEW YORK) COUNTY OF NEW YORKS 

SS: 

MARY SMITH, being duly sworn, deposes and says: I am the plaintiff herein. On May 18, 1931, there was entered in this action an order directing the defendant to pay me, as temporary alimony for the support of our two infant children and

ENFORCEMENT: CONTEMPT 

153 me, the sum of Thirty dollars every week in advance, payments to begin May 15, 1931; and One hundred and fifty dollars as counsel fees, one-half to be paid within ten days from the date of service upon the defendant or his attorney of a copy of the said order, all payments to be made at the office of my attorney.

Thereafter and on May 20, 1931, a certified copy of the said order was personally served upon the defendant, as appears from the accompanying affidavit of Henry Jones. 

The defendant has failed to make any payments under the said order, and he is now in default under the same for nonpayment of the sum of One hundred and twenty dollars for alimony, for the period of four weeks beginning May 15, 1931 and ending June 11, 1931; and Seventy-five dollars counsel fees, making a total of One hundred and ninety-five ($195.00) dollars.

The defendant has not given security, nor have I instituted sequestration proceedings because I do not know of any property belonging to the defendant which would be available. 

His failure to pay the alimony and counsel fees as aforesaid was calculated to and actually did impair, impede, prejudice and defeat my rights and remedies herein.

I have made no previous application for this relief. 

Sworn to before me this 

20th day of June, 1931

The affidavit must recite non-payment. The husband's admission is insufficient (Matzke, 185 A.D. 533, 173 Sup. 

244, 1918).

It must state facts to satisfy the court that payment is unenforceable by resort to security or sequestration (Uttal, 140 A.D. 255, 125 Sup. 2, 1910); but it is not necessary that there be actual resort to either (Taliaferro, 171 Sup. 25, 1918). If sequestration proceedings are actually pending, the motion, of course, must be denied (Bishop, 210 A.D. 226, 205 Sup. 539, 1924).

These allegations were held to be sufficient-on information and belief that the husband had no property unless he had saved his earnings, and the wife had no knowledge

as to that; and that his earnings were not available to sequestration (Rahl, 14 N. Y. W'kly Dig. 560, 1882).

This allegation was held to be insufficient-that the husband had no bank account or business of his own (Haas, 197 A.D. 619, 189 Sup. 494, 1921).

An affidavit by the attorney is insufficient (Stillman, L. 

J., Kings Co., March 23, 1931). It must be an original deposition (Taliaferro, supra).

No prior demand need be made and none need be stated in the affidavit, now that the Civil Practice Act has superseded the Judiciary Law in this respect. The old cases (Delanoy, 19 A.D. 295, 46 Sup. 106, 1897; Goldie, 77 A.D. 

12, 79 Sup. 268, 1902; Kalmanowitz, 108 A.D. 296, 95 

Sup. 627, 1905; Vogel, 146 A.D. 928, 131 Sup. 67, 1911; 

Reich, 167 Sup. 660, 1917) are still authorities for the principle that the motion must be brought at the wife's instance, not, for example, at that of her attorney. 

Proof of service of the order or judgment A certified copy of the order or judgment awarding the alimony must be personally served upon the husband before he may be punished for contempt for non-payment (Ryckman, 32 Hun, 193, 1884; Dikeman, 108 Misc. 406, 177 Sup. 506, 1919; Feit, 224 A.D. 857, 231 Sup. 740, 1928; Tucci, 230 A.D. 737, 243 Sup. 473, 1930; Glicker, L. J., N. Y. Co., Dec. 16, 1930; Cohen, id., Bronx Co., March 5, 1931; Stillman, id., Kings Co., March 23, 1931; 

Coniglio, id., April 28, 1931).

The defect is not cured by the husband's failure to oppose the contempt motion (Musolino, id., Feb. 6, 1931).

Of late a disposition has manifested itself in the decisions to overlook this requirement if the husband knew of the existence of the order or judgment and was famil-

ENFORCEMENT: CONTEMPT 

155 iar with its terms (Lawrence, 202 A. D. 844, 194 Sup. 951, 1922; McLane, L. J., N. Y. Co., Nov. 26, 1930; Schwartz, id., Bronx Co., Feb. 10, 1931), particularly when he has been making payments under it (Gold, id., N. Y. 

Co., Dec. 11, 1930) or has taken steps to modify the allowance (Wallach, 138 Misc. 5, 245 Sup. 137, 1930). The view finds support in an early decision (Davis, 83 Hun, 500, 32 Sup. 10, 1895).

Thus, the objection that the paper was served on Sunday was disregarded where the husband had taken steps in opposition to the order (Silverman, L. J., Kings Co., March 2, 1931).

This view of the matter would seem to be justified by Section 1172 itself. It makes no specification for personal service, as pointed out in the dissenting opinion in the Tucci case, supra; but the weight of authority seems to be against it.

Service may be made outside the state (Davis, supra; 

Woolworth, 115 A.D. 405, 100 Sup. 865, 1906). 

An order reducing the allowance must be personally served and by certified copy (Flor, 73 A.D. 262, 76 Sup. 

813, 1902). It would seem, however, that the husband's application to modify constitutes a waiver of such service (Paley, L. J., Kings Co., Jan. 24, 1931; Wallach, Silverman, supra). 

An order resettling the order or judgment granting the allowance is a mere re-statement and need not be served in that manner (Horter, 177 A.D. 827, 164 Sup. 889, 1917).

Service of a short-form order is insufficient unless it is clear and definite as to terms (Glicker, supra). 

The question of service may be referred (Homer, L. J., Kings Co., Nov. 13, 1930).

The husband may be punished for all arrears due at the time of service (Gunn, 120 A.D. 353, 105 Sup. 340, 1907).

But see Goldfarb, L. J., Kings Co., March 19, 1931; Schlotter, id., April 30, 1931.

The requirement of personal service, etc., is applicable only to contempt proceedings, not to other remedies for the enforcement of alimony (Knott, 6 A.D. 589, 39 Sup. 804, 1896). 

Service need not be made by one having personal knowledge of the husband, as in the case of the summons in a matrimonial action. 

Copy of the order or judgment

This saves the court the time of examining the original papers, in passing on the motion. It does not obviate, however, the necessity of requisitioning the file when the order to show cause is submitted for signature, the procedure customarily followed among the various ex parte terms. 

Service of the order to show cause

The order to show cause may be served upon the husband's attorney if the action is pending (Zimmerman, 14 

Sup. 444, 1891; Weich, 59 Misc. 238, 110 Sup. 201, 1908; 

Carr, 64 Misc. 435, 118 Sup. 625, 1909; Rosenthal, 201 

A.D. 27, 193 Sup. 702, 1922), in distinction from the order or judgment awarding the alimony (Grant vs Greene, 121 

A.D. 756, 106 Sup. 532, 1907).

It must be served upon the husband after judgment, however, because the relationship of attorney and client has ceased (Wulff, 151 A.D. 22, 135 Sup. 289, 1912), and even pending the action if the attorney has ceased representing the husband (Schwartz, L. J., Bronx Co., Feb. 

10, 1931). Cases contra (Winton vs Livey, 16 Civ. Pro. 

Rep. 348, 53 Hun 630, 6 Sup. 29, 1889) are not to be followed.

ENFORCEMENT: CONTEMPT 

157 In any event, if the order to show cause provides that it is to be served personally, it must be served in that manner (Stone, id., N. Y. Co., Jan. 19, 1931). 

Service must be within the state (Harney, 110 A.D. 20, 96 Sup. 905, 1905), in distinction from the order or judgment awarding the alimony. In Woolworth, 115 A.D. 

405, 100 Sup. 865, 1906, the court made no criticism of a service of the order to show cause outside the state, but it appears that service had been effected upon the attorney here as well.

As in the case of the order or judgment, service may be made by an ordinary process server.

THE ADJUDICATION 

(1) The order

The plaintiff having moved this Court by an order to show cause granted the 25th day of June, 1931, for an order adjudging the defendant to be in contempt with the provisions of an order entered herein on May 18, 1931, directing the defendant to pay to the plaintiff herein the sum of Thirty dollars weekly for the support and maintenance of herself and the infant children of the parties hereto, and One hundred and fifty dollars counsel fees, one-half to be paid within ten days after service upon the defendant or his attorney of a copy of the said order, and punishing the defendant for his disobedience of the provisions of the said order, and said motion having regularly come on to be heard before this Court on the 28th day of 

June, 1931; 

NOW, upon reading and filing the said order to show cause dated the 25th day of June, 1931, together with the papers upon which it was granted consisting of the affidavit of Mary 

Smith, duly sworn to the 20th day of June, 1931, the affidavit of Henry Jones, duly sworn to the 20th day of May, 1931, showing due service of the order granted on the 18th day of May, 1931, and a copy of the said order annexed thereto, all submitted in support of the said motion; and the affidavit of William Smith, sworn to the 27th day of June, 1931, submitted in opposition thereto; and after hearing respective counsel for and against the motion, and due deliberation having been had, and on motion of Frank Graham, Esq., attorney for the plaintiff, and on filing the opinion of the Court, it is 

ORDERED, ADJUDGED and DECREED that the defendant William Smith is guilty of contempt of this Court by reason of his failure to comply with the provisions of the said order granted on the 18th day of May, 1931, which required the defendant to pay to the plaintiff herein the sum of Thirty dollars per week for the support and maintenance of herself and the infant children of the said parties, and One hundred and fifty dollars counsel fees, one-half to be paid within ten days after service upon the defendant or his attorney of a copy of the said order, in that the defendant has failed and refused to pay the alimony to the plaintiff, or the counsel fee, being in default in both in the total sum of One hundred and ninetyfive ($195.00) dollars, and the said contempt of the defendant in such refusal to obey said order was calculated to and actually did impair, impede, prejudice and defeat the rights and

ENFORCEMENT: CONTEMPT 

159 remedies of the plaintiff, to her loss in the sum of One hundred and ninety-five ($195.00) dollars; and it is further 

ORDERED, ADJUDGED and DECREED that for such disobedience and disregard of the provisions of said order and for the contempt of this Court, as aforesaid, the defendant is fined the sum of One hundred and ninety-five ($195.00) dollars; and it is further 

ORDERED, ADJUDGED and DECREED that the defendant may purge himself of the said contempt of court by paying the fine so imposed to the plaintiff in the following manner: in regular weekly instalments of Five dollars each, commencing on July 5, 1931 and to be continued until the entire sum of One hundred and ninety-five ($195.00) dollars has been paid, said payments to be made at the office of plaintiff's attorney and to be in addition to the regular weekly instalments of Thirty dollars each; in the event that the defendant fails or neglects to make any of the payments as aforesaid, or any part thereof, upon the affidavit of the plaintiff showing due service of a copy of this order and of such non-compliance, and without further notice an order and warrant of commitment issue to the Sheriff of any county where the defendant may be found, directing said Sheriff to detain the said defendant in close custody in the County Jail of said county, until he shall have paid the entire fine so imposed, or any part of said fine which remains unpaid at the time of such commitment, or until he shall have otherwise been discharged according to law.

Enter 

J. S. C. (2) Purge provisions

This is called the "punish-purge" order. It is the usual one to be made in these cases, particularly if there is doubt as to the wilfulness of the husband's default (Taormina, #2, App. Div., 2nd Dept., Jan. 1931).

Leave to purge may be granted even where the husband fails to oppose the motion (McDonough, L. J., N. Y. Co., Nov. 28, 1930; Apotheker, id., Feb. 4, 1931), or the order

may provide for settlement on notice to him (Smith, id., Bronx Co., March 14, 1931).

It should be noted that the purge provision is virtually a modification of the order or judgment granting the allowance, in so far as the manner of paying the amount found to be due is concerned. 

The order may not provide that commitment issue on the husband's failure to pay current alimony. The reason is that such a course would be a substitute for a regular contempt proceeding, to which the husband is entitled by the statute. It becomes important, therefore, to determine whether payments which are made following the entry of the order must be applied by the wife towards the arrears. It would seem that she is not obliged to do so, and that remittances forwarded without any accompanying specification by the husband will not serve to pay the fine. If the husband desires to confine the payments to the accrual as determined by the order he must describe them as such. The situation is comparable to that which obtains when the husband makes payments of alimony in a manner other than that provided in the order or judgment (VB, IA2, ante). Here, as there, the wife has the choice of treating the instalments as being paid for a purpose different from the observance of the order; and, exactly as in those cases, the amounts and intervals of the payments may indicate their purpose so clearly as to charge the wife with both knowledge of that purpose and the acceptance of them as such.

Separate purge provisions may be made for counsel fees (Bennett, L. J., N. Y. Co., Dec. 9, 1930; Cook, id., Kings 

Co., April 25, 1931).

The purge conditions which the court may impose are infinite. The husband may be given five days in which to

ENFORCEMENT: CONTEMPT 

161 ! pay the whole sum (Juretie, id., Kings Co., Nov. 21, 1930), or he may be required to pay it in three instalments (Landman, id., N. Y. Co., Nov. 20, 1930), or to pay part of it in five days and the balance in equal instalments (Adler, id., Kings Co., Feb. 3, 1931), or simply to pay the arrears concurrently with future alimony (Phillips, id., Jan. 15, 1931).

The parties may agree on purge terms, in person or by counsel (Eitelberg, id., N. Y. Co., Jan. 20, 1931). (8) Other provisions

The order of adjudication is not defective for its failure to recite unenforceability by security or sequestration if the fact appears from the moving papers or the opposing papers (Peo. ex rel Allen vs Wagner, 117 Misc. 526, 191 Sup. 697, 1921; Ryer, 33 Hun, 116, 1884). If the only showing of that fact, however, is the statement by the husband's attorney that his client is out of the jurisdiction, the order is defective (Whitney, 58 Sup. Ct. 335, 11 Sup. 

582, 1890). See also Swenarton vs Shupe, 40 Hun, 41, 1886; Fischer vs Langbein, 103 N. Y. 84, 1886; Cockefair, 23 Abb. (N. C.) 219, 7 Sup. 170, 1889; Sandford, 40 Hun, 540, 9 Civ. Pro. Rep. 289, 1886; Mendell, 4 N. Y. St. Rep. 

556, 1886.

An order which is defective for its failure to make the necessary recitals may be modified, at the wife's instance, to have them inserted (Krauss, 127 A.D. 743, 111 Sup. 

790, 1908).

It may be modified by the husband if the fine is excessive, as where it is more than the amount actually due (Donovan, 153 A. D. 883, 137 Sup. 1088, 1912). Modification is his only recourse, not, for example, an application to be discharged from imprisonment (Peo. ex rel Woolf vs Jacobs, 5 Hun, 428, 1875, aff'd 66 N. Y. 8, 1875).

(4) Service of the order

There is no requirement in the statute that the order of adjudication be served personally, or, indeed, that it be served at all, although it is usually specified in "punishpurge" orders as the preliminary to the payments. The order is in no sense a mandate calling for the performance by the husband of any act. It is merely a determination of certain rights, under circumstances when the husband must be aware of its terms, or his attorney if he was represented. In any event, the provisions of the order are a favor to him. In an extraordinary situation, possibly, when the husband appears personally to oppose the motion, and decision is reserved, and he is actually at no time apprised of the order, it might be better practice by the wife's attorney to serve him. (5) Non-purge order

The court may grant the motion outright, without leave to purge (Oginsky, L. J., N. Y. Co., Dec. 15, 1930). The order in such a case reads as follows: (Repeat all the paragraphs of the purge order, except the last; then proceed-) 

ORDERED, ADJUDGED and DECREED that upon a certified copy of this order and the payment of the Sheriff's legal fees, and without further process or notice, the defendant be committed by the Sheriff of any county in this state wherein the defendant may be found and to whom a certified copy of this order shall be delivered, to the common jail of said county, there to be kept and detained in close custody until he shall have paid the said sum of One hundred and ninety-five ($195.00) dollars, with the Sheriff's legal fees, or until he is discharged according to law.

Enter 

J. S. C.

163 "T 

E. Commitment 

The order 

If the order of adjudication is not a "punish-purge" order, the subsequent procedure consists, simply, in delivering a certified copy and one or two ordinary copies to the Sheriff, who then proceeds to take the husband into custody.

If the order does provide for purging, and the husband thereafter defaults in the payments, an order and warrant of commitment must be obtained.

The order of commitment, and the warrant, are obtained ex parte. The order reads as follows: PRESENT Hon.

It should be noted that the order must recite that the order of adjudication has been served upon the defendant only when the court makes that requirement in its decision, on the motion to punish. Otherwise, as has been stated, such service need not be made and need not be specified in either the order of adjudication, the order of commitment or any incidental papers.

The order of commitment need not state that the husband has no property available to sequestration if the prior proceedings disclose it (Distasio, 26 Misc. 491, 57

Issuance and execution of process, and effect of incarceration The order, the warrant and the affidavit should be submitted to the court under separate covers. The order remains on file, and a certified copy should be obtained. The warrant, being a judge's order, and the affidavit, are returned to counsel.

The papers are then delivered to the Sheriff for execution. They consist of the following—a certified copy of the order of commitment with two ordinary copies, the original warrant with two ordinary copies and the original affidavit with one or two copies. The Sheriff requires these because he must serve one set upon the husband, file another with the court with his return and keep another for his own records.

Sheriff's counsel usually examines the papers before accepting them, to see that they are complete and regular. 

When they have been filed with the clerk and the fees paid (running generally from seven to ten dollars in the metropolitan area), arrangements must be made for the identification of the husband. The Sheriff's province is a civil one and he declines to assume the responsibility of an arrest unless accompanied by some one having personal knowledge of the husband.

The Sheriff must act as promptly as the pressure of his duties permits. His unwarranted delay in executing the order may expose him to punishment by contempt (Fiorini, 122 Misc. 325, 203 Sup. 785, 1924). He may refuse, however, to go outside the territorial limits of his county. 

In such a case, when the husband cannot be apprehended there, it is necessary to withdraw the papers and refile them in the proper county.

Execution may be effected on any day but Sunday, at

ENFORCEMENT: CONTEMPT 

169 any time of the day or night (in distinction, for example, from a bailable attachment) and anywhere, including the premises of the courthouse.

When the husband is taken into custody, the arresting officer must serve him with a complete set of the commitment papers, and may give him a reasonable opportunity to raise the money. He may be permitted to make calls both in person and on the telephone, to notify his lawyer and to avail himself in general of all necessary means and facilities to the end that he may be legally released. He is not entitled to jail liberties on giving a bond (In re Clark, 20 Hun, 551, 1880).

He must be immediately released on payment of the fine with the Sheriff's fees. In this connection, it should be noted that although there is nothing in the statute prohibiting the re-imbursement to the wife of her fees to the Sheriff and for provisions in the order and warrant to that effect, and that it has been done in some cases, the practice is not generally followed. If it is allowed, however, it is added to the fine, and the husband must pay that as well before he may be discharged. This applies to motion costs, too.

He may pay the fine with the costs while in jail, and if he does, must be immediately discharged. Payment may be made either to the Sheriff or the Warden. If payment is not made, the husband must serve the term required by law, three months if the fine is less than five hundred dollars, six months if it is five hundred dollars or more (Judiciary Law, sec. 774, as amended by Laws, 1919, ch. 184). 

He is given no credit for the time that he is under technical restraint merely, as in custody of counsel. He must be actually within the prison walls (Peo. ex rel Clark vs 

Grant, 111 N. Y. 584, 1888, aff'g 47 Hun, 604).

Formerly, and before Section 1172 was made the ex-

clusive authority for matrimonial contempts, one imprisonment barred further ones in the same action (Reese, 46 

A.D. 156, 61 Sup. 760, 1899; Peo. ex rel Levine vs Shea, 201 N. Y. 471, 1911; Chadwick, 170 A.D. 328, 156 Sup. 190, 1915). The section has now changed the rule, even as to judgments rendered prior to the new enactment (Christiano, 204 A.D. 47, 197 Sup. 72, 1922). The application of the section to them does not impair the husband's rights because the provision merely enlarges the wife's remedial facilities. It makes no difference that the first imprisonment was for temporary alimony and the second for permanent (Roberts, 3 Bradb. 323, 1913).

Service of part or the full term does not, of course, discharge the arrears, but only precludes further contempt proceedings for their recovery. Thus, it does not bar the wife from docketing the arrears and reducing them to judgment (In re Donovan, 159 A.D. 228, 144 Sup. 280, 1913; Thayer, 145 A.D. 268, 129 Sup. 1035, 1911), or from moving for a stay (Maran, 137 A.D. 348, 122 Sup. 9, 1910). 

Husband's remedies

The husband may apply for his discharge, on notice, on a showing that he cannot endure the imprisonment. He must show that it is oppressive to him and that he is absolutely unable to pay the fine. The authority for the remedy is Section 775 of the Judiciary Law. 

When the court may release offender. Where an offender, imprisoned as prescribed in this article, is unable to endure the imprisonment, or to pay the sum, or perform the act or duty, required to be paid or performed, in order to entitle him to be released, the court, judge, or referee . . . may in its or his discretion, and upon such terms as justice requires, make an order, directing him to be discharged from imprisonment. 

See Ryckman, 34 Hun, 235, 1884, aff'd 98 N. Y. 639; 

Young, 35 Misc. 335, 71 Sup. 944, 1901; Cahzin, 112 Sup.

ENFORCEMENT: CONTEMPT 

171 1 

525, 1908; Staples, 206 A.D. 196, 200 Sup. 583, 1923; 

Lynch, L. J., N. Y. Co., Jan. 23, 1931.

The motion will not be entertained on any other ground than that specified in the section. Thus, the motion was denied where the alimony had been allowed on the husband's default and he claimed that the allowance was improper (Zuenino, L. J., Kings Co., April 3, 1931). His proper course was to move to open the default. 

Writs of habeas corpus are proper only when the objection to the imprisonment is that it is illegal, as that the court had no jurisdiction by reason of certain defects or irregularities in the proceedings (Peo. ex rel Woolf vs 

Jacobs, 5 Hun, 428, 1875, aff'd 66 N. Y. 8; Peo. ex rel 

Allen vs Wagner, 117 Misc. 526, 191 Sup. 697, 1921). The court will consider the motion favorably if the discharge will make it possible for the arrears to be paid, as where the husband's employer stated that they would be paid out of his salary if he was permitted to go back to work (Cagiano, L. J., Kings Co., Feb. 6, 1931).

The husband must satisfy the court as to his good faith. 

Thus, the motion was denied where he had re-married in another state within the prohibited time (Ryer, 33 Hun, 116, 1884). The court may impose as a condition subsequent, i.e., that he be re-committed for its non-fulfillment, that he discontinue the acts which constitute his lack of good faith, as where he was living in adultery with and supporting another woman (In re Ryckman, 39 Hun, 646, 1886).

In any event, it must appear that the circumstances of his default are to some degree excusable (Lansing, 41 

How. Pr. 248, 1871; Caragano, App. Div., 1st Dep't., December, 1931; Newfeld, L. J., Kings Co., April 30, 1931).

The discharge may be conditioned on his giving a bond to secure the payment of the arrears (Endor, id., N. Y. 

Co., Feb. 10, 1931), or on his paying them following the discharge with provision for re-commitment on his failure to do so, the arrangement being a suspension of the commitment process (Rosenfeld, id., Feb. 7, 1931).

F. Miscellaneous considerations Stays 

The motion to punish may ask for additional and alternative relief, including a stay (Moncrief, 155 Sup. 592, 1915).

The husband was denied a stay of the contempt proceedings to enable him to resume his residence in the state, when he had left the jurisdiction to avoid payment (Moore, 141 A.D. 533, 126 Sup. 413, 1910).

Re-arguments The wife was granted a re-argument following the determination of another case in which the ruling was favorable to her (Carr, 64 Misc. 435, 118 Sup. 625, 1909). Appeals

The granting or denial of a contempt motion is reviewable, but only on the ground of abuse of discretion. The right to appeal, however, is absolute (Ryer, 33 Hun, 116, 1884; Jeweller's etc., vs Rothchild, 155 N. Y. 255, 1898, dismissing appeal from 6 A.D. 499, 39 Sup. 700). The husband's right is not affected by his deliberate disobedience of the order (Moore, 141 A.D. 532, 126 Sup. 412, 1910).

ENFORCEMENT: CONTEMPT 

173 Costs 

Costs, other than motion costs in contempt, may not be enforced by contempt (Jacquin, 36 Hun, 378, 1885; Weill, 10 Sup. 627, 1890; Branth, 59 Hun, 623, 13 Sup. 360, 1891). The superseding of the original remedy of execution, in so far as costs are concerned, by the enactments which we now know as Section 1172 of the Civil Practice 

Act and Article 19 of the Judiciary Law, did not permit enforcement by contempt (Lansing, 4 Lans. 377, 1871). 

Annulment actions

Alimony in annulment actions is unenforceable under Section 1172. The section specifies divorce and separation actions, and the courts have been unwilling to extend its effect to annulment actions, as they have done with the sections providing for the allowance of temporary alimony, distinguishing, presumably, between remedial and substantive, or quasi-substantive, rights. The proceeding must therefore be brought under the Judiciary Law, and care must be taken to comply with its provisions. Thus, a formal demand must be made to accompany service of the order. In the absence of such a demand, the motion will be denied; and a demand by the wife's attorney is insufficient unless his authority for it is shown (Sutton, 145 

A.D. 845, 130 Sup. 368, 1911).

A. Security ENFORCEMENT: 

Other Remedies 

VI The nature of the remedy The provision for security is the only one among the remedies provided by the statute for the enforcement of alimony that may be availed of by the wife even though the husband is not in arrears. 

It may be had when it appears that the husband will default, or has failed to pay accrued alimony. Thus, it was refused where his social position and standing in the community were such as to make it unlikely that he would default (Rosenwasser, 117 Misc. 123, 190 Sup. 774, 1921).

The remedy should be distinguished from arrest under Section 827; but security was required where the husband intended to leave the jurisdiction (Ardery, L. J., Kings

Co., Feb. 24, 1931), and even where he had left the jurisdiction (Olney, id., April 22, 1931).

The husband may be required to secure arrears (Forrest, 9 Bosw. 686, 1863). It may be limited for a specified time, and for part of the sum awarded as alimony (Roberts vs 

Donahue, 221 A.D. 348, 222 Sup. 662, 1927, mod'd 246 

N. Y. 447).

It may not be had pending sequestration proceedings (Wright, 139 Misc. 423, 248 Sup. 317, 1931). 174

The statute

Section 1171. Security for payments by defendant in action for divorce or separation; sequestration. Where a judgment rendered or an order made in an action in this state for divorce or separation, or a judgment rendered in another state for divorce upon the ground of adultery, or for separation or separate support and maintenance for any of the causes specified in section eleven hundred and sixty-one of this act, upon which an action has been brought in this state and judgment rendered therein, requires a husband to provide for the education or maintenance of any of the children of a marriage, or for the support of his wife, the court, in its discretion, also may direct him to give reasonable security, in such a manner and within such a time as it thinks proper, for the payment, from time to time, of the sums of money required for that purpose. 

If he fails to give the security, or to make any payment required by the terms of such a judgment or order, whether he has or has not given security therefor, or to pay any sum of money for the expenses of the plaintiff or for her support and maintenance or the support and maintenance of the children during the pendency of the action which he is required to pay by an order, the court may cause his personal property and the rents and profits of his real property to be sequestered, and may appoint a receiver thereof. The rents and profits and other property so sequestered may be applied, from time to time, under the direction of the court, to the payment of any of the sums of money specified in this section, as justice requires; and if the same shall be insufficient to pay the sums of money required, the court, on application of the receiver, may direct the mortgage or sale by the receiver, under such terms and conditions as it may prescribe, of sufficient of his real estate to pay such sums. (Am. by Laws, 1921, ch. 369, in effect Oct. 1, 1921)

Procedure Security may be required in the allowance of alimony; as where the judgment directed the husband to give a mortgage of his property to his wife (Le Compte, 3 Bradb. 

588, 1913). If it is requested subsequently, the application must be on notice. It may be made on a motion to

punish (Rosen, 226 A. D. 327, 235 Sup. 169, 1929; D'Amico, L. J., Queens Co., March 12, 1931).

The moving papers consist merely of a notice of motion and affidavit by the wife. The order which is entered provides that- within ten days from the date of service of this order upon or his attorney the said be and he hereby is directed to give and file security, to be approved by the Court, within twenty days from the date hereof, in the sum of $ to secure future payments of alimony. 

An undertaking given pursuant to such an order, if made by individual sureties, reads as follows:

1931, not exceeding $

Penalty for disobedience of the order

Failure to give security as required by the order of the court is punishable by contempt (Park, 80 N. Y. 156, 1880, aff'g 18 Hun, 466; Boucicault, 21 Hun, 431, 1880; Lewis, 127 Misc. 788, 217 Sup. 498, 1926; Rosen, 226 A.D. 327, 235 Sup. 169, 1929). Cases contra (Moore, 142 A.D. 459, 126 Sup. 936, 1911; Peo. ex rel Ready vs Walsh, 132 A.D. 

462, 116 Sup. 839, 1909; Pelz, 182 A.D. 923, 169 Sup. 430, 1918) are no longer authoritative. 

B. Sequestration

The nature and scope of the remedy This takes up the remedy afforded by Section 1171, not 1171-a.

The seizure of the husband's property is designed merely to enforce his obligation, not as a mode of punishment (Lansing, 4 Lans. 377, 1871).

It may be had only to enforce alimony, not costs or disbursements (Monck, 184 A.D. 656, 172 Sup. 401, 1918).

It can reach equitable assets (Moore, 208 N. Y. 97, 1913, aff'g 143 A. D. 428, 128 Sup. 259); the husband's future income if he has a present vested right in it (Tompers, 159 

Sup. 817, 1916); his pension fund, accrued and future (Zwingmann, 150 A.D. 358, 134 Sup. 1077, 1912; Monck, supra). 

When it may be had

Sequestration may be invoked only when there are arrears (Logan, 125 A.D. 724, 110 Sup. 174, 1908), but it seems to have been granted in one case simultaneously with the temporary allowance (Fuller, L. J., N. Y. Co., Feb. 28, 1931).

1 It will not be granted unless the husband has an available interest in property (Oginsky, id., Dec. 4, 1930). The property must be in the state; if it consists of the stocks or bonds of a foreign corporation doing business in this state, they must be actually found here (Dyer, App. Div., 1st Dep't., December, 1930).

It is available to the wife, by the terms of the statute, on the husband's failure to give security, but this does not mean that such failure is a condition to the granting of sequestration (Percival, 14 N. Y. St. Rep. 255, 1888; 

Moore, supra). Contrary suggestions (Olshin, 130 Misc. 

567, 224 Sup. 328, 1927) are not to be followed.

It may be had on the husband's default under a separation agreement incorporated in the decree (Lowenthal, 229 A.D. 446, 243 Sup. 21, 1930).

The motion may be made in contempt proceedings (Foster vs Townshend, 68 N. Y. 203, 1877, rev'g 6 Daly, 136).

Defences 

Payment is ordinarily a sufficient defence (Bauso, L. J., Bronx Co., March 4, 1931), even if the payment is made after service of the papers (Olney, id., Kings Co., April 

22, 1931). It may be rejected, however, if the husband has been repeatedly delinquent (Scott, 219 A.D. 451, 220 Sup. 

93, 1927, appeal dis'd 247 N. Y. 527). Payment may be made the condition of the denial of the motion (Friede, L. J., N. Y. Co., Feb. 21, 1931), or non-payment the condition of its granting (London, id., Kings Co., April 29, 1931).

It would seem that if the husband is of such financial responsibility as to make it unlikely that sequestration of his property would have to be resorted to before payment

of alimony could be effected, the remedy will be denied. 

This would be the situation particularly in cases where the arrearage is comparatively small, and the husband is possessed of assets which are available to execution under a judgment (Parkinson, 222 A.D. 838, 226 Sup. 454, 1928).

Failure of service upon the husband of the order or judgment awarding the alimony is not a defence if he actually knew of it and had paid under it (Helmbold, 127 

Misc. 761, 217 Sup. 379, 1926); or that the wife had not permitted him to see the child, in violation of the court's direction, unless the order or judgment made that provision conditional to relief by sequestration (Rogers, L. J., Kings Co., Nov. 20, 1930).

The following allegations set forth in the opposing papers were held to be insufficient-that the wife had refused to join in a deed to the property in question, that its income was hardly enough to carry it, but that it was unencumbered except for taxes, neither the amount nor the value of the property being stated, and that a garage bill was due on the automobile for as much as it was worth, no particulars being given (Dallas, 200 A.D. 19, 192 Sup. 353, 1922).

The receiver A receiver is necessary only if the assets which are to be reached are not, nor in a condition where they may become, immediately available and must first be reduced to possession, or subjected to some preliminary proceeding. Thus, a receiver may be dispensed with where the assets consist of a pension fund (Zwingmann, 150 A.D. 

358, 134 Sup. 1077, 1912).

The receiver may be another individual, a corporation or the wife herself (Foster vs Townshend, 68 N. Y. 203,

1877, rev❜g 6 Daly 136; Continental vs Wetmore, 67 Hun, 9, 21 Sup. 746, 1893; Lupoff, L. J., Kings Co., Dec. 12, 1930).

He must give a bond, like any other receiver. The absence in the order appointing him of any requirement to give a bond makes it voidable, not void. This, together with his failure to actually furnish one, does not justify the husband in disobeying the receiver's order to turn over the assets, and an adjudication in contempt lies (In re Spies, 92 A.D. 175, 86 Sup. 1043, 1904). 

His powers

The receiver can compel the husband, or a third party having in its possession property of a tangible character and whose ownership by the husband is admittedsuch as cash-to turn the same over, by an ordinary application on notice (Tompers, 159 Sup. 817, 1916).

When the property is of a more inaccessible or involved character, or where the third party resists the claim of ownership in the husband, the receiver must bring suit.

Thus, such an action was upheld for the purpose of recovering the property from the third party, or for a money judgment for its value if it could not be had (Delahunty vs Hake, 20 A.D. 430, 46 Sup. 929, 1897).

The receiver may not, ordinarily, sue to set aside a conveyance made by the husband on the ground that it is in fraud of the wife's rights. "It would seem that any proceedings to compel the application of the rents and profits, or in any way to compel the payment of the alimony from the property, must be had by the party in whose interest the sequestration was ordered and the receiver appointed. . . . He had no title in respect to which the appellant's mortgage was adverse, or upon which it was a claim affecting its value. When it shall be sought to enforce any judgment or claim against

the corpus of the property, and to compel the payment of such judgment or claim thereout, the validity of this mortgage held by the appellant may be questioned; or if the appellant shall seek, by virtue of his mortgage, to oust the plaintiff of his possession, or obstruct him in the collection of the rents and profits, he will have his proper remedy, either by action or by summary proceedings in the original action. The sequestration was not, and could not be, affected, nor the title of the plaintiff as receiver impaired, by any act or deed of the owner of the property, after the sequestration and the appointment of the receiver." (Foster vs Townshend, 68 N. Y. 203, rev'g 6 

Daly 136, 1877, pp. 206, 207)

Any one taking the property takes it subject to the receiver's equities; and for that reason it was also held that an injunction against a foreclosure of the mortgage was unnecessary and could not be had.

Where, however, the fraudulent transfer is made before the sequestration, and is thereafter set up against the receiver to prevent him from collecting the rents and profits, his action to set aside is proper. The defendant's claim that the transferee takes free and clear of the receiver's equities is an assertion of a title adverse to the receiver's, a situation which is therefore distinguishable from the foregoing case (Donnelly vs West, 17 Hun, 564, 1879). 

The receiver may resort to the income payable to the husband under a trust fund, to enforce payment of the alimony (Wetmore, 27 Misc. 700, 59 Sup. 586, 1899).

The receiver could not, formerly, sue to subject future income payable to the husband under a trust fund (Continental vs Wetmore, 67 Hun, 9, 21 Sup. 746, 1893), on the ground that such a remedy, if available at all, could be proceeded upon only by the wife herself (Moore, 208 

N. Y. 97, 1913, aff'g 143 A.D. 428, 128 Sup. 259). But anticipatory orders appointing receivers in contemplation

of the accrual of such income are now being granted (Scott, 219 A.D. 451, 220 Sup. 93, 1927, appeal dis'd 

247 N. Y. 527; In re Scott, 135 Misc. 661, 240 Sup. 703, 1928). 

Receiver's action to sequester

If the property consists of choses in action, such as bonds, the receiver may sue to sequester them, and for further relief enabling him to convert them into money to discharge the husband's arrears for alimony. A form of complaint in such an action follows (from Schreiber vs Schomaker, 152 A.D. 817, 137 Sup. 747, 1912) (after setting forth the divorce proceedings, the temporary award, the husband's default for non-payment and the amount due, the failure of other remedies and the appointment, qualification etc., of the receiver) 

Sixth: Plaintiff further alleges that the defendant Alfred L.

Garden (the husband) has certain personal property, to wit, thirteen bonds issued by the Schomaker Piano Forte Manufacturing Company, the above named defendant foreign corporation, numbered from 13 to 25, both inclusive, bearing interest at the rate of six per cent per annum with coupons attached, which said coupons are payable semi-annually on the first days of January and July in each and every year until the maturity of said bonds in 1913. That said bonds are secured by a mortgage upon property of the defendant, The Schomaker Piano Forte Manufacturing Company, located on the corner of 11th and Catharine Streets, in the City of Philadelphia, State of Pennsylvania. 

Seventh: Plaintiff further alleges that said coupons are paid on presentation by the defendant, The Schomaker Piano Forte Manufacturing Company, at its office in the City of Philadelphia, in the State of Pennsylvania, and that on the first day of July, 1911, there will be due and payable on said thirteen bonds $390.00 for interest.

The decree of the court sequestering property of the husband or of a fund payable to the husband, such as income from a trust estate, may be modified at the husband's instance by reducing the amount to be subjected to the payment of alimony, on a showing of improvement

in the wife's circumstances (Wetmore, 27 Misc. 700, 59 

Sup. 586, 1899).

Procedure The application for an order of sequestration must be by motion on notice (David, 125 Misc. 292, 210 Sup. 619, 1925), although it seems to have been granted ex parte where the husband was out of the jurisdiction and was evading process (Zwingmann, 150 A.D. 358, 134 Sup. 

1077, 1912; Monck, 184 A.D. 656, 172 Sup. 401, 1918).

In the latter cases however the proceeding was not so much a seizure of the husband's property as a disposition or determination of its liability for the payment of alimony; and it is the seizure which requires notice, for constitutional as well as statutory grounds (Matthews, 247 N. Y. 32, 1928, rev'g 220 A.D. 825, 222 Sup. 852). 

The wife's affidavit is as follows (after Dallas, 200 A.D. 

19, 192 Sup. 353, 1922): (caption)

The wife must set forth as fully and accurately as possible the husband's interest in the property in question (Meirowitz, L. J., Bronx Co., March 24, 1931). 

The notice of motion calls for an order directing that the personal property and the rents and profits of the real property of the defendant be sequestered, and that a receiver thereof be appointed, etc.

An order of sequestration without a receiver, to reach a specific fund and for that purpose exclusively, is as follows (from Zwingmann, supra):

Upon the annexed affidavit of the plaintiff, Meta W. Zwingmann, verified the 26th day of September, 1911, and upon a certified copy of the judgment herein, and upon all the proceedings heretofore had herein, wherein and whereby it appears that on the 28th day of June, 1911, after a hearing had, judgment was rendered by Mr. Justice Kapper separating the plaintiff and the defendant from bed and board ordering defendant to pay to the plaintiff the sum of Twenty-six ($26.00) dollars on the first of each and every month as alimony; and a copy of said judgment having been duly served upon the defendant, and the plaintiff having thereafter demanded of the defendant the payment of said alimony; and the said defendant having failed and refused to pay the same; and it further appearing that the only property that the defendant has is the receipt from the City of New York of Sixty-eight ($68.00) dollars monthly pension money as a retired Police

Lieutenant; and that the defendant departed from this state for the purpose of evading the process of its courts, it is

Bureau thereof pay to the plaintiff on the 1st of each and every month or as soon thereafter as it may be practicable, out of the pension money due and payable to the plaintiff at such period the sum of Twenty-six ($26.00) dollars monthly as provided for in such judgment; and the Commissioner of Police and the Pension Bureau thereof be stayed from paying to the defendant said pension money until provision has been made by the defendant for the payment of said alimony. 

Future income 

Future earnings were held to be outside of the scope of the proceeding where the husband's right to them depended on his performance of the services required (Rolt- 

Wheeler, 175 A.D. 852, 162 Sup. 491, 1916).

The Court however was influenced apparently by the consideration that since the statute specified garnishee proceedings as the mode of executing money judgments from salaries and other earnings (the arrears having been docketed), that was the only remedy open to the wife; and for that reason made the same ruling as to accrued earnings still unpaid by the husband's employer. Whatever may be said in favor of or against this reasoning, it would seem that as far as the accrued fund was concerned, the sequestration would have been in order if the wife had chosen to proceed on the arrears as such, in their undocketed state, and she would not have been precluded from such a step by the circumstance that she had elected to afford herself an additional remedy by reducing the arrearage to judgment. Indeed, without docketing them, and as far as temporary arrears are concerned, sequestration would be the only access to the accrued fund, for

temporary alimony cannot be reduced to judgment (VIC, post). 

Disposition of the fund

The receiver may not retain control over any part of the fund above and in excess of the amount required to be applied to alimony (In re Scott, 135 Misc. 661, 240 

Sup. 703, 1928); nor pay any part of the fund to the wife unless the court has first made an allowance for that purpose (Klipstein, L. J., Kings Co., March 17, 1931). 

Duration of the proceeding

The sequestration survives the disposition of the action if necessary to protect the wife (Radloski, 72 Misc. 101, 129 Sup. 818, 1911; Myers, 131 Misc. 318, 227 Sup. 13, 1928). 

Incidental proceedings

Provisional and equitable remedies in general are available to the wife in aid of the sequestration proceeding, as to enjoin the disposition of a legacy (Garden, 34 Misc. 

97, 69 Sup. 481, 1901). Appeals

The order granting or denying sequestration is reviewable (Tompers, 159 Sup. 817, 1916; Dallas, 200 A.D. 19, 192 Sup. 353, 1922).

Limitation to large accruals The proceeding is not favored if the arrears are very small (Presnick, L. J., Kings Co., April 22, 1931).

Application by husband

The application to sequester may be made by the husband (Conklin, 125 A.D. 278, 109 Sup. 187, 1908). 

C. Execution. 

Authority for and scope of the remedy

This remedy is available despite the provisions of Sections 1171 and 1172 that security, sequestration and contempt are the remedies which may be resorted to for enforcement of alimony (Bailey, 119 Misc. 433, 196 Sup. 

340, 1922).

The right to docket arrears as they accrue and to issue execution has always been recognized (Miller, 7 Hun, 208, 1876; Lansing, 4 Lans. 377, 1871; Parkinson, 222 A.D. 

838, 226 Sup. 454, 1928).

This includes arrears for support of children as well as alimony (Schafer, 118 Misc. 254, 193 Sup. 43, 1922). 

Limitation to permanent alimony

It is available, however, only to permanent alimony. 

Halsted, 21 A.D. 466, 47 Sup. 649, 1897, holding that it may be resorted to for temporary alimony is not to be followed for two reasons, (1) Section 779 of the Code of 

Civil Procedure (now 1520, C. P. A.) providing for execution where any sum of money is required to be paid contemplates a single indebtedness which the resort to execution may satisfy and discharge and (2) that section cannot be deemed to control the situation in the light of Sections 1171 and 1172 providing for enforcement of alimony (Weber, 93 A.D. 149, 87 Sup. 519, 1904). The same holds true of counsel fee, particularly where it is directed to be paid in the alimony order (Weingarten, 133 

Misc. 681, 233 Sup. 268, 1929).

5 1 Even as to permanent alimony, however, execution may not issue until the arrears have been docketed and reduced to judgment, and as to whether temporary arrears may be docketed the question is open (Weingarten, supra). They were docketed, without comment or criticism from the Court, in Rolt-Wheeler (VI, ante), and 

McGlynn, 37 Misc. 12, 74 Sup. 744, 1902.

It would seem, taking the expressions of the courts on the fundamental difference between the temporary and final allowance, particularly on the status of foreign judgments (VII, post), that the temporary arrearage may not be reduced to judgment (In re Hudes' Estate, 128 Misc. 

362, 219 Sup. 485, 1926). The order which predicates it is merely provisional and depends for its ultimate recognition and enforcement on the duration and disposition of the action itself. Temporary arrears abate with the termination of the action and are irrecoverable thereafter; with the death of either party; with the mere reconciliation of the parties. It would be strange, in view of this interlocutory quality at best which the order possesses to invest its incidents with the formal, official and permanent character of a judgment of the court. It would be virtually wiping out the distinction between it and the final decree. See Jacobson, 85 Misc. 253, 148 Sup. 341, 1914, aff'd 168 A. D. 900. Thus, an order in supplementary proceedings on an execution to enforce temporary alimony was held void (Ostrom, 38 Misc. 232, 77 Sup. 594, 1902). 

Effect of docketing and incidental remedies

The wife becomes thereby an ordinary judgment creditor and entitled to all remedies as such (Wetmore, 149 

N. Y. 520, 1896), including supplementary proceedings (Gane, 45 Sup. Ct. 355, 1879), receivers (Zitek, L. J., Queens Co., Nov. 14, 1930) and creditors' suits (Wetmore,

8 Misc. 51, 28 Sup. 377, aff'd 79 Hun, 268, 29 Sup. 440, mod'd 149 N. Y. 520, 1896). 

Supplementary proceedings are proper even though the statute requires that they be had only after personal service of the summons (the judgment having been obtained by docketing on motion). The reason for this is that the judgment is not a new one but is merely an arrangement to put the old one into practical effect (In re Donovan, 159 A.D. 228, 144 Sup. 280, 1913).

The fact that the arrears are converted into a judgment does not divest them of their original character of alimony instalments, and the particular remedies specified by the statute are still available (Wetmore, supra).

The granting of a motion to docket arrears is res adjudicata on the amount of alimony which is due, and a husband may not raise the question in subsequent proceedings, as on a motion to punish him for contempt (Ostrin vs Posner, 216 A.D. 812, 215 Sup. 262, 1926). Procedure

The wife applies for an order directing the clerk to docket the arrears. "The only practicable method is to docket successive judgments as the instalments of alimony fall due. The clerk is merely a ministerial officer, having no judicial powers. If application were to be made to him, from time to time, he would have no power to determine whether the judgment still remained effective (for the death of either party would cancel the obligation to pay), or whether the instalment or instalments which it was sought to docket still remained unpaid. The court, however, has jurisdiction over the clerk's docket (Code Civ. Proc. sec. 1269), and it is entirely proper that application should be made to the court for an order directing the clerk to docket as a judgment any instalments of alimony which remain un-

1 paid. Such an order is not the rendition of a new judgment for the amount, but merely a means of putting into practical effect the plaintiff's right to have her judgment put in such form that execution can be issued upon it." (Thayer, 145 A.D. 268, 129 Sup. 1035, 1911, p. 1036).

Section 1269 of the Code is now section 538 of the Civil 

Practice Act. 

The application may be ex parte. Otherwise, the husband could defeat the remedy by absenting himself from the jurisdiction (Thayer, supra). 

The order reads as follows (after Thayer, supra):

Where the husband resists the wife's claim of arrears, either on his application to modify or vacate the order, or when her motion is on notice, he may avail himself of the same legal defences which are open to him on a motion to punish for contempt (V, ante), including indirect compliance arising from provision made for her support accompanied by resumption of marital relations (Bailey, 119 Misc. 433, 196 Sup. 340, 1922). The principle, however, which preserves the wife's right to alimony under a separation decree despite a reconciliation applies

here, too (Schatzberg, 229 A.D. 214, 241 Sup. 684, 1930, appeal dis'd 255 N. Y. 635).

The question of arrears may be referred (Ostrin vs 

Posner, 127 Misc. 313, 215 Sup. 259, 1925). It becomes conclusively adjudicated by the order, and may not be brought up again on subsequent proceedings, such as a motion to punish (id.).

D. Arrest 

Nature and scope of the remedy

Actions for divorce and separation in which alimony is or may be awarded are included among those contemplated by Section 827. The statute supplants the old writ or ne exeat (Denton, 1 Johns. Ch. 363, 1815; Kirby, 1 

Paige Ch. 261, 1828).

The application may be made before or after judgment (Talbot vs New Amsterdam, 237 N. Y. 245, 1923, rev'g 

205 A.D. 525, 199 Sup. 726, 1923).

It may be made simultaneously with the application for alimony and counsel fees; and while the husband is in jail for contempt (id.).

The sole purpose of the remedy is to insure the presence of the husband in the jurisdiction and his availability to contempt process in the event of his default for nonpayment of alimony. The bail may not be resorted to unless and until contempt is proven unavailing (Subernick, 123 Misc. 174, 204 Sup. 437, 1924), and that cannot be done until an order to show cause has actually been obtained (Winton vs Livey, 16 Civ. Pro. Rep. 348, 53 

Hun, 630, 6 Sup. 29, 1889). It is therefore to be distinguished from ordinary security (VI A, ante).

The bail may not be resorted to for costs as long as the remedy of execution is available (Subernick, supra).

The bail, on the other hand, does not discharge its obligation by producing the husband on the motion to punish. 

It must produce him for the actual commitment (Talbot vs New Amsterdam, supra).

Nor is the undertaking limited to the performance of directions subsequently made, but to those in force at the time as well (id.).

The bail is liable for arrears at the time of the execution of the bond (id.). Recovery may be had only of arrears which are enforceable by contempt (Ferris vs 

National Surety, L. J., Kings Co., Feb. 25, 1931). Procedure

The moving papers consist of an order ex parte and affidavits by the wife and witnesses. If an award for alimony has already been made, the wife's affidavit need not state the facts which constitute her cause of action, except very briefly, because she may proceed on the alimony application and the moving papers submitted therein. In such a case, her allegations should confine themselves to the circumstances of the husband's actual or intended departure from the state, and the corroborating affidavits to the same effect. 

If no award has been made, and no proceedings have as yet been had in which any full and comprehensive statement was given to the court as to the wife's cause of action, she should set forth in her moving affidavit with the utmost particularity the facts of her grievance. 

This may be done together with the application for alimony; the same papers, that is, may be submitted for the award as for the order of arrest. In any event, a complaint must have been served (Lichstrahl, 38 Misc. 331, 77 Sup. 900, 1902).

Extreme care must be taken to allege sufficient proof of

the husband's departure or intended departure to bring the case within the section, which depends by its very language "partly upon extrinsic facts." The allegations were held insufficient, for example, where they were based on information and belief arising from conversations had with third persons that the husband was about to depart, neither the substance of the conversations nor the identity of the third persons being given; and the fact that the wife had never resorted to contempt proceedings for the enforcement of the award could not be the basis of an inference that the husband did have such an intention (Plotnick, 185 A.D. 15, 172 Sup. 584, 1918).

An undertaking by the wife to support the order may be waived by the court. A brief statement of her inability to furnish one should be given. 

The order reads as follows: 

PRESENT 

Hon. Justice

MARY SMITH At a Special Term, Part 2, of the Supreme 

Court of the State of New York, held in and for the County of New York, at the County 

Court House, in the Borough of Manhattan, City of New York, on the day of 1931 -against- 

WILLIAM SMITH 

Plaintiff Defendant

TO THE SHERIFF OF ANY COUNTY OF THE STATE OF NEW YORK: 

It having been made to appear to the Court by the affidavit of Mary Smith, duly sworn to the day of 1931, and from the verified complaint herein that this is a sufficient cause of action and that the judgment demanded requires the performance of an act, to wit, the payment by defendant to plaintiff of a just and reasonable sum for her maintenance and support, the neglect or refusal to perform which will be punishable by the court as a contempt, and that the defendant is a non-resident (or, is either not now a resident of this state, or is about to depart therefrom), by reason of which there is danger that the judgment or order requiring the performance of said act will be rendered ineffectual; and an undertaking on the part of the plaintiff having been dispensed with; You are hereby required to forthwith arrest the defendant 

William Smith, if he is found within your county, and hold him to bail in the sum of $ by written undertaking executed by two or more sufficient bail conditioned to the effect that said defendant will obey the directions of the court or of an appellate court, contained in an order or judgment requiring him to perform said act as above set forth, or, in default of his so doing, that he will at all times render himself amenable to proceedings to punish him for the omission; and return this order, with your proceedings thereunder, as prescribed by law.

Enter 

J. S. C. 

The order must be a court order. But see Lachenmeyer, 26 Hun, 26, 1882, appeal dis'd 89 N. Y. 633. No particular wording is prescribed except that it conform substantially to the language of the statute (Boucicault, 21 

Hun, 431, 1880).

A memorandum may be submitted with the order for the purpose of showing the amount of bail which should be fixed in the case.

The undertaking given for the release of the husband reads as follows: 

(caption) The above named defendant, William Smith, having been arrested by 

Sheriff of the County of New

York, upon an order of arrest granted by the Court, in a certain action commenced in the above named Court by the above named plaintiff against the said defendant, and desiring to be released on bail, The undersigned, William Smith, of No. in the Borough of Manhattan, City of New York, by occupation , of No. and in the Borough of Manhattan, City of New York, by occupation and of No., etc., to which several addresses, notice sent by mail of the pendency of any action on this undertaking, or against the Sheriff of the County of New York or requiring justification of sureties therein, shall be sufficient notice and equivalent to personal service of the same, hereby undertake jointly and severally in the sum of $ that the above named defendant arrested as aforesaid, will obey the direction of the Court, or of an appellate court, contained in an order or judgment requiring him to perform the act specified in the order, or in default of his so doing, that at all times he will render himself amenable to proceedings to punish him for the omission. 

Dated, New York City, the day of the year one thousand nine hundred and thirty-one. in (L.S.) (L.S.) (L.S.) 

Forms are available at the Sheriff's office. 

Husband's remedies and disposition of the bail

The husband may move, ex parte or on notice, to vacate the order, or reduce the bail (Zweigenbaum, L. J., Kings Co., Dec. 8, 1930).

The sureties are discharged, and money deposited must be refunded if the husband wins the case, and it does not matter that the wife appeals from the judgment (Tiedeman, 173 A.D. 537, 159 Sup. 818, 1916).

If he loses and the wife is granted a decree, the order of arrest and the bail stand and may be resorted to for subsequent defaults (Sturges, 114 Misc. 475, 186 Sup. 

693, 1920).

His motion to cancel the bond was granted where his previous discharge from imprisonment for non-payment of temporary alimony was conditioned, in the findings of fact and conclusions of law filed with the judgment in the wife's favor, on his payment of the arrears and he had paid them. This was on the theory that the provision in the findings and conclusions was intended to take the place of the bond as security for the payment of the permanent alimony (Goldwieser, 131 Misc. 382, 225 Sup. 

735, 1927). 

Recovery on the bail or bond 

If cash bail is deposited, the wife may apply to the court to apply it to the arrears, on a showing that the husband is unavailable to contempt process (Subernick, 123 

Misc. 174, 204 Sup. 437, 1924).

If an undertaking is given, suit must be brought against the sureties. (C. P. A., Secs. 870, 871; Toles vs 

Adee, 84 N. Y. 222, 1881, rev'g 9 W'kly Dig. 211, and 91 

N. Y. 562, 1883.) The complaint in such an action follows (from Talbot vs New Amsterdam, 237 N. Y. 245, 1923, rev❜g 205 A.D. 525, 199 Sup. 726, 1923):

BENEDICT BRISTOW TALBOT
