E. Miscellaneous actions and proceedings Attachment-Section 1171-a 

This section makes provision for the support of the wife from a specific fund or estate, instead of from the husband's income and resources in general. It is unenforceable against him personally, and is therefore to be distinguished from alimony. The statement of Judge 

Andrews in the Matthews case (247 N. Y. 32, 1928, p. 35) that

"prior seizure is necessary to give the court jurisdiction to decree counsel fees and alimony payable out of a nonresident's property within the State," is not to be taken as an expression of the power of the court to actually award alimony, in the particular sense in which it has been under discussion here, but to make provision for the wife's support in the more general connotation. Alimony cannot be awarded, of course, because the very situation which the statute contemplates is based upon the inability of the wife to serve the husband personally with the summons.

The fact, however, that alimony has been improperly allowed in the judgment (because of the constructive service) does not mean that the remedy will be refused if the facts are sufficient to bring the case within the statute and her application is otherwise deserving (Myers, 131 Misc. 318, 227 Sup. 13, 1928, aff'd 225 A.D. 776, 232 

Sup. 825). Injunction

The provisional remedy of injunction may be invoked in the matrimonial action to prevent fraudulent transfers of the husband's property (Kirby, 1 Paige Ch. 

261, 1828; Carey, 2 Daly 424, 1869; Foster vs Townshend, 68 N. Y. 203, 1877, rev'g 6 Daly 136). It may be granted even where it appears that the husband does not intend to remove his property from the state (Hammond, 1 Clarke Ch. 151, 1839). 

Creditors' bills

These are really judgment creditors' bills, for a creditor's bill is brought when the debtor has died (Langdell, Brief 

Survey of Equity Jurisdiction, note p. 124).

The remedy is available to the wife independently of the provisions of Section 1172 (Moore, 143 A.D. 428, 128 

Sup. 259, 1911, aff'd 208 N. Y. 97, 1913).

The wife must first docket the arrears as a judgment, and it must appear, exactly as in any other creditor's suit, that legal remedies are inadequate or unavailing (Miller, 7 Hun, 208, 1876). But see McGlynn, 37 Misc. 

12, 74 Sup. 744, 1902. "Does it make any difference because her right to support is determined only by an order of this court which the husband prevents from ripening to a final judgment by his refusal to pay? There can be no essential difference in the present status of the pecuniary as well as marital right whether she gets alimony by order or judgment. She is just as much a creditor because the arrears are sums directed to be paid for present needs as though such provision was by later judgment for the remaining years of her life, and just as much a wife entitled to support as she would be after separation by court decree. Her right to the moneys cannot be attacked collaterally." (p. 14)

The action lies to subject to the payment of the alimony income payable to the husband under a trust fund created for his benefit, as by his father's will (Thompson, 52 Hun, 465, 5 Sup. 604, 1889). Equity intervenes in such a case because of the unity of husband and wife, which has not been changed by the Married Women's Acts, as far as this point is concerned (Wetmore, 149 N. Y. 520, 1896, mod❜g 79 Hun, 268, 29 Sup. 440, aff'g 8 Misc. 51, 28 Sup. 377).

In the creditor's action, the trustees of the fund may be decreed to pay not only the surplus of the income (over and above the sum required by the husband for his own support), but the entire income if he actually has sufficient independent means (Wetmore, supra). 

For a discussion of this and all other related cases see

Moore, supra; also as to the difference between an action of this sort and one brought by the receiver appointed in sequestration, as in Continental vs Wetmore, 67 Hun, 9, 21 Sup. 746, 1893. It would seem that the justification for the direction to the trustee to pay over to the receiver the checks or other remittances of the income as they come into his possession is that the arrangement merely takes the place of the payment of alimony itself, and nothing, therefore, is added (Moore, supra).

The judgment in the matrimonial action may charge the trust fund directly only with the husband's consent. 

Such a provision is not binding upon the trustee until the income comes into his hands, for he is not a party to the action; but when he does receive the money and he has been served with a copy of the decree he is obliged to turn it over to the wife. If the husband or other person makes it impossible for the trustee to comply with the judgment, as where the husband refuses to endorse the checks or executes assignments of the fund, which threatens the wife's rights, a creditor's suit is in order. 

The complaint prays (from Hoagland vs Leask, 154 A.D. 

101, 138 Sup. 790, 1912, aff'd 214 N. Y. 645) "that the said income of the said trust fund now in the hands of the defendant trustees, amounting to the sum of 

One thousand two hundred and forty-eight ($1248.00)

Dollars, may be paid over to the plaintiff to be applied upon the alimony accrued and in arrears under the said final judgment of divorce; that the defendant trustees and their successors may be directed to pay from time to time directly to the plaintiff out of the future income of said trust fund the amount of alimony awarded to her, and due and to become due under the said final judgment of divorce; that the plaintiff have injunctive relief restraining the defendant trustees from paying any portion of said income of said trust fund to the said defendant 

Charles F. Hoagland, until all alimony due shall first be

paid. That the plaintiff have injunctive relief restraining the prosecution of the action brought by the defendant 

Edmund Batchis, referred to in the Ninth Paragraph of the complaint; And that plaintiff have such other and further relief, etc."

The wife cannot reach the husband's salary by a creditor's suit except as to the amount which has accrued at the time of the commencement of the creditor's bill (Valentine vs Williams, 159 Sup. 815, 1916, aff'd 179 A.D. 884, 165 Sup. 116, aff'd 223 N. Y. 574).

It may be noted, in connection with equitable remedies in general, that the equity courts may intervene whenever the husband attempts to defraud the wife of the alimony, on her showing that legal remedies are futile. Thus, even the federal courts have jurisdiction, as where there is a diversity of citizenship (Barber, 21 How. [U. S.] 582, 1858). 

Civil actions

Suit may be brought on notes or checks given in payment of alimony. It is not a defence that the order or judgment awarding the alimony has been reversed, for the same reason that restitution need not be made (Averett, 112 Misc. 487, 183 Sup. 48, 1920, rev'g 111 Misc. 542, 183 Sup. 702). 

Proceedings against third parties

The wife may hold third parties for unpaid alimony, as damages, if the third parties were responsible for the default and made enforcement impossible (Hoefler, 12 

A.D. 84, 42 Sup. 1035, 1896); and may even proceed against the third parties for contempt (Sidway, 156 A.D. 

375, 141 Sup. 391, 1913).

FOREIGN JUDGMENTS 

VII 

A. Recovery in general

The alimony provisions of judgments of other states have always been recognized and enforced here under the full faith and credit clause of the Federal Constitution (Lynde, 162 N. Y. 405, 1900, aff'd 181 U. S. 183, 1901; 

Stewart, 198 A.D. 337, 190 Sup. 369, 1921), and of other countries by the comity of nations (Wood, 7 Misc. 579, 28 Sup. 154, 1894).

Recovery may be had both of any sums decreed to be due by the judgment itself, such as counsel fees, and of instalments accruing thereafter (Smith, L. J., N. Y. Co., April 24, 1931). As to the latter, certain cases specify that recovery is possible only if it does not affirmatively appear that the award is subject to modification (Patton, 67 Misc. 404, 123 Sup. 329, 1910; Williamson, 169 A.D. 

597, 155 Sup. 423, 1915; Tiedemann, 172 A.D. 819, 158 

Sup. 851, 1916, aff'd 225 N. Y. 709, appeal dis'd 251 

U. S. 536; Stewart, supra). It would seem, however, that since accrued alimony under a final judgment is the wife's vested property right (Sistare, 218 U. S. 1, 1909), it cannot be altered or modified, and a statute authorizing such a modification would be unconstitutional (Van Horn, 196 

A.D. 472, 188 Sup. 98, 1921; Curran, 136 Misc. 598, 240 

Sup. 364, 1930).

The refusal of the court of one state to give recognition to the alimony provision in the judgment of another state 212

FOREIGN JUDGMENTS 

213 is reviewable by the United States Supreme Court (Laing vs Rigney, 160 U. S. 531, 1896, rev'g Rigney, 127 N. Y. 408).

B. Validity of the judgment-jurisdictional requirements

The foreign judgment must be valid, in that the court had jurisdiction both as to the decree of divorce or separation, and the alimony. It is unnecessary to take up here the principles which underlie the subject, and reference may be had for that purpose to the following cases: 

Atherton, 181 U. S. 155, 1898, rev'g 155 N. Y. 129; Haddock, 201 U. S. 562, 1906; Tiedemann, supra; Kelsey, 204 

A.D. 116, 197 Sup. 371, 1922, aff'd 237 N. Y. 520.

Thus the New York court may recognize the validity of the judgment to the extent that it determines the marital status and refuse recognition of the alimony allowance. In Laing vs Rigney, supra, it was finally held, overruling the Court of Appeals, that the husband's appearance in the action in the other state conferred jurisdiction upon the court to render judgment on supplemental proofs, following the execution of an order for the service of a supplemental bill, outside the state, and the husband's failure to appear thereto; and that his appearance thereafter to amend the decree was another consideration favoring the validity of the judgment. 

In Lynde, supra, it was held that the husband's appearance in opposition to the wife's motion to amend the decree which was rendered after constructive service and his default, for the purpose of inserting a provision for alimony, was sufficient to confer the necessary jurisdiction.

It must appear, also, before our courts will recognize

the foreign provision for alimony, that the court in the foreign state was empowered to make the award by its statute (Tiedemann, supra). In a case where the judgment was in favor of the husband, and the local statute provided for alimony only where the wife succeeded, we gave recognition to the award as an ordinary money judgment, not as alimony, which disposed, at the same time, of the objection that alimony was by the law of the foreign state subject to modification. Recognition was upheld on two grounds-(1) the provision was only part of a decree which the foreign court was authorized to grant and which was valid here, and no single part of the decree may be attacked here collaterally and (2) the husband had consented to it (France, 79 A.D. 291, 79 Sup. 

479, 1903, aff'g 38 Misc. 459, 77 Sup. 1015).

The objection that the foreign decree was collusive is also collateral; also, both parties being guilty, neither may urge the objection (France, supra). Temporary arrears are recoverable here only pending the action unless it appears that the law of the forum permits recovery after the judgment (Mills, 95 Misc. 231, 158 Sup. 753, 1916).

But nothing was said about the objection to recovery on money judgment before ten years, or that we do not regard the allowance of temporary alimony as a judgment. 

C. Remedies 

Originally only legal remedies were available on foreign judgments. That is, the wife could only bring suit for a money judgment for the amount actually due under the decree (Wood, 7 Misc. 579, 28 Sup. 154, 1894). 

Future instalments, of course, were irrecoverable because they could always be altered by modification, and they lacked, therefore, the conclusive and final character

FOREIGN JUDGMENTS 

215 to bring them within Article 4 (Lynde, 162 N. Y. 405, aff'd 181 U. S. 183, 1901).

Equitable remedies could not be had even though the decree made provision for them, because they were "provisions in the nature of execution and operative upon the defendant only as he, or property belonging to him, might be found within the jurisdiction of the foreign state" (Lynde, supra, 162 N. Y. 405, p. 420).

This does not mean, however, that the foreign judgment is enforceable only to the extent of arrears adjudged thereby to be due, or in some other way determined, but that only the amount accruing after the commencement of the action in New York is beyond the scope of our courts.

Today the situation is changed, but the old rule is worthy of note because it prevails in every other state in which modifying statutes have not been enacted, a circumstance which is important to determine when the question is the enforceability of our judgments in foreign forums. 

The dictum in Barber, 21 How. (U. S.) 582, 1858, p. 

595, that the alimony provision "becomes a judicial debt of record against the husband, which may be enforced by execution or attachment against his person, issuing from the court which gave the decree; and when that cannot be done on account of the husband having left or fled from that jurisdiction to another, where the process of that court cannot reach him, the wife, by her next friend, may sue him wherever he may be found, or where he shall have acquired a new domicile, for the purpose of recovering the alimony due to her, or to carry the decree into a judgment there with the same effect that it has in the State in which the decree was given" (italics ours)

is interpreted in the Lynde case, supra, (41 A.D. 520, 58 

Sup. 567, 572, 1899) as not meaning that non-legal remedies are available, because the judgment in that case merely adjudged a sum to be due and that the wife have execution therefor.

Section 1772 of the Code of Civil Procedure (now C. P. 

A. 1171) was amended in 1904 to include foreign judgments, and today they are enforceable by the same equitable remedies and may be given the same effect in general as our own judgments. The section being remedial is retroactive (Moore, 208 N. Y. 97, 1913, aff'g 143 A.D. 

428, 128 Sup. 259, 1911).

The wife may have such relief as she would be entitled to in a judgment creditor's action, independently of the statute (Moore, supra).

The outstanding feature of the change in the situation is that the foreign judgment is not only granted all these remedies, but that it is made the judgment of this state as though it was originally rendered here, and provision may be made for the payment of future alimony exactly as it is made in a local decree. In the Moore case, supra (208 N. Y. 97), the court states: "Under the literal wording of the Code the court had power in this action to require the appellant to pay to respondent from time to time and in such installments as it should deem proper the sums of money 'required' for the support of his wife as fixed by the Pennsylvania decree and to compel him to give a bond conditioned for such payment." (p. 102)

It would seem, however, that the court does not mean payment of alimony in futuro, but arrears, as it will be observed that the section nowheres specifies directly that our court may direct the payment of future alimony. 

It intimates the authority for it by declaring

FOREIGN JUDGMENTS 

217 • · "Where a judgment rendered . . . in this state or a judgment rendered in another state upon which an action has been brought in this state and judgment rendered therein, requires a husband to provide for the education or maintenance the court may direct him to give reasonable security, etc., etc." (italics ours)

GERTRUDE L. MOORE 

Plaintiff In other words, the only justification for such a provision in a judgment obtained here on a foreign decree would seem to be that the statute states that certain things may be done if such a provision is already made. -VS- 

HENRY L. MOORE 

D. The Moore case 

A comprehensive set of papers in an action brought for general legal and equitable relief, on a foreign judgment, is to be found in the Moore case, supra. The complaint read as follows:

Supreme Court: New York County 

Defendant

The plaintiff above named, by her attorneys, Graham & 

L'Amoureux, for complaint herein, alleges: 

1. That on or about June 1, 1892, the plaintiff duly commenced an action in the Court of Common Pleas, No. III, for the County of Philadelphia, in the State of Pennsylvania, against the defendant, to recover a divorce from the bed and board of said defendant, which said action was brought upon the ground of adultery commenced by the defendant.

2. That said Court was then and still is a Court of General 

Jurisdiction duly created by the laws of the said State of Pennsylvania. 

3. That the said action was duly and regularly begun by the issuance of process, which was thereafter duly and regularly served upon the defendant in person. 

4. That such proceedings were thereupon duly had in the said action, that on or about May 19, 1902, the plaintiff recovered a judgment or decree therein, which was duly given by such court, whereby said court did order, adjudge and decree, that this plaintiff be divorced and separated with said defendant, and that he pay to her as alimony the sum of $333.33 per month for her support, which said judgment or decree still remains in full force and effect, and not reversed, modified or otherwise vacated. 

5. That there is now due and owing to the plaintiff by the defendant the sum of $333.33 due November 19, 1902, and the sum of $333.33 due on the 19th day of each and every month after the said November 19th, 1902, up to and including the 19th day of March, 1907, none of which has been paid. 

6. That defendant has departed from the State of Pennsylvania and now is for a long time past and has been a resident in and of the City, County and State of New York. 

7. That defendant has not given any security for the payment of said alimony and has left no property in the State of Pennsylvania out of which the said judgment or decree might or could be satisfied. 

8. On information and belief that the defendant receives and for a long time past has received a monthly income of upwards of eleven hundred dollars and is the owner of valuable property in the State of New York. 

9. That the plaintiff is and will be unable to reach any of the income or property of the defendant, and will be remediless without the relief prayed for. 

Wherefore, the plaintiff prays:

FOREIGN JUDGMENTS 

219 First:

That the judgment or decree of the Court of Common 

Pleas, No. III, for the County of Philadelphia, State of Penn sylvania, be made the judgment or decree of this Court, with the same force, effect and validity, and be enforced against the defendant, by this Court, in the same manner as if said decree were the final judgment of this Court. 

Second: That defendant be adjudged to pay plaintiff the alimony in arrears and future alimony in the sum of Three hundred and thirty-three and 33/100 ($333.33) dollars a month. 

Third: That the defendant be required to give security for the payment of said future alimony. 

Fourth: That defendant's personal property, and the rents and profits of his real property be sequestered and a receiver thereof appointed.

Fifth: That defendant be enjoined from disposing of any of his said property, real or personal, unless and until he pays the alimony in arrears and give such security for the payment of future alimony as the Court may decree herein. 

Sixth: That plaintiff have such other and further relief as to the Court may seem proper, etc.

The findings of fact, summarized, were as follows: 

1. The marriage. 

2. The institution of the divorce proceedings, and an explanation of them, particularly as compared with our own. 

3. The residence of the parties at the time. 

4. The ground of the action, as recited in the Pennsylvania complaint. 

5. The defendant's appearance. 

6-7. The proceedings thereafter had, with verbatim copies of the important ones. 

8. The decision of the court. 

9. Subsequent proceedings. 

10. Their disposition-entry of the decree. 

11. Identification of the Court's initials subscribed to the decree. 

12. Plaintiff's futile efforts to collect the alimony in Pennsylvania.

13. The jurisdiction of the Pennsylvania court over that action. 

14. The nature and significance of the decree awarded, both there and here. 

15. Failure to give payment or security. 

16. Defendant's absconding from Pennsylvania and present residence here. 

17-19. Action brought against him in the City Court, here, for the arrears the proceedings, judgment and nulla bona return of execution. 

20. Existence of a trust fund under a testator's legacy, for the husband's benefit-verbatim copy of the provision in the will. 

21-24. The value of the estate and income. 

25. Identity of the executor and trustee. 

26. Payments of income being paid to defendant. 

27-28. Amounts he is entitled to and has received. 

29. Amount due to plaintiff. 

30. “I find . . . the sum of One thousand dollars per month to be a reasonable sum which the defendant should be allowed for his support, and that any surplus over and above said sum which he receives as income from his father's estate, should be devoted to the current alimony, then to the expenses of this action, then to the liquidation of the arrears of alimony, until such arrears shall have been paid in full with interest, and that thereafter and after the full and complete payment of such arrears of alimony and interest, the plaintiff shall receive out of such income the current alimony and that the defendant shall receive the balance thereof." 

31. Defendant has no property in New York available to execution. 

The conclusions of law were: 

1. Plaintiff has no adequate remedy at law. 

2. Section 1772 of the Code of Civil Procedure is retro active and applies to this judgment. 

3. It is not necessary for the grounds of the foreign action to appear on the face of the decree, they having been stated in the pleadings and judgment roll. 

4-5. Plaintiff exhausted all other remedies to collect, before commencing this action. 

6. Defendant absconded to evade contempt process.

E. Incidental considerations 

The statement that the plaintiff has no adequate remedy at law is unnecessary, for the action is statutory, not equitable (Beeck, 211 A.D. 720, 208 Sup. 98, 1925).

Contempt lies for the enforcement of the judgment rendered here (Rohden, 119 Misc. 624, 198 Sup. 16, 1922), and it must be a judgment requiring the payment of monies, as in the Moore case, supra, not a mere money judgment (Wemple, 219 A.D. 241, 219 Sup. 638, 1927).

It is not necessary that the husband be served with a copy of the foreign judgment before suit is brought upon it here (Curran, 136 Misc. 598, 240 Sup. 364, 1930).

An ordinary action to recover a money judgment for accrued alimony on a foreign judgment should not be brought under Section 1171 (Clapp, 221 A.D. 754, 222 

Sup. 556, 1927).

Originally, equitable remedies were limited to those foreign judgments which were based on adultery, but by 

Section 1171 of the Civil Practice Act (enacted 1920 

to take effect in 1921), they were made available in foreign judgments of divorce based on adultery, and separations based on the grounds for which we grant them in 

New York.

Thus, if the divorce is for a ground other than adultery, only a money judgment can be had (Tiedemann, 172 

A.D. 819, 158 Sup. 851, 1916 aff'd 225 N. Y. 709, appeal dis'd 251 U. S. 536; Beeck, supra; Miller, 219 A.D. 

61, 219 Sup. 203, 1926, aff'd 246 N. Y. 636; Hardy, 136 

Misc. 759, 240 Sup. 826, 1930).

If the complaint fails to allege the ground on which the foreign judgment was based, it is insufficient (Boissevain, 129 Misc. 5, 220 Sup. 579, 1927).

In no event will any relief be granted which is not specified by our statute, such as an accounting of community property (Tiedemann, supra).

It may be noted that under Section 1171, the judgment in the Moore case, supra, being a separation on the ground of adultery, would not be entitled to equitable remedies, if the action here had been brought under the Code. 

The section does not apply to the judgments of foreign countries (Boissevain, 252 N. Y. 178, 1929, mod❜g 224 

A.D. 576, 231 Sup. 529, 1928, rev'g 130 Misc. 161, 223 

Sup. 616).

F. Defences 

The same defences, generally, are available to the husband in actions brought here on foreign judgments as in proceedings and actions instituted in the foreign forum to enforce its judgment. He may urge the wife's re-marriage, where the judgment incorporated by reference the parties' separation agreement that alimony should cease upon that contingency (Sleicher, 251 N. Y. 

366, 1929, rev❜g 224 A.D. 529, 231 Sup. 538, aff'g 228 Sup. 711).

He may urge a release, and the same considerations are pertinent in determining its validity (Sleicher, 136 Misc. 

604, 240 Sup. 362, 1930).

FOREIGN JUDGMENTS 

227 The wife may in the one action brought here demand the cancellation of agreements made in settlement and release, for inadequacy and as being violative of Section 

51 of the Domestic Relations Law. If the agreements are upheld, they may still be repudiated insofar as the provision in the foreign judgment makes award for the support for children; but even if they are, they bar any recovery for the children because there is no way of determining what portion of the allowance was for them (Levy vs Dockendorff, 177 A.D. 249, 163 Sup. 435, 1917).

Agreements made before the foreign judgment was rendered may not be urged because they should have been offered to the other court at the trial (Stewart, 198 A.D. 

337, 190 Sup. 369, 1921).

The same is true of the objection that the wife has breached the decree, as where she was charged with violating the terms of a separation agreement merged in it. 

It was held that the husband should have applied to the foreign court to be relieved of the alimony provision (Van 

Horn, 196 A.D. 472, 188 Sup. 98, 1921).

Thus, the husband may not resist the action, where it is brought for arrears for support of children, on the ground that they have reached their majority, as he should have sought a modification from the foreign court (Smith, L. J., N. Y. Co., April 24, 1931). 

G. Merger, bankruptcy, and death of parties

It was held in In re Williams, 208 N. Y. 32, 1913, aff❜g 152 

A.D. 385, 136 Sup. 707 that the recovery of a judgment here does not discharge the foreign judgment, because there is no merger; a discharge in bankruptcy, even if it wiped out the judgment here (which it does not) could not affect the foreign judgment; and that the judgment

here is recoverable from the husband's estate. See Anonymous, 12 Abb. Pr. (N. C.) 160, 1880.

H. Status and effect of the foreign judgmentsupport for children

The absence of any provision for the support of the children, in the foreign judgment, precludes a civil recovery for them here. But where the child was unborn at the time of the action in the other court, and the question of its support was therefore not litigated, recovery may be had, even by the wife directly from the husband. It does not matter that she could apply to the foreign court to modify the decree so as to make the necessary provision (Laumeier, 237 N. Y. 357, 1924, rev'g 206 A.D. 246, 200 

Sup. 606, 1924).
